# 37. Sūrat al-Ṣāffāt

One hundred and eighty-two verses. Meccan. It takes its name from a word in its first verse: **ٱلصَّٰٓفَّٰت** — "those drawn up in ranks".

The sūra opens with three oaths, and all three are in the same mould: **a definite active participle, feminine plural, followed by a verbal noun from its own root.** This mould is the opening of several other sūras of the Qurʾān — al-Dhāriyāt, al-Mursalāt, al-Nāziʿāt — and in all of them the same disagreement has arisen: **who or what is sworn by is not said.** Those three sūras were treated in this commentary; here a fourth will be set beside them, and the comparison will be given in a table.

But the real structure of al-Ṣāffāt lies not in the oaths but **in its middle.** From the seventy-fifth verse to the hundred and forty-eighth a **sequence of prophets** is set up: Noah (Nūḥ), Abraham (Ibrāhīm) — and within his account, Isaac (Isḥāq) — Moses (Mūsā) with Aaron (Hārūn), Elijah (Ilyās), Lot (Lūṭ), Jonah (Yūnus). And what is striking about the sequence is not the accounts recounted but **how the accounts are closed.** Each ends with the same three- or four-sentence seal:

> *wa taraknā ʿalayhi fi'l-ākhirīn* — *salāmun ʿalā …* — *innā kadhālika najzi'l-muḥsinīn* — *innahu min ʿibādinā'l-muʾminīn*

**And this seal is not stamped in full on everyone.** With one, a piece of the mould is missing; with another, not one of them is there. This is a fact countable from the text, and it will be given below in a separate section in tabular form.

The end of the sūra ties to the same mould. The hundred and eighty-first verse gives peace **to all the messengers at once**: *wa salāmun ʿala'l-mursalīn.* The peace given one by one in the sequence is thus repeated collectively at the end — **the sūra closes with its own closing formula.**

The subject of the sūra is one from beginning to end and can be gathered into two sentences: **God is one** (verse 4), and **the promise given to the messengers is kept** (verses 171-173). Everything in between — the sky, the satans, clay, the scene of the reckoning, *zaqqūm*, seven prophets, the dispute over daughters and sons — is set between these two sentences.

---

## The structure of the sūra

The sūra consists of seven blocks. The block boundaries are for the most part drawn by the text's own repetitions.

| Block | Verses | Subject | What ends the block |
|---|---|---|---|
| **I** | 1-11 | Three oaths, the ruling of God's oneness, the guarding of the sky, the proof from clay | *min ṭīnin lāzib* (11) |
| **II** | 12-39 | Mockery, denial, the objection to resurrection, the reckoning and the quarrel in the fire | *mā kuntum taʿmalūn* (39) |
| **III** | 40-61 | *Illā ʿibāda'llāhi'l-mukhlaṣīn* — the picture of the garden and the scene of the *qarīn* | *fa'l-yaʿmali'l-ʿāmilūn* (61) |
| **IV** | 62-74 | *Zaqqūm*, following the forefathers, the end of those who were warned | *illā ʿibāda'llāhi'l-mukhlaṣīn* (74) |
| **V** | 75-148 | **The sequence of prophets** — Noah, Abraham, Moses-Aaron, Elijah, Lot, Jonah | *fa-mattaʿnāhum ilā ḥīn* (148) |
| **VI** | 149-170 | The dispute over daughters and sons, the angels, "we are the ones who stand in ranks" | *fa-sawfa yaʿlamūn* (170) |
| **VII** | 171-182 | The promise given to the messengers, "turn away" twice, the closing | *rabbi'l-ʿālamīn* (182) |

**Three of the block boundaries are drawn by the same sentence:**

- **Block III opens with *illā ʿibāda'llāhi'l-mukhlaṣīn*** (40) and **block IV closes with the same sentence** (74).
- The same sentence occurs once more in the account of Elijah (128) and once more in block VI (160). **Four times in all.**
- In the hundred and sixty-ninth verse the same construction occurs **in the mouths of the deniers**: *la-kunnā ʿibāda'llāhi'l-mukhlaṣīn* — "we would surely have been God's chosen servants". **The sūra, that is, sets its own key sentence at the end inside a falsehood.**

### Repetitions within the sūra — countable data

**The following are facts that can be verified from the text; the claim that they set up an order is interpretation, and I present it as an observation.**

| Repetition | First occurrence | Second occurrence | Difference |
|---|---|---|---|
| *fa'staftihim* | **11** — "are they harder to create?" | **149** — "daughters for your Lord and sons for them?" | Two block openings with the same command |
| *a-idhā mitnā wa kunnā turāban wa ʿiẓāman* | **16** — *a-innā la-**mabʿūthūn*** | **53** — *a-innā la-**madīnūn*** | One word of difference: resurrection / reckoning |
| *fa-aqbala baʿḍuhum ʿalā baʿḍin yatasāʾalūn* | **27** — with *wa*, **in the fire** | **50** — with *fa*, **in the garden** | The same sentence, two pictures |
| *illā ʿibāda'llāhi'l-mukhlaṣīn* | 40, 74, 128, 160 | — | **Four times** |
| *la-muḥḍarūn / al-muḥḍarīn* | **57** (the one saved) | **127, 158** (those to be brought) | The same mould, the reverse direction |
| *al-karbi'l-ʿaẓīm* | **76** — Noah | **115** — Moses and Aaron | Two deliverances, the same word |
| *saqīm* | **89** — Abraham | **145** — Jonah | The same word, two different states |
| *subḥāna … ʿammā yaṣifūn* | **159** — *subḥāna'llāh* | **180** — *subḥāna rabbika rabbi'l-ʿizzati* | The second an expansion of the first |
| *fa-tawalla ʿanhum ḥattā ḥīn · wa abṣir…* | **174-175** | **178-179** | An almost exact repetition |
| **ص-ف-ف** | **1** — *wa'ṣ-ṣāffāti ṣaffā* | **165** — *wa innā la-naḥnu'ṣ-ṣāffūn* | The subject of the oath speaks at the end |
| **س-ب-ح** | **143** — *mina'l-musabbiḥīn* (Jonah) | **166** — *la-naḥnu'l-musabbiḥūn* · **159, 180** — *subḥāna* | The verb that saves, the work of the angels |
| **ذ-ك-ر** | **3** — *fa't-tāliyāti dhikrā* | **13** — *lā yadhkurūn* · **155** — *a-fa-lā tadhakkarūn* · **168** — *dhikran mina'l-awwalīn* | The reminder brought / the reminder not taken |
| **ج-ح-م** | **23, 55, 64, 68, 97, 163** | — | **Six times** — the most repeated name of a place in the sūra |
| **ح-ض-ر** | 57, 127, 158 | — | Three occurrences |

### The verse-ending: three separate sound regions

**In the verse-endings of the sūra there are three separate sound regions, and their boundaries are sharp. This is a countable datum:**

| Verses | Verse-ending sound | Example |
|---|---|---|
| **1-3** | Nunated **-ā** | *ṣaffā, zajrā, dhikrā* |
| **4-11** | Long **ā/ī** + **b / d / q** | *wāḥid, al-mashāriq, al-kawākib, mārid, jānib, wāṣib, thāqib, lāzib* |
| **12-182** | Long **ī/ū/ā** + **n** or **m** | *yaskharūn, mubīn, al-jaḥīm, maknūn, al-ṣābirīn, al-ʿālamīn* |

**The first eleven verses of the sūra are thus recited in two different sounds within themselves; and the hundred and seventy-one verses from the twelfth to the end sit in a single family of sound: a long vowel, then a nasal (nūn or mīm).**

A similar observation was recorded in the section on Sūrat al-Qalam (all fifty-two of its fifty-two verses end in *nūn* or *mīm*). **In al-Ṣāffāt the same order runs over a far longer distance — a stretch of a hundred and seventy-one verses — and it is never broken.**

**I record this as a fact; I set up no reckoning or claim about numbers** — as was written in the section on al-Baqara, no reckoning of letters and numbers is entered into in this commentary. What is recorded is this: after the eleventh verse the sūra passes into a single breath, and that breath does not change to the end.

### The occasion of revelation and the historical place

On the sūra's being Meccan there is something close to unanimity. I do not transmit any report of a particular occasion of revelation; the sūra as a whole sits inside the dispute over God's oneness at Mecca and answers two claims:

1. **The claim that the source of the Qurʾān comes from the jinn** — verses 6-10 correspond to this claim and were treated in detail in `072-cin.md`.
2. **The claim that the angels are God's daughters** — verses 149-166 take up this claim; the same claim was treated at `053-necm.md` 53/21-22 and 53/27-28.

The basmala was treated in earlier sections; the verses are numbered without it.

---

## The backbone of the sūra — the sequence of prophets and the closing moulds

**This section shows the clearest and most easily verifiable structure of the sūra. All the tables below are counts of the text; they are not interpretation.**

From the seventy-fifth verse to the hundred and forty-eighth there are six sections. Each section names a prophet and each closes with a seal. **But the seal is not stamped in the same way on everyone** — and the pieces that are missing are as striking as those that are there.

### The closing moulds — who closes with which mould

| Prophet | Verses | *wa taraknā ʿalayhi fi'l-ākhirīn* | *salāmun ʿalā …* | *najzi'l-muḥsinīn* | *min ʿibādinā'l-muʾminīn* |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Noah** | 75-82 | **78** ✔ | **79** — *salāmun ʿalā Nūḥin fi'l-ʿālamīn* | **80** — *innā kadhālika* ✔ | **81** — *innahu* ✔ |
| **Abraham** | 83-113 | **108** ✔ | **109** — *salāmun ʿalā Ibrāhīm* | **110** — ***kadhālika*** (with **no** *innā* before it) | **111** — *innahu* ✔ |
| **Moses – Aaron** | 114-122 | **119** — *ʿalayhimā* (dual) | **120** — *salāmun ʿalā Mūsā wa Hārūn* | **121** — *innā kadhālika* ✔ | **122** — *innahumā* (dual) |
| **Elijah** | 123-132 | **129** ✔ | **130** — *salāmun ʿalā Il Yāsīn* | **131** — *innā kadhālika* ✔ | **132** — *innahu* ✔ |
| **Lot** | 133-138 | **none** | **none** | **none** | **none** |
| **Jonah** | 139-148 | **none** | **none** | **none** | **none** |

**And once more outside the sequence, in the very middle of the scene of the sacrifice:**

| Place | Expression | About whom |
|---|---|---|
| **105** | *innā **kadhālika najzi'l-muḥsinīn*** | Abraham — at the moment he fulfils the dream, before the account ends |

**The *najzi'l-muḥsinīn* mould thus occurs twice in the Abraham section** (105 and 110). With no other prophet is there this repetition. **This is a fact that can be verified from the text.**

### The introductory moulds — how each is presented

The openings are as ordered as the closings, and there too there is a break:

| Prophet | Opening sentence | Mould |
|---|---|---|
| **Noah** | 75 — *wa laqad **nādānā** Nūḥun* | A verbal sentence: **he called** |
| **Abraham** | 83 — *wa inna min **shīʿatihi** la-Ibrāhīm* | A nominal sentence: **of Noah's following** |
| **Moses – Aaron** | 114 — *wa laqad **manannā** ʿalā Mūsā wa Hārūn* | A verbal sentence: **We bestowed** |
| **Elijah** | 123 — *wa inna Ilyāsa **la-mina'l-mursalīn*** | A nominal sentence: **of the messengers** |
| **Lot** | 133 — *wa inna Lūṭan **la-mina'l-mursalīn*** | A nominal sentence: **of the messengers** |
| **Jonah** | 139 — *wa inna Yūnusa **la-mina'l-mursalīn*** | A nominal sentence: **of the messengers** |

**The last three are in exactly the same mould:** *wa inna + [name] + la-mina'l-mursalīn.* The first three are presented in three different forms.

### What does it mean that some are missing?

**The fact must first be set apart: in the Lot and Jonah sections all four of the four moulds are absent. This is certain. But *why* they are absent is not said in the text, and in the classical commentaries there is no single explanation on this.**

I give in a table the explanations that are transmitted and can be supported from the text. **I do not impose a preference.**

| Explanation | Ground | Its weak side |
|---|---|---|
| **The mould belongs not to those whose people are saved but to those whose people are destroyed while they themselves are praised** | Noah, Abraham, Moses, Elijah — in all four the people are either drowned or destroyed or set in opposition. Jonah's people, however, **believe** (148) | The people of Lot are destroyed too (136); yet there is no mould |
| **The Lot and Jonah sections are short; the mould belongs to the long sections of the sequence** | Lot 6 verses, Jonah 10 verses; the sections with the mould are 8-31 verses | The Jonah section is longer than Lot's, which lacks the mould; number is not the measure |
| **Both sections receive their own separate closing** | Lot: *wa innakum la-tamurrūna ʿalayhim muṣbiḥīn* (137) — a closing that turns to the one addressed. Jonah: *fa-āmanū fa-mattaʿnāhum ilā ḥīn* (148) — a closing that ends with the people's deliverance | This describes the fact but does not give its cause |
| **In the Jonah section there is an element of blame** | 142 — *wa huwa **mulīm*** ("in a state deserving blame"); at al-Qalam 68/48 the same person is named as **a negative example** (*wa lā takun ka-ṣāḥibi'l-ḥūt*) | The section still opens *wa inna Yūnusa la-mina'l-mursalīn*; the praise has not been withdrawn |

**As my own reading I record this, and it is not binding:** the shared work of the four moulds is to announce a **lastingness** — *taraknā ʿalayhi fi'l-ākhirīn* (We left upon him [a remembrance] among those who came after) and then a peace. In the Lot section the lastingness is provided by something else: **the ruin itself.** *"You pass by them in the morning and at night"* (137-138). It is not said "We left"; **what was left is shown.** In the Jonah section the lastingness is in **the hundred thousand who were saved.** This is an explanation, not a ruling.

### The symmetry within the sequence itself

| | Noah | Abraham | Moses-Aaron | Elijah | Lot | Jonah |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Who is saved** | *his progeny* (77) | *himself* (98) | *themselves and their people* (115) | *the mukhlaṣīn* (128) | *himself and his household* (134) | *himself* (145) |
| **The other side** | *aghraqna'l-ākharīn* (82) | *fa-jaʿalnāhumu'l-asfalīn* (98) | *fa-kānū humu'l-ghālibīn* (116) | *la-muḥḍarūn* (127) | *dammarna'l-ākharīn* (136) | *fa-āmanū* (148) |
| **The subject of the verb** | God | God | God | (passive) | God | **The people** |

**The last column is the one exception in the sequence:** in five sections the subject of the verb that determines the end is God; in the Jonah section the subject of the last verb is **the people themselves** — *fa-āmanū* ("they believed"). **This is a difference that can be read off the text.**

### And the closing of the sūra ties to this table

Every peace in the sequence had been given to a single person: *ʿalā Nūḥin*, *ʿalā Ibrāhīm*, *ʿalā Mūsā wa Hārūn*, *ʿalā Il Yāsīn.* **In the hundred and eighty-first verse the peace is given not to one person but to all the messengers at once:**

> *wa **salāmun ʿala'l-mursalīn***

**And this verse takes in the two prophets who received no peace (Lot and Jonah) as well** — because both had been presented as *mina'l-mursalīn* (133, 139). **The sūra, that is, gives collectively at the end the peace it left out in the sequence.**

**I record this as my own reading, and its ground is two textual data:** (a) the word *mursalīn* in the introductory mould of Lot and Jonah, and (b) that the word *mursalīn* in verse 181 is the very same word. Whether this link is made is not said openly in the verse.

---

## 37/1-5 — وَٱلصَّٰٓفَّٰتِ صَفًّا · فَٱلزَّٰجِرَٰتِ زَجْرًا · فَٱلتَّٰلِيَٰتِ ذِكْرًا · إِنَّ إِلَٰهَكُمْ لَوَٰحِدٌ · رَّبُّ ٱلسَّمَٰوَٰتِ وَٱلْأَرْضِ وَمَا بَيْنَهُمَا وَرَبُّ ٱلْمَشَٰرِقِ

*Wa'ṣ-ṣāffāti ṣaffā · Fa'z-zājirāti zajrā · Fa't-tāliyāti dhikrā · Inna ilāhakum la-wāḥid · Rabbu's-samāwāti wa'l-arḍi wa mā baynahumā wa rabbu'l-mashāriq*

"By those drawn up in ranks · and those who drive on, driving · and those who recite a reminder: **your God is one.** The Lord of the heavens and the earth and what is between them, and the Lord of the risings."

### The matter of the oath

The matter of the oath (*qasam*) was set out in this commentary in the section on al-ʿAṣr, widened into a long series in the section on al-Shams, and applied to this mould in particular in the sections on al-Mursalāt and al-Dhāriyāt. **I do not repeat the findings there.**

Its summary is this: in the Qurʾān an oath is not made to prove truthfulness — the one addressed is already disputing the truthfulness of the one who swears — but **to draw attention to something**. What is sworn by is at the same time a **proof**.

### The mould itself — four sūras side by side

**All three of the three oaths have the same structure: a definite active participle (feminine plural) + an accusative verbal noun from its own root.**

| | 1 | 2 | 3 |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Active participle** | ٱلصَّٰٓفَّٰت | ٱلزَّٰجِرَٰت | ٱلتَّٰلِيَٰت |
| **Accusative** | صَفًّا (the same root) | زَجْرًا (the same root) | ذِكْرًا (**a different root**) |
| **Conjunction** | وَ | فَ | فَ |

**Two observations must be recorded:**

**One — the cognate accusative.** In the first two oaths the verb is reinforced with a verbal noun from its own root (*ṣaffan*, *zajran*). In Arabic this is called the **cognate accusative**, and it strengthens the verb: "drawing up in ranks upon ranks", "driving on and on". **In the third this breaks:** beside the word *tāliyāt* (those who recite) comes not *tilāwatan*, from its own root, but ***dhikran*, from another root**. In the third oath, then, there is not reinforcement but **an object**: what is recited is stated.

**The same break was recorded in the section on al-Nāziʿāt**, and there the series was in the form "two different at the outside, three the same within". **Here the break is at the end:** two the same, one different. **This shows that the series is tied in its last verse to a subject** — and the subject it is tied to is *dhikr*, that is, the carrier of the ruling to be stated in the fourth verse of the sūra.

**Two — the conjunctions.** One *wāw*, two *fāʾ*. **فَ** in Arabic announces succession and the absence of interval (*fāʾ al-taʿqīb*). As was recorded in the sections on al-ʿĀdiyāt and al-Dhāriyāt, the order *wāw* + *fāʾ* makes the verses **a single oath**: the three attributes are not three separate oaths but **three steps of a single movement following one upon another.**

### ٱلصَّٰٓفَّٰت — root ص-ف-ف

**The root was analysed in `061-saff.md`.** The definition there: **to set several things side by side on a straight line, in the same alignment.** Its derivatives were given there too (*maṣfūf* — set in rows; al-Ghāshiya 88/15).

**What is to be added here is this:** the word comes here as **a feminine plural active participle** — *ṣāffāt*. Not "those that have been set in ranks", then, but **"those who form ranks / who stand in ranks"**. The verb is active.

**And the root will return at the end of the sūra:** in the hundred and sixty-fifth verse, *wa innā la-naḥnu'ṣ-**ṣāffūn*** — "we, we indeed are those who stand in ranks". **The party speaking there appears to be the very party left unnamed in the first verse.** I will treat this link separately in a later section.

### ٱلزَّٰجِرَٰت — root ز-ج-ر

**The root was analysed in `079-naziat.md` (79/13 — *zajratun wāḥida*) and in `054-kamer.md` (54/4 — *muzdajar*; 54/9 — *uzdujira*). I do not repeat it.**

The definition there: **to drive with the voice, to drive off by shouting, to direct by rebuke.** The concrete use comes from the keeping of animals — the herdsman does not touch the beast, he **shouts**; the beast rises and moves. In the section on al-Nāziʿāt this verse (37/2) was already cited.

**What is to be added here:** at the centre of the word there is **sound**, and there is that sound's **moving something from its place**. *Zajr* does two pieces of work: it **drives on** and it **drives away.** Which of the two comes forward changes according to who is sworn by — this will be seen in the table below.

**And the root occurs once more within the sūra:** in the nineteenth verse, *fa-innamā hiya **zajratun** wāḥida* — "it is but a single cry". **The sūra, that is, names the resurrection by the very act it swore by in its first verses.** This is a countable datum.

### ٱلتَّٰلِيَٰت — root ت-ل-و

**The root was treated in `002-bakara.md` and in `062-cuma.md`** (*yatlū ʿalayhim āyātihi*). **The basic note there holds here too and deserves repeating, because the whole of the verse rests on it:**

**The original sense of the root is not "to recite" but "to follow after".** *Talā* — he came after, he followed. *Tālī* — the next, the one who comes behind. *Wa'l-qamari idhā talāhā* (al-Shams 91/2) — "by the moon when it follows it". There is no reciting there; **there is following.**

**The sense of reciting arises from this picture:** one who recites a text sets the letters **one after another**. *Tilāwa*, that is, is to set words in order and say them in succession.

**And this shows how the three oaths are joined to one another — I record it as my own reading:**

| Verb | Its concrete picture | Shared point |
|---|---|---|
| **صَفّ** | To set side by side | **Order** |
| **زَجْر** | To drive with the voice | **Movement** |
| **تِلَاوَة** | To follow after | **Sequence** |

**The common denominator of the three is "being set in line":** one in place (side by side), one in movement (front to back), one in speech (word by word). **And the last word of the series (*dhikran*) says what it is that is set in line: a reminder.**

### Who or what is sworn by — the disagreement

**The text gives no name.** Through three verses only attributes are set out. **This is a recurring situation in the Qurʾān's series of oaths, and it has met us in three other sūras in this commentary.**

| Sūra | Series of oaths | How many oaths | Where treated |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Al-Dhāriyāt 51/1-4** | *al-dhāriyāt, al-ḥāmilāt, al-jāriyāt, al-muqassimāt* | 4 | `051-zariyat.md` |
| **Al-Mursalāt 77/1-5** | *al-mursalāt, al-ʿāṣifāt, al-nāshirāt, al-fāriqāt, al-mulqiyāt* | 5 | `077-murselat.md` |
| **Al-Nāziʿāt 79/1-5** | *al-nāziʿāt, al-nāshiṭāt, al-sābiḥāt, al-sābiqāt, al-mudabbirāt* | 5 | `079-naziat.md` |
| **Al-Ṣāffāt 37/1-3** | *al-ṣāffāt, al-zājirāt, al-tāliyāt* | **3** | here |

**What the four series have in common:** all are definite feminine plural active participles; in none of them is the name of what is sworn by given; and in all four the same disagreement has arisen.

**Their differences must also be recorded:**

| | Al-Dhāriyāt | Al-Mursalāt | Al-Nāziʿāt | **Al-Ṣāffāt** |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Number** | 4 | 5 | 5 | **3 (the shortest)** |
| **Locked by the Qurʾān's own usage** | *dhāriyāt* → wind (al-Kahf 18/45) | — | — | **none** |
| **Cognate accusative** | none | none | in 3 of them | **in 2 of them** |
| **Return of the root within the sūra** | — | — | *zajra* (79/13) | ***ṣāffūn* (165), *zajra* (19)** |

**The last row is peculiar to al-Ṣāffāt and bears directly on the dispute over preference:** the root of the attribute sworn by is used at the end of the sūra **by a speaking party of itself.**

**Now the views:**

| | **The angels** | **The ranks of those at prayer** | **Birds** | **Mixed / more than one** |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **ٱلصَّٰٓفَّٰت** (those who form ranks) | **Where it is strongest.** Verse 165 of the sūra: *wa innā la-naḥnu'ṣ-ṣāffūn* — that the speakers are angels is the widespread view; also 164: *wa mā minnā illā lahu maqāmun maʿlūm* | Forming ranks in prayer; the Qurʾān uses the word *ṣaff* in this sense too (al-Ṣaff 61/4 — *ṣaffan ka-annahum bunyānun marṣūṣ*) | Birds ranged in the sky with wings spread; the Qurʾān uses the word *ṣāffāt* of birds: *wa'ṭ-ṭayru ṣāffāt* (al-Nūr 24/41), *ṣāffātin wa yaqbiḍn* (al-Mulk 67/19) | Each attribute to a different being |
| **ٱلزَّٰجِرَٰت** (those who drive) | The angels' driving the clouds or driving off the satans; **verses 6-10 of the sūra are exactly a scene of driving off** | The one who prays holding himself back from evil (indirect) | The sound of birds (weak) | The winds / drivers of clouds |
| **ٱلتَّٰلِيَٰت ذِكْرًا** (those who recite a reminder) | **Where it is strongest.** The angels bring down the revelation and recite it; the same field as *al-mudabbirāti amrā* (al-Nāziʿāt 79/5) | **Where it is strongest.** The Qurʾān is recited in prayer; *dhikr* is one of the Qurʾān's own names | **Where it is weakest.** Birds "reciting a reminder" can only be set up as a figure | Prophets / reciters |
| **Its weak side** | For the first two attributes there is no direct indication in the Qurʾān; the word *ṣāffāt* is used in the Qurʾān plainly of **birds** | The second verse (*zājirāt*) is strained; and the Qurʾān does not use this word for the ranks of prayer | The third verse is left unexplained | It conflicts with the single-oath structure set up by the order *wāw* + two *fāʾ* |

### Assessment — and the refusal to impose a preference

**No preference is imposed in this commentary.** I write out the grounds:

**One. There is no name in the text.** The principle recorded in the sections on al-Mursalāt and al-Nāziʿāt holds here too: **when the text swears by something it does not name, this is not a vagueness but a gain.** An oath not locked to a single object can show more than one scene at once.

**Two. In al-Ṣāffāt the language does not narrow as it does in al-Dhāriyāt.** In the section on al-Dhāriyāt the first two verses were locked to the wind by the Qurʾān's own usage (al-Kahf 18/45; al-Aʿrāf 7/57), and for that reason it was recorded there that "the head of the series is locked". **Here the situation is the reverse:** the Qurʾān uses the word *ṣāffāt* plainly of **birds** (al-Nūr 24/41; al-Mulk 67/19) — the intra-Qurʾānic indication, that is, supports not the angel reading but the bird reading. **But the third verse cannot be set up with birds.** The indication pulls one way at the head of the series and another way at its end.

**Three. The indication within the sūra is strong but not decisive.** In the hundred and sixty-fourth and hundred and sixty-fifth verses a company says *"each of us has a known station"* and *"we are those who stand in ranks"*. That the speakers are angels is the widespread view. **This is the one datum within the sūra sharing a root with the first verse.** But there is no explicit connecting sentence between the two places; it is the reader who makes the link.

**If I must state my own inclination — and I record it not as a preference but as an inclination:** the common denominator of the three attributes (order, movement, sequence) describes not so much a particular kind of being as an **operation**: a thing's being set in line, driven on, and delivered in sequence. In the section on al-Mursalāt this link was recorded as "the logic of being sent", in al-Dhāriyāt as "carrying and distributing". **In al-Ṣāffāt its counterpart is this: an orderly chain of carriage.** And the answer to the oath requires it as well: *your God is one.* The claim of oneness is supported in the language not of scattering but of order.

**This is not binding and it eliminates none of the views above.**

### إِنَّ إِلَٰهَكُمْ لَوَٰحِدٌ — the answer to the oath

**And the answer is one of the shortest answers to an oath in the Qurʾān: four words.**

**The word order must be recorded.** The sentence carries two reinforcements: **إِنَّ** at the head and **لَـ** on the predicate (*lām al-ibtidāʾ*). In Arabic, when these two come together the sentence rises to the highest degree of emphasis. **The expression is therefore not "your God is one" but "your God is certainly, beyond any doubt, one".**

**And there is a further point:** the sentence does not say *inna'llāha wāḥid* ("God is one"). It says **إِلَٰهَكُمْ** — **"your God".** The addressee's own admission is taken and a ruling built upon it: whatever it is you serve, it is one. **This is exactly the technique recorded in the section on al-Najm (53/21-22):** to catch the other side not by a measure from outside but by proceeding from his own admission.

***Wāḥid* — root و-ح-د.** Its difference from *aḥad* was treated in detail in `112-ihlas.md`; **I do not repeat it.** The summary there was this: *aḥad* brings forward indivisibility in number, *wāḥid* the absence of anything of its kind. **That *wāḥid* has been chosen here answers directly the other side's claim of "more than one god": a matter of number.**

### وَرَبُّ ٱلْمَشَٰرِقِ — the risings only

**And in the fifth verse there is a notable absence.**

The verse names three domains of lordship: the heavens, the earth, what is between them. Then it adds **a fourth construct:** *wa rabbu'l-**mashāriq*** — "the Lord of **the risings**".

**The settings are not named.**

**Yet in other places the Qurʾān names the two together:**

- *rabbu'l-**mashriqayni** wa rabbu'l-**maghribayn*** — "the Lord of the two risings and the Lord of the two settings" (al-Raḥmān 55/17). **Treated in `055-rahman.md`.**
- *bi-rabbi'l-**mashāriqi** wa'l-**maghārib*** — "by the Lord of the risings and the settings" (al-Maʿārij 70/40). **Treated in `070-mearic.md`.**
- *al-**mashriqi** wa'l-**maghribi*** — the singular pair (al-Baqara 2/115, 142; al-Muzzammil 73/9).

**The Qurʾān thus sets up this construct in four different forms: the singular pair, the dual pair, the plural pair, and here — the plural rising alone.**

| Place | Form | Its counterpart |
|---|---|---|
| Al-Baqara 2/115 | *al-mashriq* + *al-maghrib* | Singular, paired |
| Al-Raḥmān 55/17 | *al-mashriqayn* + *al-maghribayn* | Dual, paired |
| Al-Maʿārij 70/40 | *al-mashāriq* + *al-maghārib* | Plural, paired |
| **Al-Ṣāffāt 37/5** | ***al-mashāriq*** | **Plural, alone** |

**This is a countable datum. Why it is so is disputed:**

| Explanation | Ground |
|---|---|
| **For the sake of the verse-ending** | The verse ends in the sound *-iq* and fits the sound region of verses 4-11; if *maghārib* were added the sound would break |
| **The rising implies the setting** | In Arabic, when one end of a contrasting pair is named the other is understood (*iktifāʾ*). The Qurʾān does this elsewhere too |
| **The rising has been brought forward** | The subject of the sūra is **coming forth and resurrection**; it is the rising, not the setting, that is emphasised |
| **The multiplicity of the risings** | The sun rises from different points through the year; the plural answers to this |

**The first of these explanations (the verse-ending) rests on a verifiable fact; the others are plausible but cannot be proved. I do not impose a preference.**

**And a note of limitation — per the method (`STYLE.md`):** no astronomical information is derived from this verse. That the word *mashāriq* is plural is not a statement of astronomy; describing the changing points of rising over the year in a given place is an everyday use of language in Arabic and rests on the observation of those addressed.

### Context — from the oath to the ruling

**The five verses are built in a single movement:** three oaths (order, movement, sequence) → one ruling (oneness) → the extent of the ruling (the heavens, the earth, what is between, the risings).

**I record this as my own reading:** the answer to the oath rests on number (*wāḥid*), and the oath itself rests on sequence and order. **The sūra, that is, sets down oneness not as an abstract proposition but as the outcome of an operation.** A thing set in line has a single ordering.

### What this says to the present

The shared picture of the three oaths — to be set in line, to be driven on, to be delivered in sequence — describes a **discipline of operation.** The end of the sūra ties this to the human being as well: *"Each of us has a known station"* (164).

I record it thus: what is praised at the opening of the sūra is neither power nor greatness; **it is knowing one's place and waiting one's turn.** This is something measurable in everyday life — a task runs when it is clear who stands where, and jams when it is not.

---

## 37/6-10 — إِنَّا زَيَّنَّا ٱلسَّمَآءَ ٱلدُّنْيَا بِزِينَةٍ ٱلْكَوَاكِبِ · وَحِفْظًا مِّن كُلِّ شَيْطَٰنٍ مَّارِدٍ · لَّا يَسَّمَّعُونَ إِلَى ٱلْمَلَإِ ٱلْأَعْلَىٰ وَيُقْذَفُونَ مِن كُلِّ جَانِبٍ · دُحُورًا ۖ وَلَهُمْ عَذَابٌ وَاصِبٌ · إِلَّا مَنْ خَطِفَ ٱلْخَطْفَةَ فَأَتْبَعَهُۥ شِهَابٌ ثَاقِبٌ

*Innā zayyanna's-samāʾa'd-dunyā bi-zīnatini'l-kawākib · Wa ḥifẓan min kulli shayṭānin mārid · Lā yassammaʿūna ila'l-malaʾi'l-aʿlā wa yuqdhafūna min kulli jānib · Duḥūrā, wa lahum ʿadhābun wāṣib · Illā man khaṭifa'l-khaṭfata fa-atbaʿahu shihābun thāqib*

"We have adorned the nearest sky with an adornment, the stars; and guarded it against every rebellious satan. They cannot give ear to the highest assembly; they are pelted from every side, driven off, and theirs is a lasting punishment. Except one who snatches a fragment — a piercing flame pursues him."

### First a limit

**This subject was treated in detail in the section on `072-cin.md` 72/8-9, and these verses (37/6-10) were already cited there. I do not repeat the findings;** here I write only what is peculiar to al-Ṣāffāt.

**But the note of limitation set out there must be repeated here in bold as well, because these verses are among the most strained passages of the modern period:**

> The verse describes no heavenly body. It recounts no physical event. It gives no astronomical information.

**That road is not taken in this commentary.** No matching will be made with notions such as meteorites, meteors or cosmic radiation. As was written in the section on al-Jinn: this group of verses is part not of a lesson in cosmology but of a **dispute about a source.** One of the accusations levelled at the Prophet in Mecca was that what he said came from a jinn; these passages are the answer to that accusation.

### What is peculiar to al-Ṣāffāt: the sequence

**Sūrat al-Jinn recounted the same scene *from the mouths of the jinn* — "we sought the sky and found it filled". Al-Ṣāffāt recounts the same scene *from outside* and puts it in an order:**

| Step | Verse | What happens |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | 6 | The sky was **adorned** |
| 2 | 7 | The sky was **guarded** |
| 3 | 8 | Listening is **blocked**; they are pelted |
| 4 | 9 | They are driven off, the punishment continues |
| 5 | 10 | **The exception:** if one snatches, a flame follows |

**And the first two steps in the order are worth noting: the adornment first, the guarding after.** Two different pieces of work are laid upon the same thing.

### بِزِينَةٍ ٱلْكَوَاكِبِ — the adornment

**Root ز-ي-ن: to adorn, to make appear beautiful.** The root was treated at `067-mulk.md` 67/5 (*wa laqad zayyanna's-samāʾa'd-dunyā bi-maṣābīḥ*) and at `050-kaf.md` 50/6. **I do not repeat it.**

***al-samāʾa'd-dunyā* — "the nearest sky".** The root د-ن-و and the sense of the word *dunyā* as "what is near" were analysed in detail at `067-mulk.md` 67/5. **I rely on that treatment.** The note there holds here too: the word is not the name of something bad but the name of **what is near**.

**كَوَاكِب — the plural of *kawkab*: a star, a heavenly body.** In al-Mulk, in the same place, *maṣābīḥ* (lamps) was said; here, *kawākib*. **The same sky is named with two different words** — one looking to the function (what gives light), one to the body.

**A variant reading:** the readings *bi-zīnatini'l-kawākib* (with nunation, then an appositive) and *bi-zīnati'l-kawākib* (in a genitive construct) are transmitted. **The difference of sense is not marked:** in the first, "with an adornment — that is, the stars"; in the second, "with the adornment of the stars". **I give no reciter's name.**

### وَحِفْظًا — and the guarding

**Root ح-ف-ظ:** to guard, to preserve. The root was treated in `050-kaf.md` (*kitābun ḥafīẓ*, *awwābin ḥafīẓ*).

**A point of word order:** *ḥifẓan* is in the accusative, and the grammarians count it either as an **object of cause** (*mafʿūl li-ajlih*) or as a **cognate accusative** attached to an elided verb: "…and We guarded, with a guarding".

**But the real point is that the word has been set beside *zīna*.** Two accusatives are attached to the same sentence: **an adornment and a guarding.**

**I record this as my own reading:** the same thing is seen as two different things from two sides. **To one who looks from below, an adornment; to one who tries to climb, an obstacle.** The text sets up this distinction itself — by attaching two accusatives to a single sentence.

### شَيْطَٰنٍ مَّارِدٍ — the rebellious satan

**مَارِد — root م-ر-د.** The lexicographers give its concrete sense thus: **a thing's having no down, leaf or bark left upon it; becoming bare.** *Amrad* — a youth whose beard has not come; *shajaratun mardāʾ* — a tree without leaves; *ṣarḥun mumarrad* — a smooth, plastered tower (in the Qurʾān: al-Naml 27/44 — *ṣarḥun mumarradun min qawārīr*).

**From this a second sense arises:** one on whom no trace of good is left, **stripped bare in rebellion.** *Marada* — he overstepped the bound, he went far in disobedience.

**The Qurʾān uses the same root of the hypocrites as well:** *wa min ahli'l-madīnati **maradū** ʿala'n-nifāq* (al-Tawba 9/101) — "they have gone far in hypocrisy".

**A *mārid*, then, does not mean "bad"; it means "one on whom there is no longer any hold".** This is the picture of the word.

### لَّا يَسَّمَّعُونَ — they cannot give ear

**Root س-م-ع.** The verb here is the assimilated form of the **تَسَمَّعَ** (Form V) pattern: *yatasammaʿūn* → *yassammaʿūn*. **Form V announces "to listen with one's own effort, straining the ear"** — not ordinary hearing, that is, but **listening in which effort is spent.**

**A variant reading and the sense:**

| Reading | Pattern | Sense |
|---|---|---|
| *lā **yassammaʿūna*** | Form V (تَفَعُّل) | **They cannot strain to listen** — they cannot even make the attempt |
| *lā **yasmaʿūna*** | Form I | **They cannot hear** — focused on the outcome |

**There is a difference of sense, and it is worth noting:** the first negates the attempt itself, the second the outcome. **Both are transmitted; I give no reciter's name and impose no preference.**

***ila'l-malaʾi'l-aʿlā* — "to the highest assembly".** The root **مَلَأ** is م-ل-أ (to fill): the company of leading men who fill an assembly. The Qurʾān uses the word of Pharaoh's circle too (*malaʾi firʿawn*). **This construct occurs in only a few places in the Qurʾān, and what it means is not plainly described.** The widespread explanation is that it is the assembly of angels above. **I issue no firm ruling;** the verse does not describe it.

**And note the preposition: *ilā*.** Not "they do not listen to it" but **"they cannot give ear toward it"**. *Ilā* announces a direction. What is blocked, then, is not hearing but **turning toward**.

### وَيُقْذَفُونَ … دُحُورًا — pelting and driving off

**قَذَفَ — root ق-ذ-ف: to hurl something from a distance, to throw.** The Qurʾān uses the same root of Moses' being placed in a chest and **cast into the water** (Ṭāhā 20/39), and of casting a slander as well (*qadhf* in Sūrat al-Nūr).

**The verb is passive: *yuqdhafūn* — they are pelted.** The agent is not named.

***min kulli jānib* — "from every side".** Not one door, that is, has been closed; **the whole perimeter has been closed.**

**دُحُورًا — root د-ح-ر: to push away, to drive off.** In the Qurʾān it occurs in the form *madḥūr* (driven off) (al-Aʿrāf 7/18; al-Isrāʾ 17/18).

**Word order:** *duḥūran* is in the accusative and the grammarians count it either as an **object of cause** ("they are pelted so as to be driven off") or as the cognate accusative of an elided verb. **Both are transmitted; it does not markedly change the sense.**

### عَذَابٌ وَاصِبٌ — a lasting punishment

**وَاصِب — root و-ص-ب.** The lexicographers record two branches of sense:

- **Continuance:** *waṣaba* — it continued, it did not break off. In the Qurʾān: *wa lahu'd-dīnu **wāṣibā*** (al-Naḥl 16/52) — "and religion is His lastingly".
- **Weariness / exhaustion:** *waṣab* — sickness, an ache that wears one out.

**Most of the lexicographers bring the first sense forward here; the second is also transmitted.** If the two senses are joined: **unbroken and wearying.**

**The word occurs in only two places in the Qurʾān** (here and al-Naḥl 16/52), **and in both occurrences the same attribute is given to two opposite things:** the one the lastingness of religion, the other the lastingness of the punishment. **This is a textual datum.**

### شِهَابٌ ثَاقِبٌ — the piercing flame

**The word *shihāb* was analysed at `072-cin.md` 72/8** — root ش-ه-ب: the brightness of fire, a grizzled colour; *shihāb* = **a blazing piece of fire, an ember**. **The most important note there must be repeated: the word is not the name of a heavenly body; it means "flame".**

**ثَاقِب — root ث-ق-ب: to pierce.** *Thiqāb* — a hole; *mithqab* — a drill. From the same root the Qurʾān has *al-najmu'th-**thāqib*** (al-Ṭāriq 86/3), treated in `086-tarik.md`.

**The lexicographers give two senses for *thāqib*:**

| Sense | Its picture |
|---|---|
| **Piercing** | Piercing through the darkness; penetrating |
| **Burning / blazing** | *Thaqaba'n-nār* — the fire blazed, it flared |

**In this context both are at work:** a flame that both pierces the darkness and burns.

### A link within the sūra: the symmetry of the attributes

**There is a balance worth noting, and it is read off the text:**

| Side | Attribute | Root | Its sense |
|---|---|---|---|
| **The satan** | *mārid* | م-ر-د | One on whom there is no hold left |
| **The flame** | *thāqib* | ث-ق-ب | Piercing through |

**I record this as my own reading:** the one is **slippery**, the other **piercing.** The two attributes are set against one another in the same scene. This is an observation about word choice; I do not claim that the text sets up a deliberate contrast.

### إِلَّا مَنْ خَطِفَ ٱلْخَطْفَةَ — the exception

**خَطِفَ — root خ-ط-ف: to snatch a thing away suddenly.** In the Qurʾān: *yakādu'l-barqu **yakhṭafu** abṣārahum* (al-Baqara 2/20) — "the lightning almost snatches away their sight".

**The object is definite: *al-khaṭfa*** — "that snatching". The definiteness here indicates **the kind**: a known, described act of snatching.

**And a point of word order must be recorded:** the sentence comes with *illā* — the absolute obstacle in the eighth verse, that is, is not fully absolute. **A gap is left, but immediately behind the gap comes the pursuit:** *fa-atbaʿahu* — with *fāʾ*, without interval.

**The scene, then, is this:** the snatching is possible, **the escape is not.** This says the same thing as the expression *shihāban raṣadā* ("a watching flame") in Sūrat al-Jinn — there **waiting**, here **pursuing.**

| | **72/9** | **37/10** |
|---|---|---|
| The attribute of the flame | *raṣad* — watching, lying in wait | *thāqib* — piercing through |
| Its time | **Before** the snatching | **After** the snatching |
| Its work | To wait | To catch up |

**The two sūras show the same scene from two different moments.**

### Context

**What is the work of these five verses in the sūra?** In the fourth verse it had been said *"your God is one"*. The sixth verse gives its first proof: **the sky is built by a single ordering and guarded by a single management.**

**And there is a further link:** the second of the attributes sworn by in the first three verses was *al-zājirāt* (those who drive). **Verses six to ten are a scene of driving:** they are pelted, driven off, pursued. **The second word of the oath turns into a scene five verses later.** This is a textual observation; it is not a ruling about who is sworn by.

### What this says to the present

The "measure of knowledge" recorded in the section on al-Jinn holds here too and I do not repeat it. I add only one thing here:

**The two words the sixth and seventh verses set side by side — *zīna* and *ḥifẓ* — describe a bearing:** the same thing is beauty to one and a limit to another. **The limit itself is not ugly.** That an order looks like an adornment from outside and like a wall when one presses against it from within is something with a counterpart in everyday life too.

---

## 37/11-12 — فَٱسْتَفْتِهِمْ أَهُمْ أَشَدُّ خَلْقًا أَم مَّنْ خَلَقْنَآ ۚ إِنَّا خَلَقْنَٰهُم مِّن طِينٍ لَّازِبٍ · بَلْ عَجِبْتَ وَيَسْخَرُونَ

*Fa'staftihim a-hum ashaddu khalqan am man khalaqnā. Innā khalaqnāhum min ṭīnin lāzib · Bal ʿajibta wa yaskharūn*

"Now ask them for their opinion: are they the stronger in creation, or those We have created? We created them from a sticky clay. · Rather, you have marvelled, while they mock."

### فَٱسْتَفْتِهِمْ — ask them for their opinion

**Root ف-ت-ي.** And the semantic field of the root is worth noting: *fatā* — a young man, a youth; *fatwā* — an opinion given on a question; *iftāʾ* — to give an opinion; ***istiftāʾ* — to ask someone for his opinion.**

**The link the lexicographers make is this:** a *fatwā* is the answer that **renews, makes young again** a question that has remained closed — the word, that is, that unties the knot and gives the matter fresh life. I transmit this link as the lexicographers record it.

**The choice of the verb carries a point here.** The verse does not say *fa's-alhum* ("ask them"); it says **fa'staftihim** — **"ask them for their opinion".**

**The difference is this, and I record it as my own reading:** *suʾāl* asks for information, *istiftāʾ* asks for a **judgement**. What is asked of the addressee, that is, is not that he state something he does not know, but that he **reach a decision with what he does know.** The question is not an examination; it is a demand for **a comparison**.

**And the verb occurs twice in the sūra — both times opening a block:**

| Verse | Question | Subject |
|---|---|---|
| **11** | *a-hum ashaddu khalqan am man khalaqnā* | **Creation** — who is the stronger |
| **149** | *a-li-rabbika'l-banātu wa lahumu'l-banūn* | **Attribution** — daughters to whom, sons to whom |

**Both are a "which of the two" question, and both catch the addressee by his own measure.** This is a countable structural datum.

### أَهُمْ أَشَدُّ خَلْقًا أَم مَّنْ خَلَقْنَآ — the structure of the proof

**The proof is this:** can the one who made the greater not make the lesser?

**This proof was treated with the same structure at `040-gafir.md` 40/57** (*la-khalqu's-samāwāti wa'l-arḍi akbaru min khalqi'n-nās*), and it was tied there to `046-ahkaf.md` 46/33 as well. **I rely on that treatment and do not repeat it.**

**The differences here must be recorded — and there are three:**

| | **Ghāfir 40/57** | **Al-Aḥqāf 46/33** | **Al-Ṣāffāt 37/11** |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Kind of sentence** | Statement | Question (*a-wa lam yaraw*) | **Question — but put to the addressee** |
| **What is compared** | Heavens-earth / human beings | The creation of heavens-earth / raising the dead | **They / *man khalaqnā*** |
| **The word of measure** | *akbar* (greater) | *bi-qādir* (able) | ***ashadd* (firmer/stronger)** |
| **Who gives the ruling** | The text | The text | **The addressee** |

**The last row is the most important.** In Ghāfir the ruling is given; here **the ruling is not even asked for — an opinion is requested.** The addressee will give his own answer.

***Ashadd* — root ش-د-د: to bind, to tighten, to make firm.** The root was mentioned in the section on the seventh verse (in connection with the discussion of al-Jinn). **The sense here is not "great" but "firm"** — the comparison, that is, is built on **structure**, not on bulk.

***Man khalaqnā* — "the one whom We created".** **And note: not *mā* but *man*.** In Arabic *man* is used of rational beings. **This is a point the grammarians record**, and it gives rise to this explanation: what is meant may be not only the heavens and the earth but **all rational creatures, the angels included.**

**The disagreement in a table:**

| Who *man khalaqnā* is | Ground |
|---|---|
| **The angels and the beings in the sky** | *Man* indicates the rational; the preceding verses speak of the highest assembly |
| **The heavens, the earth and what is between** | Those enumerated in the fifth verse; the parallel of Ghāfir 40/57 |
| **The peoples of the past** | A later block of the sūra will name the earlier nations (71-73) |

**I do not impose a preference.** The text does not specify, and all three readings accord with the context.

### مِّن طِينٍ لَّازِبٍ — sticky clay

**These two words are analysed here for the first time in this commentary; they were not treated in any other section.**

**طِين — root ط-ي-ن.** The mixture of water and earth: **clay.** The Qurʾān recounts the creation of the human being with different words, and these form a series:

| Word | Its sense | Where |
|---|---|---|
| **تُرَاب** | Dry earth | Al-Ḥajj 22/5; Ghāfir 40/67 |
| **طِين** | Clay (earth mixed with water) | **Al-Ṣāffāt 37/11**; al-Anʿām 6/2; al-Sajda 32/7 |
| **حَمَإٍ مَّسْنُون** | Fetid, moulded loam | Al-Ḥijr 15/26 |
| **صَلْصَٰل كَٱلْفَخَّار** | Dry clay like fired pottery | Al-Raḥmān 55/14 |
| **سُلَٰلَة** | A strained extract | Al-Muʾminūn 23/12 |

**This is an enumerated list; the claim that there is an "order of stages" among the words is widespread in the classical commentaries, but the Qurʾān declares no such order. I issue no ruling.**

**لَّازِب — root ل-ز-ب.** **And the word occurs only here in the Qurʾān.**

The sense the lexicographers give: **sticking, clinging together, adhering to the hand.** *Lazaba* — it stuck, it locked together. *Lāzib* is also used in the sense of **"lasting, inseparable"**: *ṣāra dhālika ḍarbatan lāzib* — "this became an inseparable habit".

**The lexicographers also draw attention to the closeness of the word in sound and sense to *lāzim* (necessary, inseparable);** that the letters ب and م interchange in certain roots is a fact. **I give this link as a transmission from the lexicographers; it is not a finding of my own.**

**And the work of the word is this — I record it as my own reading:** the verse could have said "We created them from clay" and stopped. **An attribute is added: sticky.** Sticky clay stays on the hand, takes shape, but at the same time **soils.** The word carries at once both its capacity to be shaped and its capacity to cling. This accords with the context of the sentence: the side being compared is named not with the sky but **with a substance that stays on the hand.**

### Word order: how the two sentences are joined

**The eleventh verse is two sentences, with no conjunction between them:**

1. *A-hum ashaddu khalqan am man khalaqnā* — a question.
2. *Innā khalaqnāhum min ṭīnin lāzib* — a statement.

**The second sentence is not the answer to the question but its *ground*.** That is: "Are they the firmer? — We created them from sticky clay." The answer is not stated; **what is given is the knowledge that will supply the answer.**

**This is consistent with the verb *istiftāʾ* in the first sentence:** an opinion is asked for, a ruling is not imposed. **The verse leaves a gap that forces the addressee to think.**

### بَلْ عَجِبْتَ وَيَسْخَرُونَ — marvelling and mockery

**بَلْ — in Arabic the particle of retraction (*iḍrāb*): leaving the preceding statement and passing to something else.** A similar use was treated in the section on Qāf (50/2 — *bal ʿajibū*).

**عَجِبَ — root ع-ج-ب: to be astonished, to find strange.** The root was analysed at `050-kaf.md` 50/2 (*bal ʿajibū an jāʾahum mundhirun minhum fa-qāla'l-kāfirūna hādhā shayʾun ʿajīb*). **I rely on that treatment.**

**And here there is one of the most discussed variant readings in the Qurʾān.**

| Reading | Agent | Sense |
|---|---|---|
| **عَجِبْتَ** (*ʿajibta*) | **You** — the Prophet | "You have marvelled (at their denial), while they mock" |
| **عَجِبْتُ** (*ʿajibtu*) | **I** — God | "I have marvelled, while they mock" |

**Both readings are transmitted in the sources on the variant readings. I give no reciter's name.**

**A long discussion has been held over the second reading**, because "to marvel" seems a state requiring ignorance. **The answers given in the classical period fall under two headings, and I give them in a table:**

| Approach | What it says |
|---|---|
| **A solution by way of language** | In Arabic, when the verb *ʿajab* is attributed to God it means not "to meet with something one did not know" but **"to count a thing great / not to approve it"**. Indeed among people too "I marvel at this" most often announces reproach, not ignorance |
| **Consignment (*tafwīḍ*)** | The manner of the attribute is not entered into; how it is is consigned to God. This is the application of the early community's bearing to this question |

**No preference is imposed in this commentary, and the theological dispute is not entered into.** What is recorded is this: **both readings are transmitted, and both set up the same contrast — on one side taking seriously, on the other mockery.**

### وَيَسْخَرُونَ — they mock

**Root س-خ-ر.** It has two branches of sense, and both are used in the Qurʾān:

- **To make submit / to press into service:** *sakhkhara lakumu'sh-shamsa wa'l-qamar* — "He made the sun and the moon subservient to you". This branch was treated in `043-zuhruf.md`.
- **To mock:** to make someone an object of amusement.

**The link the lexicographers make:** one who mocks presses the other into the **service** of his own amusement. The two branches, that is, meet in the common denominator "to make another subject to one's own purpose". **I give this link as a transmission from the lexicographers.**

**And the mood of the verbs is worth noting:** *ʿajibta* is perfect (past), *yaskharūn* imperfect (continuous). **On one side a state that has come about, on the other a behaviour that goes on.** The contrast is set up by tense as well.

**The difference of mood will continue in the next verse:** *wa idhā dhukkirū lā yadhkurūn* — imperfect throughout.

---

## 37/13-17 — وَإِذَا ذُكِّرُوا۟ لَا يَذْكُرُونَ · وَإِذَا رَأَوْا۟ ءَايَةً يَسْتَسْخِرُونَ · وَقَالُوٓا۟ إِنْ هَٰذَآ إِلَّا سِحْرٌ مُّبِينٌ · أَءِذَا مِتْنَا وَكُنَّا تُرَابًا وَعِظَٰمًا أَءِنَّا لَمَبْعُوثُونَ · أَوَءَابَآؤُنَا ٱلْأَوَّلُونَ

*Wa idhā dhukkirū lā yadhkurūn · Wa idhā raʾaw āyatan yastaskhirūn · Wa qālū in hādhā illā siḥrun mubīn · A-idhā mitnā wa kunnā turāban wa ʿiẓāman a-innā la-mabʿūthūn · A-wa ābāʾuna'l-awwalūn*

"When they are reminded they do not remember. When they see a sign they seek to make a mockery of it and say, 'this is nothing but plain sorcery'. 'When we are dead and have become dust and bones, are we really to be raised? And our forefathers of old?'"

### Five verses, one chain

**Five verses build a single movement step by step, and the steps are marked by verbs:**

| Step | Verb | What is done |
|---|---|---|
| 13 | *lā yadhkurūn* | **Not remembering** — passive resistance |
| 14 | *yastaskhirūn* | **Seeking mockery** — active reaction |
| 15 | *qālū* | **Naming** — "this is sorcery" |
| 16 | *a-innā la-mabʿūthūn* | **Objection** — rejecting resurrection |
| 17 | *a-wa ābāʾuna'l-awwalūn* | **Widening** — enlarging the objection |

**The chain widens from silence to speech, from reaction to claim, from themselves to their forefathers. This is a structure read off the order of the verses.**

### وَإِذَا ذُكِّرُوا۟ لَا يَذْكُرُونَ — the same root, in two directions

**Root ذ-ك-ر.** And the verse uses the same root in two different forms:

- **ذُكِّرُوا۟** — Form II, passive: **they were reminded.** Work coming from outside.
- **يَذْكُرُونَ** — Form I, active: **they remember.** Work that must come from inside.

**The sentence says this, then: the outer act was performed, the inner response did not form.** A negation comes between the two forms of the same root.

**And this ties to the third verse.** In the third verse what was sworn by was *al-tāliyāti **dhikrā*** — "those who recite a reminder". **In the thirteenth verse that reminder comes but is not taken.** The sūra shows, that is, that what was carried arrived but was not received.

| Verse | Root ذ-ك-ر | Who |
|---|---|---|
| **3** | *dhikrā* — what is carried | Those sworn by |
| **13** | *dhukkirū / lā yadhkurūn* — what arrives but is not taken | The deniers |
| **155** | *a-fa-lā tadhakkarūn* — "will you still not reflect?" | The deniers |
| **168** | *dhikran mina'l-awwalīn* — what they asked for and rejected when it came | The deniers |

**Four occurrences tell a single story, and this is countable from the text.** In the hundred and sixty-eighth verse the same people will say *"if only a reminder from those of old had come to us"* — **whereas in the thirteenth verse it had come.**

### يَسْتَسْخِرُونَ — mockery in Form X

**In the twelfth verse it was *yaskharūn* (Form I); here it is *yastaskhirūn* (Form X).** The difference of pattern carries sense.

**In Arabic Form X (استفعال) does three pieces of work:**

| Function | Its counterpart here |
|---|---|
| **To seek** | To **seek** to mock — to look for a pretext for mockery |
| **To count a thing as such** | To **count a thing an object of mockery** — to find what one sees laughable |
| **Intensification** | To **go far** in mockery |

**All three are transmitted by the lexicographers; on which comes forward I issue no firm ruling.**

**But the increase between the two verses is plain:** in the twelfth verse they were mocking; in the fourteenth they mock **in the face of a sign**. **As the proof comes, that is, the reaction hardens.** This is a fact read off the order of the verses.

***Āya* — root أ-ي-ي: a sign, a mark, a proof.** The root was analysed in `002-bakara.md`. **Its use here is in the direction of "miracle":** a sign seen, a sign shown.

### إِنْ هَٰذَآ إِلَّا سِحْرٌ مُّبِينٌ — the naming

***In … illā*** is one of the sharpest moulds of restriction in Arabic: "nothing but". **What they say, then, is not a conjecture but a ruling.**

***Siḥr* — root س-ح-ر.** The lexicographers give the root's original picture as **"to show a thing as other than it is, to turn the eye"**. From the same root *saḥar* — the hour before dawn when darkness and light are mixed. **At the centre of the root, that is, there is *confusion*: the state in which one cannot tell what is what.**

**This accusation is the most widespread accusation levelled at messengers in the Qurʾān, and it was treated at `051-zariyat.md` 51/52** (*mā ata'lladhīna min qablihim min rasūlin illā qālū sāḥirun aw majnūn*). **I rely on that treatment.**

**The point here must be recorded:** ***mubīn*** — "plain". **Root ب-ي-ن: to separate, to distinguish.** *Bayān* — distinguishing speech.

**There is a tension in the sentence, then, and I record it as my own reading:** in the root of *siḥr* there is **confusing**, in the root of *mubīn* **distinguishing**. To say "a plain confusion" is to say a confusion that can be distinguished — that is, "a deception whose being a deception is evident". **The choice of words carries the difficulty inside the claim itself.** This is an observation; I do not claim that the text intends it.

### أَءِذَا مِتْنَا وَكُنَّا تُرَابًا وَعِظَٰمًا — and its twin within the sūra

**This sentence occurs twice in the sūra, and between them there is one word of difference. This is a fact that can be verified from the text:**

| Verse | Sentence | Last word | Who says it |
|---|---|---|---|
| **16** | *a-idhā mitnā wa kunnā turāban wa ʿiẓāman* | *a-innā la-**mabʿūthūn*** | The deniers (in this world) |
| **53** | *a-idhā mitnā wa kunnā turāban wa ʿiẓāman* | *a-innā la-**madīnūn*** | **The *qarīn*** (he had said it in this world; it is recounted in the garden) |

**The two words:**

- **مَبْعُوثُون — root ب-ع-ث:** to be sent, to be raised up. **Resurrection.**
- **مَدِينُون — root د-ي-ن:** to be called to account, to be requited. The root was analysed in `001-fatiha.md` (*māliki yawmi'd-dīn*).

**The same objection, then, rejects two different things: the first *the rising*, the second *the reckoning*.** And the sūra gives them from two different mouths — one the deniers as a body, one a single person.

**I record this as my own reading:** at the second occurrence the one who makes the objection is shown in the midst of the fire (55). **The sūra shows in the fifty-third verse where the word said in the sixteenth arrives** — by repeating the sentence and changing who says it.

***Turāb* and *ʿiẓām*.** The ground of the objection is **the dispersal of matter**: dust (formlessness) and bone (dryness). **And the eleventh verse had already answered this:** *min ṭīnin lāzib* — from sticky clay. **The answer to the question "how is dust to be gathered?" was given five verses earlier: when water is mixed in, it gathers.** I record this link as my own reading; the verse does not set it up openly.

### أَوَءَابَآؤُنَا ٱلْأَوَّلُونَ — the widening of the objection

**The sentence begins with an interrogative particle (*a*) and a conjunction (*wa*): *a-wa*.** The grammarians count this as "an attachment to the preceding question": "…we? And our forefathers of old too?"

**A point of word order and of variant reading:** if *ābāʾunā* is read in the nominative it attaches to an elided verb *yubʿathu* or to the noun of *innā*; the accusative reading (*ābāʾanā*) is also transmitted, and then it is attached directly to the noun of *innā*. **The sense does not change markedly.**

**And the logic of the objection must be recorded — as my own reading:** by adding the forefathers the objection is **enlarged**, because more time has passed over them. The argument, that is, is this: "if even the newly dead cannot be gathered, the long dead can never be gathered at all." **This is a proof resting on time, and the verse will answer it not with time but with a single sound:** *fa-innamā hiya zajratun wāḥida* (19). **The sūra's answer speaks not of the span but of the span's irrelevance.**

***Al-awwalūn* — "those of old".** The word will occur twice more in the sūra: 71 (*aktharu'l-awwalīn* — most of those of old had gone astray) and 168 (*dhikran mina'l-awwalīn* — a reminder from those of old). **All three occurrences are from the mouths of the same side or about them.**

---

## 37/18-21 — قُلْ نَعَمْ وَأَنتُمْ دَٰخِرُونَ · فَإِنَّمَا هِىَ زَجْرَةٌ وَٰحِدَةٌ فَإِذَا هُمْ يَنظُرُونَ · وَقَالُوا۟ يَٰوَيْلَنَا هَٰذَا يَوْمُ ٱلدِّينِ · هَٰذَا يَوْمُ ٱلْفَصْلِ ٱلَّذِى كُنتُم بِهِۦ تُكَذِّبُونَ

*Qul naʿam wa antum dākhirūn · Fa-innamā hiya zajratun wāḥidatun fa-idhā hum yanẓurūn · Wa qālū yā waylanā hādhā yawmu'd-dīn · Hādhā yawmu'l-faṣli'lladhī kuntum bihi tukadhdhibūn*

"Say: Yes — and abased at that. It is but a single cry; and at once there they are, looking on. 'Woe to us! This is the day of reckoning,' they say. — This is the day of separation, which you used to deny."

### قُلْ نَعَمْ — a one-word answer

**And this is one of the shortest answers in the Qurʾān.**

**The sixteenth and seventeenth verses had built a long objection:** after we are dead and have become dust and bones? And our forefathers too? **The answer is a single word: *naʿam* — yes.**

**I record this as my own reading:** the objection is not met with a proof of the same length. **The difference between the length of the question and the shortness of the answer shows that the answer is not a debate but an announcement.** The proof had already been given in the eleventh verse.

***Naʿam* — the particle of affirmative answer in Arabic.** It occurs in only a few places in the Qurʾān (al-Aʿrāf 7/44, 114; al-Shuʿarāʾ 26/42; here). **That a rarely used word is placed at exactly this point is worth recording.**

### وَأَنتُمْ دَٰخِرُونَ — a circumstantial clause

**And the answer does not end at "yes"; a circumstance is added.**

**دَاخِر — root د-خ-ر: to be made small, to be brought low, to submit.** *Dakhara* — he was abased; *adkhara* — he brought low.

**The root had occurred at `040-gafir.md` 40/60** (*sa-yadkhulūna jahannama **dākhirīn***) and there it concerned the end of those who grow proud. **The same word is used in the Qurʾān in the context of prostration as well:** *wa li'llāhi yasjudu mā fi's-samāwāti… wa hum **dākhirūn*** (al-Naḥl 16/48-49).

**A point of word order:** *wa antum dākhirūn* is **a circumstantial clause** — it announces, that is, how the verb "you will be raised" will come about.

**And the contrast must be recorded:** the objecting side was **mocking** in the twelfth and fourteenth verses — that is, holding the other in contempt. **The answer turns the same act back on them: brought low.** This turn between *sukhriyya* (mockery) and *dukhūr* (being brought low) is a method the sūra uses often. **I record this observation as my own reading.**

### فَإِنَّمَا هِىَ زَجْرَةٌ وَٰحِدَةٌ — a single cry

**The root ز-ج-ر was treated in the second verse, in reliance on `079-naziat.md` and `054-kamer.md`.**

**And here is one of the plainest internal links of the sūra:** in the second verse the oath was *al-**zājirāt**i **zajrā***. **In the nineteenth verse the name of the resurrection becomes *zajra*.**

| Verse | Form | Who / what |
|---|---|---|
| **2** | *al-zājirāti zajrā* | Those sworn by — those who drive |
| **19** | *zajratun wāḥida* | **The resurrection — a single sound of driving** |

**This is a repetition that can be verified from the text.** And its meaning is this: the act praised at the head of the sūra becomes, later in the sūra, **the resurrection itself.** The picture of the herdsman raising the beast with his voice turns into the raising of the dead by a sound.

**At al-Nāziʿāt 79/13 the same sentence occurs almost exactly:** *fa-innamā hiya zajratun wāḥida · fa-idhā hum bi's-sāhira.* **Treated in `079-naziat.md`. The two sūras use the same sentence and part only at the last word.**

| | **Al-Nāziʿāt 79/13-14** | **Al-Ṣāffāt 37/19** |
|---|---|---|
| Shared | *fa-innamā hiya zajratun wāḥida · fa-idhā hum* | *fa-innamā hiya zajratun wāḥida · fa-idhā hum* |
| Divergent | *bi's-sāhira* — **on the bare plain** | *yanẓurūn* — **they look on** |

**The one gives the place, the other the act.** The two sūras show the same moment through two different framings.

***Innamā* — the particle of restriction: "only".** The whole matter is compressed here: one single sound.

***Wāḥida* — "single".** And the word is from the same root as the *wāḥid* of the fourth verse (و-ح-د). **The sūra uses again, to announce the singleness of the resurrection, the word by which it announced the oneness of God.** This is a textual datum; I set up no claim of intent.

### فَإِذَا هُمْ يَنظُرُونَ — and at once they are looking

***Idhā*** here is the *idhā* of **suddenness** (*fujāʾiyya*): "and at once there you see". It announces no condition; it announces **abruptness**.

***Yanẓurūn* — root ن-ظ-ر: to look, to turn the eye.** The imperfect: they keep looking.

**I record this as my own reading:** the verse does not say "they rise" or "they are raised". **It says "they are looking".** The resurrection is recounted, that is, not by the act itself but by the first movement of the one raised: opening the eyes and looking about.

**And this ties to the fourteenth verse:** *wa idhā **raʾaw** āyatan yastaskhirūn* — "when they see a sign they seek to make a mockery of it". **The same side, in the same verb: in this world he laughs at what he sees, there he stands staring at what he sees.** Two lookings, two reactions.

### يَٰوَيْلَنَا — woe to us

**وَيْل — in Arabic an exclamation announcing ruin, destruction, punishment.** In `077-murselat.md` the refrain *waylun yawmaʾidhin li'l-mukadhdhibīn* was treated in detail. **I rely on that treatment.**

**But the difference here must be recorded:** in al-Mursalāt the *wayl* **was said to them**; here **they say it themselves.** *Yā waylanā* — "woe to us".

| | Al-Mursalāt | Al-Ṣāffāt 37/20 |
|---|---|---|
| Who says it | The text | **The deniers themselves** |
| Form | *waylun … li'l-mukadhdhibīn* | *yā wayla**nā*** |

**The judgement comes, that is, not from outside but from within.** This is a method recurring in the Qurʾān's scenes of the resurrection: to make the addressee pronounce the verdict himself.

### هَٰذَا يَوْمُ ٱلدِّينِ · هَٰذَا يَوْمُ ٱلْفَصْلِ — two names, two mouths

**And the point of word order is very marked here: the same day is named twice, but from two different mouths.**

| Verse | Sentence | Who says it | The name given |
|---|---|---|---|
| **20** | *hādhā yawmu'd-dīn* | **They** | The day of reckoning |
| **21** | *hādhā yawmu'l-faṣli'lladhī kuntum bihi tukadhdhibūn* | **The text / from outside** | **The day of separation** |

**And in the second sentence the address turns to the second person: *kuntum* — "you".** In the twentieth verse they were recounted in the third person (*wa qālū*); in the twenty-first it is said directly to their faces. **In Arabic this transition is called *iltifāt* (a shift in the direction of address).**

**The table "the same day, three names" recorded in the section on Qāf is set up here with two names.** And the two names complete one another: **reckoning** and **separation.**

***Yawmu'l-faṣl* — root ف-ص-ل: to separate, to open a space between two things.** The root was analysed in detail at `077-murselat.md` 77/13-14 (*li-yawmi'l-faṣl · wa mā adrāka mā yawmu'l-faṣl*). **I do not repeat it.**

***Yawmu'd-dīn* — analysed in `001-fatiha.md`.** The branches of the root د-ي-ن — "debt / requital / submission" — were given there.

---

## 37/22-26 — ٱحْشُرُوا۟ ٱلَّذِينَ ظَلَمُوا۟ وَأَزْوَٰجَهُمْ وَمَا كَانُوا۟ يَعْبُدُونَ · مِن دُونِ ٱللَّهِ فَٱهْدُوهُمْ إِلَىٰ صِرَٰطِ ٱلْجَحِيمِ · وَقِفُوهُمْ ۖ إِنَّهُم مَّسْـُٔولُونَ · مَا لَكُمْ لَا تَنَاصَرُونَ · بَلْ هُمُ ٱلْيَوْمَ مُسْتَسْلِمُونَ

*Uḥshuru'lladhīna ẓalamū wa azwājahum wa mā kānū yaʿbudūn · Min dūni'llāhi fa'hdūhum ilā ṣirāṭi'l-jaḥīm · Wa qifūhum, innahum masʾūlūn · Mā lakum lā tanāṣarūn · Bal humu'l-yawma mustaslimūn*

"Gather those who did wrong, and their consorts, and what they used to serve apart from God; and lead them to the road of the blaze. And halt them — they are to be questioned. — What is the matter with you, that you do not help one another? — Rather, today they have surrendered."

### The scene changes — and the commands begin

**With the twenty-second verse the manner of telling changes: from declarative sentences it passes to imperatives.**

| Verse | Command | Is it said to whom |
|---|---|---|
| 22 | *uḥshurū* — **gather** | **No** |
| 23 | *fa'hdūhum* — **lead** | No |
| 24 | *wa qifūhum* — **halt** | No |

**Three commands one after another, and in none of them is the addressee named.** In the classical commentaries it is widely said that the commands are directed to the angels; **but the verse does not state this, and I give no name.**

**I record this as my own reading:** that the addressee is not named makes the scene **visual**. We hear the command, the giver is clear, the receiver is indefinite — and this sets up the impression that the command **is carried out at once**. The step in between is not recounted.

### ٱحْشُرُوا۟ — gather

**Root ح-ش-ر: to drive together and gather what is scattered.** The concrete use of the word comes from the context of war and exile: to raise a community from its place and drive it somewhere. The root was treated at `050-kaf.md` 50/44 (*dhālika ḥashrun ʿalaynā yasīr*) and in `059-hasr.md`.

**And there is *compulsion* inside the word:** *ḥashr* is not an invitation but a **driving**.

### وَأَزْوَٰجَهُمْ — their consorts / their like

**Root ز-و-ج.** And there is disagreement over the sense of the word here:

| View | What *azwāj* means | Ground |
|---|---|---|
| **Their spouses** | Spouses who share the same belief | The most widespread sense of the word; at al-Taḥrīm 66/10 the separate reckoning of spouses is recounted |
| **Their like / those of the same kind** | Those who do the same, their peers | In Arabic *zawj* also means "each of a pair, a like"; at al-Wāqiʿa 56/7 *azwājan thalātha* means "three classes" |
| **Their partisans / their *qarīn*s** | The satans who accompany them | The word *qarīn* will occur in verse 51 of the sūra |

**In `056-vakia.md` the sense of *azwāj* as "class" was treated.** **All three views are transmitted in the classical sources; I do not impose a preference.** The verse does not specify.

**And a note is required — per the method (`STYLE.md`):** even if the first view is adopted, the verse does not say "spouses bear one another's sin". **What those gathered have in common is, in the verse's own words, *that they did wrong*** — *alladhīna **ẓalamū***. The attribute is what is shared, not the kinship.

### وَمَا كَانُوا۟ يَعْبُدُونَ — and the things they served

**Note: not *man* but *mā*.** Not "those whom they served", then, but **"the things they served".** In Arabic *mā* is used of non-rational beings.

**This will tie to the ninety-fifth verse:** *a-taʿbudūna **mā** tanḥitūn* — "do you serve what you carve?" **The same particle, the same work.**

**And the sūra keeps to this choice consistently:** the things they serve are named with *mā*, they themselves with *alladhīna*. **This is a countable datum of the language.**

### فَٱهْدُوهُمْ إِلَىٰ صِرَٰطِ ٱلْجَحِيمِ — and the word turned about

**And this is one of the sūra's harshest choices of word.**

**هَدَىٰ — root ه-د-ي: to lead to a way, to guide.** The root was analysed in `001-fatiha.md` (*ihdina'ṣ-ṣirāṭa'l-mustaqīm*), and as was recorded there, at the centre of the word are **showing a way and leading along it**.

**In the Qurʾān this verb is positive almost everywhere.** Here it is used for leading **to the road of the blaze.**

**And the construct carries the same reversal:**

| Al-Fātiḥa 1/6 | Al-Ṣāffāt 37/23 |
|---|---|
| *ihdinā **al-ṣirāṭa'l-mustaqīm*** | *fa'hdūhum ilā **ṣirāṭi'l-jaḥīm*** |
| "Lead us to the straight path" | "Lead them to the road of the blaze" |

**The same verb, the same word (*ṣirāṭ*), the opposite direction.**

**In Arabic rhetoric this is called *tahakkum* (a mocking style): producing sharpness by using a word in the opposite context.** In the section on al-Wāqiʿa the use of the word *nuzul* (hospitality) for punishment was another instance of the same method, and it was treated in `056-vakia.md`.

***Al-Jaḥīm* — root ج-ح-م: the fire's blazing in its fiercest heat.** *Jaḥmatu'n-nār* — the fire at its fiercest. **And this word occurs six times in the sūra** (23, 55, 64, 68, 97, 163) — **it is the most repeated name of a place in the sūra.**

### وَقِفُوهُمْ إِنَّهُم مَّسْـُٔولُونَ — halt them

**وَقَفَ — root و-ق-ف: to stand, to halt.** And the verb is used here **transitively**: *qifūhum* — "halt them".

**A point of word order and my own reading:** in the twenty-third verse the command was to **lead**; in the twenty-fourth comes the command to **halt.** **The driving, that is, is broken off midway.** They are being led to the road of the blaze but are stopped before arriving.

**The reason is stated at once:** *innahum masʾūlūn* — "they are to be questioned". **The halt, then, is for a questioning.**

***Masʾūl* — root س-أ-ل: to ask.** A passive participle: **the one asked, the one of whom an account is required.** The Qurʾān uses this word elsewhere too: apart from the *wa qifūhum* scene, *inna's-samʿa wa'l-baṣara wa'l-fuʾāda kullu ulāʾika kāna ʿanhu **masʾūlā*** (al-Isrāʾ 17/36).

**And what will be asked is not said here.** The twenty-fifth verse brings a question, but that question is not a question for information.

### مَا لَكُمْ لَا تَنَاصَرُونَ — do you not help one another?

***Mā lakum* — "what is the matter with you?"** In Arabic a mould announcing astonishment and reproach. **It will occur twice more in the sūra:** 92 (*mā lakum lā tanṭiqūn* — to the idols), 154 (*mā lakum kayfa taḥkumūn* — to the deniers).

| Verse | To whom | Question |
|---|---|---|
| **25** | To the deniers | *lā tanāṣarūn* — do you not help one another? |
| **92** | To the idols (from Abraham's mouth) | *lā tanṭiqūn* — do you not speak? |
| **154** | To the deniers | *kayfa taḥkumūn* — how do you judge? |

**All three occurrences are questions about an *inability to function*, and this is a countable repetition.** Two are directed at what is served, one at those who serve — **and in both cases no answer comes.**

***Tanāṣarūn* — root ن-ص-ر, Form VI (تَفَاعُل): reciprocity.** *Tanāṣara* — they helped one another. Its original is *tatanāṣarūn*; one *tāʾ* has dropped.

**The choice of Form VI is worth noting:** the question is not "who will help you?" but **"why do you not help one another?"**. No rescuer from outside is asked after, that is; **what is asked after is the solidarity among themselves.**

**And this is the mirror of their state in this world — as my own reading:** in the twenty-second verse they had been gathered **together** (themselves, their consorts, what they served). They came as a body but can do nothing as a body.

### بَلْ هُمُ ٱلْيَوْمَ مُسْتَسْلِمُونَ — they have surrendered

***Bal*** is again *iḍrāb*: it leaves the question and announces the situation. **The question is not answered; the impossibility of an answer is announced.**

**مُسْتَسْلِم — root س-ل-م, Form X.** The root was analysed in `002-bakara.md`, `050-kaf.md` and `049-hucurat.md`: **to be sound, wholeness, to surrender.**

**The work of Form X here:** *istaslama* — **he surrendered himself entirely, he gave up resisting.** It is a word from the language of war: to lay down one's weapon.

**And the contrast within the sūra is very marked — this is a textual datum:**

| Verse | Word | Root | Who | How |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **26** | *mustaslimūn* | س-ل-م | The deniers | **Perforce** surrendered — because resistance is over |
| **103** | *aslamā* | س-ل-م | **Abraham and his son** | **Willingly** surrendered |

**The same root, the same sūra, two extremes.** In the twenty-sixth verse surrender is a defeat; in the hundred and third it is a choice. **The sūra uses the word twice and lets the scenes make the difference between them.**

***Al-yawma* — "today".** The bringing forward of the adverb creates emphasis: **not yesterday, today.** Yesterday they were resisting.

---

## 37/27-32 — وَأَقْبَلَ بَعْضُهُمْ عَلَىٰ بَعْضٍ يَتَسَآءَلُونَ · قَالُوٓا۟ إِنَّكُمْ كُنتُمْ تَأْتُونَنَا عَنِ ٱلْيَمِينِ · قَالُوا۟ بَل لَّمْ تَكُونُوا۟ مُؤْمِنِينَ · وَمَا كَانَ لَنَا عَلَيْكُم مِّن سُلْطَٰنٍ ۖ بَلْ كُنتُمْ قَوْمًا طَٰغِينَ · فَحَقَّ عَلَيْنَا قَوْلُ رَبِّنَآ ۖ إِنَّا لَذَآئِقُونَ · فَأَغْوَيْنَٰكُمْ إِنَّا كُنَّا غَٰوِينَ

*Wa aqbala baʿḍuhum ʿalā baʿḍin yatasāʾalūn · Qālū innakum kuntum taʾtūnanā ʿani'l-yamīn · Qālū bal lam takūnū muʾminīn · Wa mā kāna lanā ʿalaykum min sulṭān, bal kuntum qawman ṭāghīn · Fa-ḥaqqa ʿalaynā qawlu rabbinā, innā la-dhāʾiqūn · Fa-aghwaynākum innā kunnā ghāwīn*

"They turn to one another, questioning. They say: 'You used to come at us from the right.' The others: 'No, you were not believers to begin with. And we had no power over you; you were a people who transgressed. The word of our Lord has come true against us; we too shall taste it. We led you astray, because we ourselves had gone astray.'"

### وَأَقْبَلَ بَعْضُهُمْ عَلَىٰ بَعْضٍ يَتَسَآءَلُونَ — and its twin in the sūra

**This sentence occurs twice in the sūra, with one letter of difference:**

| Verse | Sentence | Conjunction | Where |
|---|---|---|---|
| **27** | ***wa** aqbala baʿḍuhum ʿalā baʿḍin yatasāʾalūn* | **وَ** | **In the fire** |
| **50** | ***fa** aqbala baʿḍuhum ʿalā baʿḍin yatasāʾalūn* | **فَ** | **In the garden** |

**This is one of the plainest structural data of the sūra, and it is entirely countable.**

**The difference of conjunction is also worth noting:** in the twenty-seventh verse *wāw* (simple joining), in the fiftieth *fāʾ* (succession without interval). **In the garden the conversation begins immediately after the picture of the favours, without a pause; in the fire it comes as a separate movement after the scene of the halting.** I record this as an observation.

***Aqbala* — root ق-ب-ل: to turn toward, to turn one's face.** *Qibla* — the direction faced; *qabl* — front. **The verb announces, that is, a *turning of the face*: they turned to one another.**

***Yatasāʾalūn* — root س-أ-ل, Form VI: to question one another.** In the twenty-fourth verse it had been said *masʾūlūn* (they are to be questioned). **Here they question one another.** The same root, two different interrogations: one vertical, one horizontal. **This is a textual datum.**
