**The two words in verses seventy-eight and eighty-two are from the same root and are distinguished by a single vowel** (*al-ākhirīn* / *al-ākharīn*). **This is a fact of the same kind as the *mundhirīn / mundharīn* pair in verses seventy-two and seventy-three, and it is countable.**

### سَلَٰمٌ عَلَىٰ نُوحٍ فِى ٱلْعَٰلَمِينَ — and the one addition in the sequence

**And in this verse there is an addition not present in the other salutations of the sequence:**

| Verse | The sentence of peace | Is there an addition |
|---|---|---|
| **79** | *salāmun ʿalā Nūḥin **fi'l-ʿālamīn*** | **Yes** — "among all the worlds" |
| **109** | *salāmun ʿalā Ibrāhīm* | No |
| **120** | *salāmun ʿalā Mūsā wa Hārūn* | No |
| **130** | *salāmun ʿalā Il Yāsīn* | No |
| **181** | *wa salāmun ʿala'l-mursalīn* | No |

**An adverbial phrase is added only to the peace given to Noah: *fi'l-ʿālamīn*. This is a fact that can be verified from the text.**

**I record this as my own reading, and it is not binding:** that the addition falls in the **first** salutation of the sequence is striking. The first peace is given with the widest extent, and those that follow are set upon it. **Moreover the seventy-seventh verse had said that Noah's progeny are the ones who remain — the adverb *ʿālamīn* accords with this:** a name remembered among everyone, in a world in which everyone remains within his line. **This is an explanation, not a ruling.**

***Salām* — root س-ل-م.** The root was analysed in `002-bakara.md`, `050-kaf.md`, `056-vakia.md` (56/91 — *salāmun laka min aṣḥābi'l-yamīn*) and `049-hucurat.md`. **The basic note there: the original sense of the root is "wholeness, soundness, freedom from defect".** To give peace is **to wish wholeness** to the other.

**And *salām* comes indefinite:** *salāmun*, not *al-salāmu*. **In Arabic the indefinite is common in sentences of prayer and announces generality (*ʿumūm*):** "peace of every kind".

**A point of word order and my own reading:** the sentence is **a nominal sentence** and has no verb. In Arabic a nominal sentence announces **continuance and fixity.** Not *"let there be peace upon Noah"* but *"there is peace upon Noah"* — not a wish but **a statement of a state.** And this is consistent with the verb "We left" in the seventy-eighth verse: what was left was left in order to last.

### إِنَّا كَذَٰلِكَ نَجْزِى ٱلْمُحْسِنِينَ — the mould of requital

**This sentence occurs five times in the sūra: 80, 105, 110 (without *innā*), 121, 131. It was given in a table in the section on the structure.**

***Najzī* — root ج-ز-ي: to requite, to pay what is equal.** The same field was treated in the thirty-ninth verse (*wa mā tujzawna illā mā kuntum taʿmalūn*) and in the thirty-fourth (*nafʿalu bi'l-mujrimīn*).

***Muḥsin* — root ح-س-ن, the active participle of Form IV.** The root was treated at `051-zariyat.md` 51/16 (*innahum kānū qabla dhālika muḥsinīn*) and at `055-rahman.md` 55/60 (*hal jazāʾu'l-iḥsāni illa'l-iḥsān*). **I rely on those treatments.**

**The definition there holds here too:** *iḥsān* is more than "doing good" — **to do a thing beautifully, completely and with more than is due.** In the root there is *ḥusn* (beauty).

**And the sentence in al-Raḥmān gives the logic of this mould:** *hal jazāʾu'l-**iḥsān**i illa'l-**iḥsān*** — "is the requital of *iḥsān* anything but *iḥsān*?" **The requital, that is, is of the same kind as the act.**

**I record this as my own reading:** this is why the closing mould of the sequence says not *najzi'l-**muʾminīn*** or *najzi'l-**muttaqīn*** but ***najzi'l-muḥsinīn***. The subject of the mould is not belief but **the doing of the work well.** And the next verse states the belief separately: *innahu min ʿibādina'l-**muʾminīn***. **Two verses record two different things: the work he did and the place where he stands.**

### إِنَّهُۥ مِنْ عِبَادِنَا ٱلْمُؤْمِنِينَ — and the last seal of the sequence

**This sentence occurs three times in the singular in the sūra (81, 111, 132) and once in the dual (122 — *innahumā*).**

And the construct is worth noting: *ʿibādi**nā*** — "**Our** servants". **In the fortieth verse it was said *ʿibāda'llāh* ("God's servants").** The two constructs say the same thing, but the one is first person and the other third. **And the closing mould of the sequence prefers the first person** — because the whole mould is built in the first person (*taraknā*, *innā*, *najzī*).

***Al-muʾminīn* — root أ-م-ن: to be safe, to give security.** The root was analysed in `002-bakara.md`.

**And a point of word order:** the sentence is not *innahu muʾminun* but *innahu **min ʿibādina'l-muʾminīn***. **The attribute is not given directly; the person is placed in a group.** In the fifty-second verse the *qarīn*'s speech had the same structure: *a-innaka **la-mina'l-muṣaddiqīn***. **The sūra prefers to name persons not singly but by the group they belong to.** This is an observation about the language.

### ثُمَّ أَغْرَقْنَا ٱلْءَاخَرِينَ — and the one "then" of the sequence

***Aghraqnā* — root غ-ر-ق, Form IV: to drown.** The root had occurred at `079-naziat.md` 79/1 (*wa'n-nāziʿāti **gharqā***) — there as a verbal noun and in a different work.

***Thumma* announces time coming between.**

**And the point of word order is very marked — I record it as my own reading:** the sentence of punishment has been set **behind** the closing mould. The order is this: deliverance (76) → progeny (77) → remembrance (78) → peace (79) → requital (80) → faith (81) → **then punishment (82).**

**The destruction comes, that is, not in the middle of the account but at its end and as an appendix.** And the particle *thumma* pushes it further away still: "then". **The weight of the telling is on the side of those saved; the side that drowned is passed over in one sentence.**

**And the same structure will be repeated in the Lot section:** *thumma dammarna'l-ākharīn* (136) — **the same particle, the same word (*al-ākharīn*), the same position.** This parallel between the two sections is countable from the text.

---

## 37/83-87 — وَإِنَّ مِن شِيعَتِهِۦ لَإِبْرَٰهِيمَ · إِذْ جَآءَ رَبَّهُۥ بِقَلْبٍ سَلِيمٍ · إِذْ قَالَ لِأَبِيهِ وَقَوْمِهِۦ مَاذَا تَعْبُدُونَ · أَئِفْكًا ءَالِهَةً دُونَ ٱللَّهِ تُرِيدُونَ · فَمَا ظَنُّكُم بِرَبِّ ٱلْعَٰلَمِينَ

*Wa inna min shīʿatihi la-Ibrāhīm · Idh jāʾa rabbahu bi-qalbin salīm · Idh qāla li-abīhi wa qawmihi mādhā taʿbudūn · A-ifkan ālihatan dūna'llāhi turīdūn · Fa-mā ẓannukum bi-rabbi'l-ʿālamīn*

"And of those who followed his way was Abraham. When he came to his Lord with a sound heart. When he said to his father and his people: 'What is it you serve? Is it fabricated gods, apart from God, that you want? Then what do you suppose of the Lord of all the worlds?'"

### وَإِنَّ مِن شِيعَتِهِۦ — of his way

***Shīʿa* — root ش-ي-ع.** And the original sense of the root is worth noting: **to spread, to disperse, to multiply.** *Shāʿa'l-khabar* — the news spread; *ashāʿa* — he spread it. **From this *shīʿa* arises: a company gathered around a person and spreading his view.**

**At the centre of the word, then, there are two things: *to follow a person* and *to spread his cause*.**

**The Qurʾān uses the word both positively and negatively:** *wa laqad ahlaknā **ashyāʿakum*** (al-Qamar 54/51) — "We have destroyed your like"; and at al-Qaṣaṣ 28/15 a man is named as *min **shīʿatihi*** (of his party).

**To whom does the pronoun refer?** *Shīʿatihi* — "of his party". **And this is disputed:**

| To whom the pronoun refers | Sense | Ground |
|---|---|---|
| **To Noah** | "Abraham too was of Noah's way" | The nearest name mentioned is Noah (75-82) |
| **To the Prophet** | "Abraham too is of your way" | The addressee of the sūra is the Prophet; and the Qurʾān ties Abraham's religion to the Prophet's |

**The first view is nearer grammatically** (a pronoun refers to the nearest antecedent) **and is transmitted more widely in the classical commentaries. The second is also transmitted. I do not impose a preference.**

**And a note as my own reading:** the choice of the word announces that the sequence is a **chain.** **Abraham is presented not as a separate beginning but as a continuation.** In the thirty-seventh verse it had been said of the Prophet *wa **ṣaddaqa'l-mursalīn*** ("he confirmed the messengers"); here too a prophet is joined to the one before him. **What is built through the sūra is not separate heroes but a single line.**

### إِذْ جَآءَ رَبَّهُۥ بِقَلْبٍ سَلِيمٍ — coming with a sound heart

**And this is one of the most often cited expressions of the sūra.**

***Jāʾa … bi-*** — in Arabic the mould announcing "to bring a thing". **Not "he came", then, but "he brought".** *Jāʾa bi-qalbin salīm* — **he brought his heart.**

**I record this as my own reading:** the mould carries the idea of an **offering.** In the thirty-seventh verse it had been said of the Prophet *jāʾa **bi'l-ḥaqq*** ("he brought the truth") — the same mould. **In both places the sūra uses the same structure: a person brings something.** The one the truth, the other his heart.

***Salīm* — root س-ل-م.** And this is the third place in which the sūra uses the s-l-m root:

| Verse | Word | Pattern | Who |
|---|---|---|---|
| **26** | *mustaslimūn* | Active participle of Form X | The deniers — surrendered perforce |
| **84** | ***salīm*** | The *faʿīl* adjective | **Abraham's heart** |
| **103** | *aslamā* | A Form IV verb | **Abraham and his son** — surrendered willingly |
| **79, 109, 120, 130, 181** | *salām* | A verbal noun | **The sentences of peace** |

**This is a countable fact and shows that this is one of the densest roots of the sūra.**

**The *faʿīl* pattern** in Arabic can carry both an active and a passive sense. **The senses transmitted for *salīm* here:**

| Sense | What it says |
|---|---|
| **Without defect, sound** | Free of illness — the original sense of the word *salīm* |
| **Purified of associating partners** | Clean of setting up partners |
| **Clean of rancour and envy** | Moral purification |
| **Surrendered** | *Salīm* in the sense of "one who surrenders" |

**All these senses are transmitted in the classical commentaries, and most exegetes count them as complementary. I impose no preference.**

**And an intra-Qurʾānic link:** *illā man ata'llāha bi-**qalbin salīm*** (al-Shuʿarāʾ 26/89). **The same construction, the same verbal mould (*atā … bi-*).** **And there too the words are Abraham's.** This is a textual datum.

***Qalb* — root ق-ل-ب: to turn, to turn over.** This sense of the root was treated in `050-kaf.md` and `002-bakara.md`. **The name of the organ, that is, means "what turns, what passes from state to state".** That the attribute *salīm* is given to this root is worth noting: **soundness is asked of a thing that is continually changing.**

### مَاذَا تَعْبُدُونَ — a question, or a reproach?

**Abraham's first utterance is a question. And the question does not ask for information.**

**In Arabic this is called *istifhām inkārī* (an interrogative of denial) or *tawbīkh* (an interrogative of reproach):** to ask about something whose answer is known, so as to bring the addressee face to face with his own answer.

**And note: not *man taʿbudūn* (whom do you serve) but *mādhā taʿbudūn* (what do you serve).** **The same particle was used in the twenty-second verse:** *wa **mā** kānū yaʿbudūn.* **The sūra names the things served consistently with the particle for non-rational beings.** This is a countable datum of the language.

***Li-abīhi wa qawmihi* — "to his father and his people".** **The order is worth noting: first the father, then the people.** He begins from the nearest.

### أَئِفْكًا ءَالِهَةً دُونَ ٱللَّهِ تُرِيدُونَ — a point of word order

**And this verse is one of the densest sentences in the sūra in point of word order.**

**The ordinary order would have been:** *a-turīdūna ālihatan ifkan dūna'llāh.* **In the verse the order has been changed entirely:**

| Order | Word | Where it would ordinarily be |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | **إِفْكًا** | At the end (an adjective / circumstantial) |
| 2 | **ءَالِهَةً** | After the verb (the object) |
| 3 | **دُونَ ٱللَّهِ** | After the object |
| 4 | **تُرِيدُونَ** | **At the head (the verb)** |

**The verb, that is, has been thrown to the very end, and the word *ifk* has been brought to the very front.**

**In Arabic bringing forward (*taqdīm*) sets up emphasis and *ḥaṣr*.** **The centre of gravity of the sentence becomes not "do you want?" but "is it a fabrication?"**

**I record this as my own reading:** the question is not "do you serve?" but **"is it a fabricated thing that you want?"** The emphasis falls not on the verb but on the **quality** of the object. Abraham questions not the serving but the **kind** of what is served.

***Ifk* — root أ-ف-ك: to turn a thing from its direction, to overturn it.** *Afaka* — he turned it; *maʾfūk* — one whose reason has been turned. **The same root was treated in the Qurʾān at `051-zariyat.md` 51/9:** *yuʾfaku ʿanhu man ufik* — "turned away from it is he who is turned".

**And the picture of the root is worth noting:** *ifk* does not mean exactly "a lie"; it means **"speech that has turned the truth about"**. A lie is a fabrication; *ifk* is the overturning of what exists.

**And the word will occur once more in the sūra:** *alā innahum min **ifkihim** la-yaqūlūn · walada'llāh* (151-152) — "out of their fabrication they say 'God has begotten'". **Both occurrences do the same work: fabricating an attribution.** This is a countable repetition.

***Dūna'llāh* — "apart from God, short of God".** *Dūn* in Arabic announces "below, short of". It had occurred in the twenty-third verse too (*min dūni'llāh*).

### فَمَا ظَنُّكُم بِرَبِّ ٱلْعَٰلَمِينَ — the second question

**And Abraham's second question does different work from the first.**

***Ẓann* — root ظ-ن-ن: an opinion that is not certain, supposing.** **But the root is two-directional in Arabic:** sometimes it means "to suppose" (a weak opinion), sometimes "to know with certainty". Both uses are present in the Qurʾān: *alladhīna **yaẓunnūna** annahum mulāqū rabbihim* (al-Baqara 2/46 — certainty here).

**What is transmitted about its sense here:**

| Explanation | What the question means |
|---|---|
| **"What do you suppose?"** | "What do you suppose He will do to you?" — a question of threat |
| **"What do you take Him to be?"** | "What do you take the Lord of all the worlds to be, that you leave Him and turn to these?" — a question of comparison |

**Both are transmitted; I do not impose a preference.** The sentence is open to both readings.

**And the order of the two questions is worth noting — as my own reading:** the first question asked about **what they serve** (85-86); the second asks about **what they do not serve** (87). **Abraham shows first what is in their hand, then what is not.** And the object of the second question is given not with a name but with a construct: *rabbu'l-**ʿālamīn*** — the Lord of all the worlds. **The other side's gods are "ālihatan" (plural, indefinite), what Abraham names is "the Lord of all the worlds" (singular, all-encompassing).**

***Rabbu'l-ʿālamīn* — the same construct as in the last verse of the sūra:** *wa'l-ḥamdu li'llāhi **rabbi'l-ʿālamīn*** (182). **The sūra closes with the construct it set in Abraham's mouth.** This is a fact that can be verified from the text.

---

## 37/88-90 — فَنَظَرَ نَظْرَةً فِى ٱلنُّجُومِ · فَقَالَ إِنِّى سَقِيمٌ · فَتَوَلَّوْا۟ عَنْهُ مُدْبِرِينَ

*Fa-naẓara naẓratan fi'n-nujūm · Fa-qāla innī saqīm · Fa-tawallaw ʿanhu mudbirīn*

"Then he cast a glance at the stars and said, 'I am sick'. Thereupon they turned their backs and left him."

### First a limit

**These three verses are among the places in the sūra that require the most care. The reason is this: the text says very little, and what it does not say has been filled in, in the classical sources, in very different ways.**

**What the text says is this, and this is all of it:**

1. He looked once at the stars.
2. He said "I am sick".
3. They turned and went.

**What the text does not say:** why he looked, what he saw, what he meant by "sick", why they went, where they went. **None of this is in the verse.**

**In this commentary those gaps are not filled with details of the *isrāʾīliyyāt* kind.** I will give the classical explanations in a table and **will set up no firm ruling.**

### فَنَظَرَ نَظْرَةً — a glance

***Naẓara* — root ن-ظ-ر: to look.** And there is a cognate accusative: *naẓratan* — **"a glance".**

**And the word *naẓra* is in the *marra* pattern** (the *faʿla* pattern): **it announces a single occurrence.** Not "he kept looking", then, but **"he looked once".**

**This is a linguistic datum read off the text and worth noting:** the verse describes not a continued observation but **a single, brief glance.**

***Fi'n-nujūm*** — "**in** the stars". **And the preposition is *fī*, not *ilā*.** *Naẓara ilā* is "to look at", while *naẓara fī* in Arabic generally means **"to think about a thing, to examine it"**. **The grammarians record this distinction.**

**The expression is therefore closer to "he cast an eye over the stars / he considered them" than to "he looked at the stars". I give this as a linguistic note.**

***Nujūm* — the plural of *najm*, root ن-ج-م: to rise, to come forth.** The root was analysed in detail in `053-necm.md`, and it was recorded there that the word is used of plants too (*wa'n-najmu wa'sh-shajaru yasjudān* — al-Raḥmān 55/6). **I rely on that treatment.**

### إِنِّى سَقِيمٌ — "I am sick"

***Saqīm* — root س-ق-م: illness, the breakdown of the body.** *Saqam* — illness.

**And the word occurs once more in the sūra — this is a countable fact:**

| Verse | Who | Context |
|---|---|---|
| **89** | **Abraham** | *fa-qāla innī **saqīm*** — he says it himself |
| **145** | **Jonah** | *fa-nabadhnāhu bi'l-ʿarāʾi wa huwa **saqīm*** — the text says it |

**The same word, two prophets, two different situations.** One says it of himself, of the other it is said. **Whether the text sets up this link is not clear; the sharing of the word is a datum.**

### The classical explanations — the table of disagreement

**What this expression means is one of the most disputed questions of the classical commentaries. I give the principal explanations transmitted. The table is not a list of preferences.**

| Explanation | What it says | Ground | Its weak side |
|---|---|---|---|
| **He really was unwell** | Abraham was at that time bodily ill or about to fall ill; what he said is true | The simplest reading; the dictionary sense of the word | The verse speaks of no illness; and in the following verses he does not behave like a sick man (he breaks the idols) |
| **A reference to the future** | "I shall be sick" — in Arabic the present form can point to the future; every human being who will die is in a sense sick | This usage is transmitted in Arabic | It has been found strained |
| **He was sick at heart** | The illness is the name not of the body but of the distress caused by the association he sees: "I am sick of this state of yours" | *Saqīm* is used in Arabic for inward distress too | The verse gives no reason |
| **An oblique utterance (*maʿārīḍ*)** | The utterance is one that is in fact true but that the addressee understands otherwise. In Arabic this is called *taʿrīḍ* and is not counted a lie | The most widespread explanation in the classical period; it is true that every man is in a way "sick" | The explanation presupposes the aim of the utterance (to be parted from them) — the verse does not say this |
| **The looking at the stars was a way of framing an excuse** | His people were a community that looked at the stars and drew judgements from them; Abraham spoke in their own language | That the people gave weight to heavenly bodies is implied elsewhere in the Qurʾān (al-Anʿām 6/76-78) | The verse does not say the people had such a custom |

**And a further note is required.** In some sources this expression is taken up together with the report of "three utterances" attributed to Abraham. **I am not in a position to assess the text and the chain of that report; for that reason I do not transmit it and build nothing upon it.**

**No firm ruling is set up in this commentary.** What is recorded is this: **the verse gives no explanation, and to fill in the explanation that was not given changes the work the verse does.**

**As my own reading I add only this, and it is not binding:** all three of the three verses are joined with *fāʾ* (*fa-naẓara*, *fa-qāla*, *fa-tawallaw*) — that is, **an uninterrupted chain.** Between the look, the utterance and the departure there is no explanation whatever. **That the telling is this fast suggests that the detail is withheld deliberately.** The text recounts not what happened but **what it resulted in.**

### فَتَوَلَّوْا۟ عَنْهُ مُدْبِرِينَ — they turned their backs and left

***Tawallā* — root و-ل-ي, Form V: to turn one's face away and go.** The root was treated in `092-leyl.md` and `080-abese.md`.

***Mudbirīn* — root د-ب-ر: the back.** *Adbara* — he turned his back. An active participle in the plural, in a circumstantial position.

**And the two words announce the same thing: *tawallaw* and *mudbirīn*.** In Arabic this is called reinforcement — the same sense said with two words.

**The Qurʾān uses the same pair elsewhere too:** *thumma **wallaytum mudbirīn*** (al-Tawba 9/25).

**And the contrast within the sūra is worth noting — this is a textual datum:**

| Verse | Verb | Who | Direction |
|---|---|---|---|
| **90** | *tawallaw … **mudbirīn*** | The people | **Moving away** |
| **94** | *fa-**aqbalū** ilayhi yaziffūn* | The people | **Approaching** |

**The same people, four verses apart, two opposite directions.** And the two verbs are opposites by their very roots: *dubur* (the back) and *qubul* (the front). **The sūra sets up this contrast by its choice of words.**

---

## 37/91-93 — فَرَاغَ إِلَىٰٓ ءَالِهَتِهِمْ فَقَالَ أَلَا تَأْكُلُونَ · مَا لَكُمْ لَا تَنطِقُونَ · فَرَاغَ عَلَيْهِمْ ضَرْبًۢا بِٱلْيَمِينِ

*Fa-rāgha ilā ālihatihim fa-qāla alā taʾkulūn · Mā lakum lā tanṭiqūn · Fa-rāgha ʿalayhim ḍarban bi'l-yamīn*

"Then he slipped away to their gods and said: 'Will you not eat? What is the matter with you, that you do not speak?' Then he turned upon them, striking with his right hand."

### رَاغَ — and its link with `051-zariyat.md`

***Rāgha* — root ر-و-غ.** **The root was analysed in detail at `051-zariyat.md` 51/26** (*fa-rāgha ilā ahlihi fa-jāʾa bi-ʿijlin samīn*) **and I do not repeat it.**

The definition there: **to draw aside quietly, without being noticed; to slip off by changing direction.** *Rāgha'th-thaʿlab* — the fox fled by turning aside; *murāwagha* — to dodge, to turn by feinting.

**And what must be recorded here is this: the same verb, the same prophet, two different sūras, two different pieces of work — and twice in al-Ṣāffāt.**

| Place | Expression | To where / to whom | For what |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Al-Dhāriyāt 51/26** | *fa-rāgha **ilā ahlihi*** | **To his household** | To prepare food for the guests |
| **Al-Ṣāffāt 37/91** | *fa-rāgha **ilā ālihatihim*** | **To their gods** | To speak / to call to account |
| **Al-Ṣāffāt 37/93** | *fa-rāgha **ʿalayhim*** | **Upon them** | To strike |

**This is a fact that can be verified from the text, and in all three occurrences the shared feature of the verb is the same: a movement made quietly, without notice.**

**In the section on al-Dhāriyāt its counterpart in the etiquette of hospitality was recorded:** the host does not say "I will prepare food for you", **he goes out without notice.** **Here the same fineness works in the opposite direction:** the people have gone, Abraham has stayed behind, and **without notice to anyone** he enters the house of idols.

### The preposition changes: إِلَىٰ → عَلَىٰ

**And one of the finest points of word order in the sūra is here. The same verb takes two different prepositions two verses apart:**

| Verse | Expression | Preposition | Its sense |
|---|---|---|---|
| **91** | *fa-rāgha **ilā** ālihatihim* | **إِلَىٰ** — direction toward | He slipped **toward** them |
| **93** | *fa-rāgha **ʿalayhim*** | **عَلَىٰ** — over, upon | He came **upon** them |

**In Arabic *ilā* announces going in a direction, *ʿalā* going up onto / coming upon a thing.** **The same verb, that is, passes from speaking to striking between two verses, and the passage is marked only by the preposition.**

**This is a linguistic fact that can be verified from the text and has been found worth noting.**

### أَلَا تَأْكُلُونَ · مَا لَكُمْ لَا تَنطِقُونَ — two questions

**And the two questions Abraham puts to the idols tie to other places in the sūra.**

**The first question: *alā taʾkulūn* — "will you not eat?"**

**This question sits in a context: food being set before the idols.** The verse does not say this openly, but the question requires it. **I write no more than that.**

**And at `051-zariyat.md` 51/27 the same sentence had occurred** — but there Abraham said it **to his guests**: *fa-qarrabahu ilayhim qāla **alā taʾkulūn***. **The same sentence, the same person, two different addressees.**

| Place | To whom *alā taʾkulūn* is said | The answer |
|---|---|---|
| **Al-Dhāriyāt 51/27** | **To the guests** (angels) | They do not eat — and Abraham is afraid (51/28) |
| **Al-Ṣāffāt 37/91** | **To the idols** | They do not eat — and Abraham strikes |

**This is one of the most striking overlaps of wording between the two sūras and is entirely a textual datum.** **I record this as my own reading:** in both places the same sentence marks the moment of **an expectation going unmet.** But the outcome is opposite: in the one fear, in the other action.

**The second question: *mā lakum lā tanṭiqūn* — "what is the matter with you, that you do not speak?"**

***Naṭaqa* — root ن-ط-ق: to speak, to utter.** *Manṭiq* — speech, utterance; *nuṭq* — the faculty of speech.

**And the *mā lakum* mould occurs for the third time in the sūra** (25, 92, 154). **It was given above in a table.** This occurrence stands in the middle of the three and is the only one of the questions directed at the side that is served.

**I record this as my own reading:** in the twenty-fifth verse **the deniers** were asked *"why do you not help one another?"*; in the ninety-second **their gods** are asked *"why do you not speak?"* **Both questions show an inability to function and both go unanswered.** The sūra applies the same method both to the one who serves and to what is served.

### ضَرْبًۢا بِٱلْيَمِينِ — striking with the right hand

***Ḍarb* — root ض-ر-ب: to strike.** And the word is in the accusative, in the position of a **cognate accusative**: it reinforces the verb.

**A point of word order:** *fa-rāgha ʿalayhim **ḍarban*** — the grammarians count *ḍarban* either as the cognate accusative of *rāgha* (attached to an elided verb *yaḍribu*) or as a circumstantial. **Both solutions are transmitted; the sense does not change.**

***Bi'l-yamīn* — "with the right".** **And the expression occurs for the second time in the sūra:**

| Verse | Expression | Who | Its sense |
|---|---|---|---|
| **28** | *taʾtūnanā **ʿani'l-yamīn*** | The deniers (to one another) | **Disputed** — the table was given above |
| **93** | *ḍarban **bi'l-yamīn*** | Abraham | **Disputed** — below |

**The same word, the same sūra, two different prepositions (*ʿan* / *bi*), two different disagreements. This is a textual datum.**

**And on the sense of *yamīn* here too there is a difference of view:**

| View | What *bi'l-yamīn* means | Ground |
|---|---|---|
| **With the right hand** | Physical: he struck with his right hand | The most widespread sense of the word; the right hand is the strong hand for striking |
| **With force** | *Yamīn* in the sense of strength (al-Ḥāqqa 69/45) | The second branch of the root |
| **By an oath / because of a vow** | He struck as required by an oath he had sworn earlier | At al-Anbiyāʾ 21/57 it is transmitted that Abraham said *wa ta'llāhi la-akīdanna aṣnāmakum*; this may be tied to that verse |

**All three are transmitted in the classical sources. The third view has an intra-Qurʾānic ground, and this is worth noting;** but the verse does not set up such a link here. **I do not impose a preference.**

**And a note as my own reading:** the first and the second sense do not exclude one another — in Arabic *yamīn* means the right hand precisely because it is the "strong hand". **The third sense, however, requires a separate piece of information, and the text does not give it.**

---

## 37/94-98 — فَأَقْبَلُوٓا۟ إِلَيْهِ يَزِفُّونَ · قَالَ أَتَعْبُدُونَ مَا تَنْحِتُونَ · وَٱللَّهُ خَلَقَكُمْ وَمَا تَعْمَلُونَ · قَالُوا۟ ٱبْنُوا۟ لَهُۥ بُنْيَٰنًا فَأَلْقُوهُ فِى ٱلْجَحِيمِ · فَأَرَادُوا۟ بِهِۦ كَيْدًا فَجَعَلْنَٰهُمُ ٱلْأَسْفَلِينَ

*Fa-aqbalū ilayhi yaziffūn · Qāla a-taʿbudūna mā tanḥitūn · Wa'llāhu khalaqakum wa mā taʿmalūn · Qālū'bnū lahu bunyānan fa-alqūhu fi'l-jaḥīm · Fa-arādū bihi kaydan fa-jaʿalnāhumu'l-asfalīn*

"They came hurrying toward him. He said: 'Do you serve what you yourselves carve? When God created you and what you make.' They said: 'Build him a structure and cast him into the blaze.' They wanted a plot against him; and We made them the lowest."

### يَزِفُّونَ — hurrying

**Root ز-ف-ف.** And the concrete picture of the word is worth noting: **the running of the ostrich** — going fast with its wings spread, half flying and half running. *Zaffa'ẓ-ẓalīm* — the ostrich went fast.

**From the same root *zifāf* is the leading of a bride to the bridegroom's house** — the Turkish word *zifaf* ("the bridal night") comes from this root. **The link the lexicographers make: in both there is a fast, collective leading.**

**The word occurs only here in the Qurʾān.**

**And a variant reading is transmitted:** *yaziffūn* (Form I) and *yuziffūn* (Form IV). **In the second the sense becomes "urging one another on" or "urging their camels on". The sense does not change markedly; I give no reciter's name.**

**And the contrast with the ninetieth verse was given above in a table:** they had turned away and gone (*mudbirīn*), now they come back hurrying (*aqbalū*). **The sūra shows the same community twice, in two opposite directions.**

### أَتَعْبُدُونَ مَا تَنْحِتُونَ — what you carve?

**And Abraham's question is the densest sentence of the whole section.**

***Naḥata* — root ن-ح-ت.** **This root is analysed here for the first time in this commentary.** Its sense: **to give shape to a hard material (stone, wood) by carving.** *Nuḥāta* — the chips that fall in carving; *manḥūt* — carved.

**The Qurʾān uses the root of Thamūd too:** *wa **tanḥitūna** mina'l-jibāli buyūtan fārihīn* (al-Shuʿarāʾ 26/149) and *kānū **yanḥitūna** mina'l-jibāli buyūtan āminīn* (al-Ḥijr 15/82).

**And the choice of the verb carries the whole proof — I record it as my own reading:** Abraham does not say "do you serve stone?" **He says "do you serve what you carve?"** **What is brought forward, that is, is not the material of the object but *whose hand it came from*.** The centre of the proof is not in the material but **in the making.**

**And the verb is imperfect: *tanḥitūn*** — "you carve". **The work is not finished, then, but going on.** What is served is still in production.

### وَٱللَّهُ خَلَقَكُمْ وَمَا تَعْمَلُونَ — two readings

**And there are two readings of the *mā* in the last word of this sentence. This is one of the classical questions of the Arabic language, and I give it in a table.**

| Reading | What *mā* is | The sense of the sentence | Ground |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Relative (*mawṣūla*)** | "the thing that you…" | "God created you and **the things you make** (the idols you carve)" | In the preceding verse *mā tanḥitūn* is relative in the same way; that the two *mā*s do the same work is consistent in point of word order |
| **Verbal-noun (*maṣdariyya*)** | "your making" | "God created you and **your making** (your act, your work)" | In Arabic *mā* is also used as a verbal-noun particle; *wa mā taʿmalūn* = *wa ʿamalakum* |

**Both readings are transmitted by the grammarians, and both have been defended in the classical commentaries.**

**And a note is required — per the method (`STYLE.md`):** **this verse has stood at the centre of a long dispute in theology.** The second reading (verbal-noun) has been counted one of the grounds of that dispute. **That dispute is not entered into in this commentary** — because the matter stands here as a matter of language, and both readings are linguistically possible.

**What can be recorded in point of word order is this, and it is not a ruling:** in the ninety-fifth verse *mā tanḥitūn* is plainly relative ("what you carve"). The *mā taʿmalūn* of the ninety-sixth comes immediately after it and is in the same mould. **That the two verses come one after another gives the first reading an ease in point of word order.** This does not eliminate the second reading.

**And the proof itself arrives at the same place on either reading:** both the maker and the made are created. **What is created cannot serve what is created.**

***Wa'llāhu khalaqakum* — a point of word order:** the sentence begins with *wāw*, and the grammarians count this as **the *wāw* of circumstance**: "…you serve, **when** God created you." **The sentence, that is, is not a separate piece of information but an objection set inside the question.**

### قَالُوا۟ ٱبْنُوا۟ لَهُۥ بُنْيَٰنًا — and the form of the answer

**And the people's answer is worth noting: not a proof but a command.**

**Abraham had asked two questions (95-96). No answer comes.** Instead they give instructions to one another.

**I record this as my own reading:** in the ninety-second verse Abraham had asked the idols *"why do you not speak?"* **In the ninety-seventh verse the people too give no answer to the question.** Both sides do the same thing: not respond to a question. **The sūra does not set up this parallel; I record it as an observation.**

***Ubnū lahu bunyānan* — the verb and the object are from the same root (ب-ن-ي).** **This is the third time in the sūra:** *qāla qāʾilun* (51), *yaʿmali'l-ʿāmilūn* (61), *ubnū … bunyānan* (97). **This is a countable linguistic fact.**

***Bunyān* — a structure, a building.** **What it was is not said.** In the classical commentaries it is said to be a furnace or a wall built for kindling a fire. **The verse does not give it, and neither do I.**

***Fa-alqūhu fi'l-jaḥīm* — "and cast him into the blaze".** **And the choice of word is worth noting: not *nār* (fire) but *al-jaḥīm*.**

***Al-jaḥīm* occurs for the sixth time in the sūra** (23, 55, 64, 68, 97, 163). **And this is the only place where the word is used of a fire in this world.**

**I record this as my own reading, and it is not binding:** throughout the sūra *al-jaḥīm* is the name of the punishment of the hereafter. In the ninety-seventh verse the same word is used for a fire kindled by a human community. **Human beings, that is, set up with their own hands a thing bearing the name of the punishment of the hereafter** — and in the next verse its outcome comes: *fa-jaʿalnāhumu'l-asfalīn.*

***Alqū* — root ل-ق-ي, Form IV: to cast.** The root was treated in detail in `050-kaf.md`, and it was given there in a table that the same root is used in that sūra in four different pieces of work (*alqaynā*, *yatalaqqā*, *alqiyā*, *alqa's-samʿ*). **And *alqiyā fī jahannam* there stands in the same field as this verse.**

### فَأَرَادُوا۟ بِهِۦ كَيْدًا فَجَعَلْنَٰهُمُ ٱلْأَسْفَلِينَ — a plot and its outcome

***Kayd* — root ك-ي-د: to lay a scheme in secret in order to do harm.** The root was treated in detail at `086-tarik.md` 86/15-16 (*innahum yakīdūna kaydā · wa akīdu kaydā*). **I rely on that treatment.**

**And the word order of *arādū bihi kaydan* is worth noting:** *kayd* comes indefinite and is the object of the verb *arāda*. **"They wanted a plot"** — the intention is emphasised, that is, not the plot itself.

**As my own reading:** the verse does not say "they laid a plot" but **"they wanted to lay a plot"**. And immediately after it gives the outcome. **What happened to the fire, what happened to Abraham, is not recounted in between.** The Qurʾān recounts this elsewhere (al-Anbiyāʾ 21/69). **Here it is not recounted, and I do not add it.**

***Al-asfalīn* — root س-ف-ل: to be low.** *Sifla* — the lower side; *asfal* — the lowest. **In the Qurʾān: *thumma radadnāhu asfala sāfilīn* (al-Tīn 95/5) — treated in `095-tin.md`.**

**And the contrast must be recorded — this is a textual datum:**

| Verse | What they wanted to do | What they became |
|---|---|---|
| **97** | *ubnū lahu **bunyānan*** — to **raise** a structure | — |
| **98** | — | *jaʿalnāhumu'l-**asfalīn*** — **the lowest** |

**They wanted to raise; they fell low.** And both words are on the axis of height and lowness: *bunyān* (a structure, a thing raised) and *asfal* (the lowest). **This is a contrast read off the choice of words.**

**And a comparison with the other sections of the sequence:** in the Noah section the other side is named with the verb *aghraqnā* (We drowned), in the Lot section with *dammarnā* (We razed). **In the Abraham section there is not destruction but *being brought low*.** The people are not wiped out; **their position is changed.** This is the one exception in the sequence, and it is countable from the text.

---

## 37/99-101 — وَقَالَ إِنِّى ذَاهِبٌ إِلَىٰ رَبِّى سَيَهْدِينِ · رَبِّ هَبْ لِى مِنَ ٱلصَّٰلِحِينَ · فَبَشَّرْنَٰهُ بِغُلَٰمٍ حَلِيمٍ

*Wa qāla innī dhāhibun ilā rabbī sa-yahdīn · Rabbi hab lī mina'ṣ-ṣāliḥīn · Fa-bashsharnāhu bi-ghulāmin ḥalīm*

"And he said: 'I am going to my Lord; He will guide me. My Lord, grant me [a child] from among the righteous.' So We gave him the good news of a forbearing boy."

### إِنِّى ذَاهِبٌ إِلَىٰ رَبِّى — a sentence of migration

**A point of word order, and an important one:** *innī **dhāhibun*** — **a nominal sentence and an active participle.** *Sa-adhhabu* (I shall go) or *adhhabu* (I am going) could have been said.

**In Arabic the active participle announces the act in its settled state, become an attribute of the person.** Not "I shall go", then, but **"I am one who goes"** — the decision is made, the matter is settled.

***Dhāhibun ilā rabbī* — "I am going to my Lord".** **And a note is required on the expression:** the sentence announces a change of place, but the place to be reached is given not by the name of a place but **by an attribution.** Elsewhere the Qurʾān names this as a migration: *innī **muhājirun** ilā rabbī* (al-ʿAnkabūt 29/26).

**What is said, that is, is not the place gone to but the direction gone in.** This accords with the Qurʾān's choice not to give a place name, and no place name is given in this commentary either.

***Sa-yahdīn* — "He will guide me".** **And the *yāʾ* at the end of the verb has dropped:** *sa-yahdīn*, not *sa-yahdīnī*. **This is for the sake of the verse-ending and is a widespread usage in the Qurʾān.** In the section on Qāf the same fact was recorded for the word *waʿīd*.

**And the root ه-د-ي occurs for the second time in the sūra:**

| Verse | Expression | Who | Direction |
|---|---|---|---|
| **23** | *fa'**hdū**hum ilā ṣirāṭi'l-jaḥīm* | The wrongdoers | **To the blaze** |
| **99** | *sa-**yahdī**n* | **Abraham** | **To his Lord** |
| **118** | *wa **hadaynā**huma'ṣ-ṣirāṭa'l-mustaqīm* | **Moses and Aaron** | **To the straight path** |

**Three occurrences, the same root, two opposite directions. This is a fact countable from the text.**

**I record this as my own reading:** Abraham does not say "I know the way", he says **"He will guide me"**. When he makes the decision, that is, he does not know the point of arrival. **The ground of the decision is not knowledge but trust.**

### رَبِّ هَبْ لِى مِنَ ٱلصَّٰلِحِينَ — and the gap in the word order

**And this is one of the most striking points of word order in the sūra: an object is missing from the sentence.**

***Hab lī mina'ṣ-ṣāliḥīn* — "grant me … from among the righteous".** **What he is asking to be granted is not said.**

The grammarians count this as an *ellipsis* and complete the elided word as *waladan* (a child) or *ghulāman* (a boy). **The completion comes from the next verse:** *fa-bashsharnāhu bi-**ghulāmin** ḥalīm.*

**I record this as my own reading:** the prayer does not name the thing asked for; **it names the *quality* of the thing asked for.** Not "give me a son" but **"give me from among the righteous".** At the centre of the request, that is, is not a being but **an attribute**.

***Wahaba* — root و-ه-ب: to give without return, to bestow.** *Hiba* — a thing given without return. **At the centre of the word is the absence of return:** not a sale, not a reward, but **a gift.**

***Ṣāliḥ* — root ص-ل-ح: a thing's being free of defect, serviceable, sound.** Its opposite is *fasād* (corruption). The root was analysed in `002-bakara.md`.

**And the word will occur once more in the sūra:** *wa bashsharnāhu bi-Isḥāqa nabiyyan **mina'ṣ-ṣāliḥīn*** (112). **The attribute asked for in the prayer (to be of the righteous), that is, will be plainly given to Isaac in the hundred and twelfth verse.** This is a link that can be verified from the text.

### فَبَشَّرْنَٰهُ بِغُلَٰمٍ حَلِيمٍ — good news

***Bashshara* — root ب-ش-ر, Form II.** The root was treated at `051-zariyat.md` 51/28 (*wa bashsharūhu bi-ghulāmin ʿalīm*) and in `080-abese.md`.

**The note there: the original sense of the root is connected with *bashara* (skin)** — good news is the report that **changes the skin of a person's face.** **And the root can be used both for good and for bad news; the Qurʾān uses it for bad news too (*fa-bashshirhum bi-ʿadhābin alīm*).**

***Ghulām* — a male child approaching or newly come to maturity.** The word had occurred at `052-tur.md` 52/24 (*ghilmānun lahum*).

### And the difference between the two annunciations — this is a textual datum

**Two annunciations are given to Abraham in the Qurʾān, and al-Ṣāffāt contains both. Their attributes differ:**

| Verse | The good news | Attribute | Root |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Al-Ṣāffāt 37/101** | *bi-**ghulāmin ḥalīm*** | **Ḥalīm** — forbearing | ح-ل-م |
| **Al-Ṣāffāt 37/112** | *bi-**Isḥāqa nabiyyan mina'ṣ-ṣāliḥīn*** | **A prophet, righteous** — and **by name** | — |
| **Al-Dhāriyāt 51/28** | *bi-**ghulāmin ʿalīm*** | **ʿAlīm** — knowing | ع-ل-م |
| **Al-Ḥijr 15/53** | *bi-**ghulāmin ʿalīm*** | **ʿAlīm** | ع-ل-م |

**The good news of a *ghulāmin ʿalīm* (a knowing boy), then, occurs in two places in the Qurʾān, while the good news of a *ghulāmin ḥalīm* (a forbearing boy) occurs only here.**

**This difference is one of the grounds of the question of "which son", which I shall take up below, and it will be set in the table there.**

***Ḥalīm* — root ح-ل-م.** And the sense of the word is worth noting: **not to be hasty where one has reason to be angry, to have command of oneself, to be steady.** *Ḥilm* — to be patient and gentle; its opposite is *safah* (lightness, thoughtlessness).

**The Qurʾān uses the word of Abraham himself too:** *inna Ibrāhīma la-awwāhun **ḥalīm*** (al-Tawba 9/114) and *inna Ibrāhīma la-**ḥalīmun** awwāhun munīb* (Hūd 11/75). **The attribute used of the father, that is, is used of the son as well.** This is a fact that can be verified from the text.

**And I record this as my own reading:** the attribute *ḥalīm* is exactly the attribute required for the scene that will take place in the hundred and second verse. **Steadiness, not being hasty, not growing angry** — this is precisely the answer the child will give. **The attribute is given one verse before the scene.**

---

## 37/102 — فَلَمَّا بَلَغَ مَعَهُ ٱلسَّعْىَ قَالَ يَٰبُنَىَّ إِنِّىٓ أَرَىٰ فِى ٱلْمَنَامِ أَنِّىٓ أَذْبَحُكَ فَٱنظُرْ مَاذَا تَرَىٰ ۚ قَالَ يَٰٓأَبَتِ ٱفْعَلْ مَا تُؤْمَرُ ۖ سَتَجِدُنِىٓ إِن شَآءَ ٱللَّهُ مِنَ ٱلصَّٰبِرِينَ

*Fa-lammā balagha maʿahu's-saʿya qāla yā bunayya innī arā fi'l-manāmi annī adhbaḥuka fa'nẓur mādhā tarā. Qāla yā abati'fʿal mā tuʾmar, sa-tajidunī in shāʾa'llāhu mina'ṣ-ṣābirīn*

"When he reached the age of walking about with him, he said: 'My dear son, I see in my sleep that I am sacrificing you; look, then, what do you say?' He said: 'My dear father, do what you are commanded. God willing, you will find me among the steadfast.'"

### فَلَمَّا بَلَغَ مَعَهُ ٱلسَّعْىَ — the age of walking

**And this construction occurs only here in the Qurʾān. It announces the child's age not by a number but by a verb.**

***Balagha* — root ب-ل-غ: to reach a place, to arrive.** *Bulūgh* — reaching. **The root was treated at `040-gafir.md` 40/56 (*mā hum bi-bālighīh*) and 40/67 (*li-tablughū ashuddakum*).**

***Saʿy* — root س-ع-ي.** The root was treated at `079-naziat.md` 79/22 (*thumma adbara **yasʿā***), in `092-leyl.md` and at `053-necm.md` 53/39 (*wa an laysa li'l-insāni illā mā **saʿā***). **I rely on those treatments.**

**The definition there: the original picture of the root is *to walk fast*** — not to run, but faster than an ordinary walk. **And the word takes its sense of "to work, to strive" from this: to go quickly to and fro for a task.**

**And the structure of the construction is worth noting: *balagha maʿahu's-saʿya*** — "he reached [the age of] walking **with him**".

**A point of word order:** the expression *maʿahu* (with him) has come between *balagha* and *al-saʿy*. **What is reached, that is, is not "walking" but "walking with him".**

**What does this mean? The classical explanations:**

| Explanation | What the age of *saʿy* means |
|---|---|
| **The age of being able to walk with his father** | The child can now travel beside his father; he need not be carried separately |
| **The age of being able to work with his father** | The sense of *saʿy* as "work": the child is in a state to help his father in his labour |
| **Approaching maturity** | The threshold of leaving childhood |

**All three are transmitted and do not exclude one another. I give no number for the age — the verse gives none, and the numbers in the classical sources do not agree.**

**I record this as my own reading:** at the centre of the construction is **being together**. The child is now someone who **can walk** beside his father. **And at exactly this point what is asked of the father is to bring that togetherness to an end.** The time of the scene is the first moment at which the child can be a companion to his father. This is an explanation, not a ruling.

### يَٰبُنَىَّ — "my dear son"

***Bunayy* — the diminutive (*taṣghīr*) of the word *ibn* (son).** **And in Arabic the diminutive does not always announce smallness; most often it announces *tenderness and love*.**

The word, then, is closer to **"my dear son, my darling"** than to "my little son".

**And that the word is set here is worth noting — as my own reading:** the content of the sentence is the harshest thing there is, while the address is in the gentlest form. **This tension between what is said and the way of saying it is read off the word order.**

And the child's answer is in the same mould: *yā **abati*** — "my dear father". **Both sides address one another with the warmest form of address.** This is a countable linguistic fact.

### إِنِّىٓ أَرَىٰ فِى ٱلْمَنَامِ أَنِّىٓ أَذْبَحُكَ — the dream

***Arā* — imperfect: "I see".** **And that the verb is imperfect is worth recording:** not *raʾaytu* (I saw). **In Arabic the imperfect can announce continuance or repetition.**

**In the classical commentaries it is said on this basis that the dream was seen more than once.** **The verse does not say this openly; the imperfect is open to such a reading but does not require it. I issue no ruling.**

***Al-manām* — root ن-و-م: sleep.** A noun of place / a *maṣdar mīmī*: **the state of sleep, the place where one sleeps.** The root was treated at `051-zariyat.md` 51/17 (*mā **yahjaʿūn***) with a different word.

***Adhbaḥuka* — root ذ-ب-ح.** **The root was analysed at `002-bakara.md` 2/67-71.** The note there: **the lexicographers record that the original picture of the root is "to split", and that slaughtering is the splitting of the throat.**

**And the verb is imperfect: "I am sacrificing you".** The scene within the dream is recounted in the present.

**And a note is required:** the verse does not transmit an instruction in the **imperative**. **It does not say "I have been commanded to sacrifice you"; it says "I see in my sleep that I am sacrificing you".** The word "command" will occur in the child's answer: *ifʿal **mā tuʾmar***.

**This difference is read off the text and has been one of the grounds of the discussion of the status of a prophet's dream in the classical commentaries. I do not enter that discussion in this commentary;** what I record is a datum of word order: **the father transmits a dream, the son names it as a command.**

### فَٱنظُرْ مَاذَا تَرَىٰ — a point of word order

**And this is the place in the section that most needs to be dwelt on.**

**The father conveys the command and then *asks for an opinion*.**

***Fa'nẓur* — "look".** ***Mādhā tarā* — "what do you see / what do you say".**

**And the two verbs are from the same root (ر-أ-ي) but in two different pieces of work:**

| Expression | Who | Its sense |
|---|---|---|
| *innī **arā** fi'l-manām* | The father | **To see in a dream** |
| *mādhā **tarā*** | The son | **To state an opinion / a judgement** |

**The same verb, one in sleep and one awake.** This is a linguistic fact that can be verified from the text.

**A variant reading is transmitted:** *mādhā **tarā*** (active: what do you see) and *mādhā **turā*** (passive: what is shown to you / what is seen). **I give no reciter's name.** On the second reading the question becomes gentler still: "what appears to you?"

### The consultation itself — as a point of word order

**And what must be recorded here is this, and it is read entirely off the text:**

**The father does two things, and between the two there is a conjunction (*fāʾ*):**

1. **He announces the command / the dream** — *innī arā fi'l-manāmi annī adhbaḥuk.*
2. **He asks for an opinion** — *fa'nẓur mādhā tarā.*

**And these two appear to conflict:** if this is a command, what place has consultation? If he is consulting, is the command certain?

**The explanations transmitted in the classical commentaries. I give them in a table:**

| Explanation | What it says |
|---|---|
| **He consults not about the command but about submission** | Whether the command will be carried out is not asked; what is asked is **how the child will bear it**. The question is directed not at the command itself but at the child's state |
| **To prepare the child** | To give notice instead of acting suddenly; to make the child a partner in what is to happen |
| **To obtain the child's consent** | For the command to be carried out the child's submission was needed as well; verse 103 says *aslamā* (the two of them submitted) — **in the dual** |
| **The testing of his patience** | The trial is not the father's alone; verse 106 says *al-balāʾu'l-mubīn* and does not name whose it is |

**All these explanations are transmitted in the classical sources. I do not impose a preference.**

**But one datum of word order is certain and must be recorded:** **the verb of the hundred and third verse is dual — *fa-lammā aslamā* ("when the two of them submitted"). The text, that is, attributes the submission not to one person but to both. This shows from within the text that the consultation was not idle.**

**And as my own reading I record this:** the sentence *fa'nẓur mādhā tarā* adds a **partner** to the command. The command came to the father; but the person who is the subject of the command is also brought into the process. **And this changes the moral weight of the scene:** the child becomes not the *object* of the command but a *party* to it.

### يَٰٓأَبَتِ ٱفْعَلْ مَا تُؤْمَرُ — the answer

***Yā abati* — "my dear father".** The final *tāʾ* is a transformation of the possessive *yāʾ* (my): *yā abī* → *yā abati*. **In Arabic this is a form of address announcing tenderness and respect.**

***Ifʿal mā tuʾmar* — "do what you are commanded".**

**And the point of word order is sharp:** the child does not say *ifʿal **mā raʾayta*** ("do what you saw"); he says *ifʿal **mā tuʾmar*** ("do **what you are commanded**").

**The father spoke of a dream, that is, and the child named it a command.** This is a fact that can be verified from the text and was recorded above as well.

***Tuʾmar* — root أ-م-ر, passive: "you are commanded".** **The name of the one who commands is not said.** The passive hides the agent, but it is plain.

**I record this as my own reading:** the child does not name himself at all in the sentence. He does not say "sacrifice me"; he says **"do what you are commanded".** **He does not even name himself as the object of the command; he defines the matter as his father's obligation.** And then he adds his own share.

### سَتَجِدُنِىٓ إِن شَآءَ ٱللَّهُ مِنَ ٱلصَّٰبِرِينَ — and a qualification

***Sa-tajidunī* — "you will find me".** **The verb is *wajada* (to find) with the future particle *sīn*.**

**And a point of word order:** the child does not say *sa-**aṣbiru*** ("I shall be patient"). **He says "you will find me among the steadfast".** **He passes no judgement on himself, that is; he announces what his father will see.**

**I record this as my own reading:** the sentence transfers the claim **from the first person to the second.** "I am patient" would have been a boast; "you will find me patient" is **an undertaking**, and its verification is left to the other party.

***In shāʾa'llāh* — "God willing".** **And the place of this qualification is worth noting:** the child ties his word to a condition. **He does not rest his own patience on his own strength.**

**The Qurʾān commands this qualification separately:** *wa lā taqūlanna li-shayʾin innī fāʿilun dhālika ghadā · **illā an yashāʾa'llāh*** (al-Kahf 18/23-24).

***Al-ṣābirīn* — root ص-ب-ر.** The root was treated at `068-kalem.md` 68/48 and in `050-kaf.md`. **The note there: the concrete sense of the root is "to hold a thing in a place, to confine it"** — patience is not passive endurance but **an active holding back.**

**And the word order here too uses the mould of the group:** *mina'ṣ-ṣābirīn* — "**among** the steadfast". **This preference (placing the person in a group), seen in other parts of the sūra too, was recorded in the section on verses eighty and eighty-one.**

---

## 37/103-107 — فَلَمَّآ أَسْلَمَا وَتَلَّهُۥ لِلْجَبِينِ · وَنَٰدَيْنَٰهُ أَن يَٰٓإِبْرَٰهِيمُ · قَدْ صَدَّقْتَ ٱلرُّءْيَآ ۚ إِنَّا كَذَٰلِكَ نَجْزِى ٱلْمُحْسِنِينَ · إِنَّ هَٰذَا لَهُوَ ٱلْبَلَٰٓؤُا۟ ٱلْمُبِينُ · وَفَدَيْنَٰهُ بِذِبْحٍ عَظِيمٍ

*Fa-lammā aslamā wa tallahu li'l-jabīn · Wa nādaynāhu an yā Ibrāhīm · Qad ṣaddaqta'r-ruʾyā, innā kadhālika najzi'l-muḥsinīn · Inna hādhā la-huwa'l-balāʾu'l-mubīn · Wa fadaynāhu bi-dhibḥin ʿaẓīm*

"When the two of them had submitted and he had laid him down upon his temple — We called out to him: 'Abraham! You have fulfilled the dream. Thus do We requite those who do good. This was indeed the plain trial.' And We ransomed him with a great sacrifice."

### فَلَمَّآ أَسْلَمَا — a dual verb

**And that the verb is dual is the most important datum of word order in this section.**

***Aslamā*** — the **dual** of the verb *aslama* (he submitted): **"the two of them submitted".**

**The text attributes the submission, that is, not to one person but to both.** This is the counterpart of the consultation in the hundred and second verse: the father asked, the son answered, **and the two of them submitted together.**

**And the root س-ل-م does its fourth piece of work in the sūra here. It was given above in a table (in the section on verse 84).** What is to be added here: **the *mustaslimūn* of the twenty-sixth verse (those who surrender perforce) and the *aslamā* here (those who submit willingly) are two opposite states from the same root.** This is a contrast that can be verified from the text.

***Lammā* — "when".** In Arabic it announces **a temporal condition** and requires an apodosis. **And there is a dispute of word order over the apodosis here:**

| Solution | What the apodosis of *lammā* is |
|---|---|
| **Elided** | The apodosis is not stated; its supplied sense is something like "…when they did so, the thing happened / something great happened". **Most of the grammarians prefer this** |
| **Verse 104** | *Wa nādaynāhu* — the *wāw* is counted redundant and it is made the apodosis |

**Both solutions are transmitted.** **And the first is worth noting in point of sense — as my own reading:** that the apodosis is not stated shows that the scene is **cut off** at exactly that point. **What happened is not recounted.**

### وَتَلَّهُۥ لِلْجَبِينِ — laying him down upon his temple

**And this is one of the places where the Qurʾān says the most with the fewest words.**

***Talla* — root ت-ل-ل.** **And the word occurs only here in the Qurʾān.**

**The sense the lexicographers give: to throw someone to the ground, to lay him down, to tip him face down.** *Tall* — **a mound, a heap of earth.** **The link the lexicographers make:** *talla* is to lay someone out on the **earth** (the mound). I give this link as a transmission.

***Al-jabīn* — root ج-ب-ن.** **This root is analysed here for the first time in this commentary.**

**The sense the lexicographers give: *jabīn* is the *side* of the forehead — the region where the temple meets the brow.** A person has two *jabīn*s (right and left); the flat region in the middle is called *jabha* (the forehead).

**From the same root *jubn* — cowardice.** The lexicographers make the link thus: the colour and expression of the face of one who fears change. **I give this link as a transmission; I issue no firm ruling.**

**And the choice of the word carries the whole scene — I record it as my own reading:**

**The verse does not say "he laid him down"; it says "he laid him down upon his temple". That means the face is turned *to the side*.**

**And this has a single consequence: the meeting of eyes is broken off.** If you lay a person on his back his face turns toward you; if you turn him to the side, it does not. **The word announces that a mutual gaze has been broken.**

**I do not present this as a claim of intent;** what I record is that the word is **temple** (*jabīn*) and not **forehead** (*jabha*), and this is a choice that can be verified from the text.

### And the scene is cut off at exactly that point

**The hundred and third verse begins with a *lammā* clause and its apodosis does not come.**

**What the text does:**

| What is given | What is not given |
|---|---|
| The submission of the two | What they said |
| The act of laying down | What the knife did |
| The call (104) | How much time passed |
| The ransom (107) | How the scene ended |

**I record this as my own reading:** **the telling is cut off at the peak of the event and never recounts what came after.** The hundred and fourth verse passes straight to the voice. **The reader, that is, is left at the most strained moment of the scene, and that moment is never completed.**

**And this is a fact noticed in the classical period as well:** that the apodosis of *lammā* is elided is the solution most of the grammarians prefer, and its ground is exactly this — **what is not said is heavier than anything that could be said.**

**In this commentary that gap is not filled.** The details recounted in the classical sources about the knife, the manner of the laying down, the words spoken, are of *isrāʾīliyyāt* origin and **are not transmitted.**

### وَنَٰدَيْنَٰهُ أَن يَٰٓإِبْرَٰهِيمُ — the call

***Nādaynā* — root ن-د-و/ن-د-ي.** **Its link with the seventy-fifth verse was given above in a table:** Noah *called upon us*; to Abraham *We called out.*

***An* — here the explicative *an* (*an tafsīriyya*).** The grammarians render it "that is": it comes after a verb of speaking and opens the content of the utterance. **"We called out to him — that is: Abraham!"**

**And note: he is called by name.** **This is the only place in the sūra's sequence of prophets where a prophet's name is called out directly in address.** This is a fact countable from the text.

### قَدْ صَدَّقْتَ ٱلرُّءْيَا — you have fulfilled the dream

***Ṣaddaqa* — root ص-د-ق, Form II: to hold true, to confirm.**

**And the word occurs for the third time in the sūra:**

| Verse | Expression | Who | What he holds true |
|---|---|---|---|
| **37** | *wa **ṣaddaqa**'l-mursalīn* | The Prophet | **The messengers** |
| **52** | *la-mina'l-**muṣaddiqīn*** | (The *qarīn*'s mockery) | **The resurrection** |
| **105** | *qad **ṣaddaqta**'r-ruʾyā* | **Abraham** | **The dream** |

**Three occurrences, the same root. This is a textual datum.**

**And the sense of the verb is worth noting here — as my own reading:** *taṣdīq* is not merely to **count** a word true; it is to **make it come true**. In Arabic *ṣaddaqa'l-waʿda* means "he fulfilled his promise". **"You have confirmed the dream", then, means "you have made real what the dream said".**

***Al-ruʾyā* — root ر-أ-ي: what is seen in sleep.** And the expression *arā fi'l-manām* of the hundred and second verse is here reduced to a single word: *al-ruʾyā.*

**And there is a note in the word order:** the verse does not say "you have carried out the command"; **it says "you have fulfilled the dream".** **The name of the thing in question remains *dream*.** This is consistent with the distinction recorded in the hundred and second verse: the father spoke of a dream, the son said command, **the text says dream.**

### إِنَّا كَذَٰلِكَ نَجْزِى ٱلْمُحْسِنِينَ — the early arrival of the mould

**And this is the most striking structural fact in the sequence.**

The mould normally comes at **the end** of the account (80, 110, 121, 131). **Here it comes in the middle of the account** — and the account has not yet ended (the ransom will be given in verse 107, Isaac announced in 112).

| Prophet | Where the mould is |
|---|---|
| Noah | **80** — at the end of the account |
| **Abraham** | **105 (in the middle)** and **110 (at the end)** — **twice** |
| Moses-Aaron | **121** — at the end |
| Elijah | **131** — at the end |

**This is a fact that can be verified from the text and is the one exception in the sequence.**

**I record this as my own reading, and it is not binding:** the placing of the mould here shows that the reward is given **before the ransom.** The requital, that is, is not tied to the sacrifice's taking place; it is tied to **the submission itself.** And the ransom in the hundred and seventh verse becomes not the reward itself but an addition that comes after it.

### إِنَّ هَٰذَا لَهُوَ ٱلْبَلَٰٓؤُا۟ ٱلْمُبِينُ — the plain trial

**The word order again has three reinforcements:** *inna* + *lām* + *huwa* (*ḍamīr al-faṣl*). **Exactly the same structure as the sixtieth verse:** *inna hādhā **la-huwa'l**-fawzu'l-ʿaẓīm.*

| Verse | Sentence | Subject |
|---|---|---|
| **60** | *inna hādhā la-huwa'l-**fawzu'l-ʿaẓīm*** | **The great triumph** |
| **106** | *inna hādhā la-huwa'l-**balāʾu'l-mubīn*** | **The plain trial** |

**The same mould, two different rulings. This is a repetition countable from the text.**

***Balāʾ* — root ب-ل-و: to try, to test.** **And the root has two directions in Arabic, and both are used in the Qurʾān:** *balāʾ* is testing both **with favour** and **with hardship**. The Qurʾān: *wa nablūkum bi'sh-sharri wa'l-khayri **fitna*** (al-Anbiyāʾ 21/35).

**And the lexicographers record a second branch of sense:** *baliya* — **to wear out, to be worn.** The link is made thus: a testing wears down the one tested. **I give this link as a transmission.**

***Al-mubīn* — root ب-ي-ن: plain, distinguishing.** The word occurs five times in the sūra: 15 (*siḥrun mubīn*), 106 (here), 113 (*ẓālimun li-nafsihi mubīn*), 117 (*al-kitāba'l-mustabīn* — from the same root), 156 (*sulṭānun mubīn*).

**And whom this verse concerns is disputed:**

| View | What *hādhā* points to |
|---|---|
| **The trial itself** | "This [event] was a plain trial" |
| **The weight of the command** | "This [command] was a plain testing" |

**Both arrive at the same place.** Moreover **whose trial it was** is not specified either — the father's, the son's, or both. **The text does not specify and neither do I;** but the dual verb in the hundred and third verse (*aslamā*) points to both.

### وَفَدَيْنَٰهُ بِذِبْحٍ عَظِيمٍ — the ransom

***Fadaynā* — root ف-د-ي: to save someone by giving a substitute in his place.** *Fidya* — a price of redemption; *fidāʾ* — a substitute. **The root was treated at `047-muhammed.md` 47/4 (*fa-immā mannan baʿdu wa immā fidāʾan*) and at `070-mearic.md` 70/11-14 (*yawaddu'l-mujrimu law yaftadī min ʿadhābi yawmaʾidhin bi-banīh*).**

**And the comparison with the verse in al-Maʿārij is worth noting — this is a textual datum:**

| Place | Who, whom, with what |
|---|---|
| **Al-Maʿārij 70/11-14** | The guilty man wishes to give **his sons** as a ransom to save **himself** |
| **Al-Ṣāffāt 37/107** | **God** gives a sacrificial ransom to save the son |

**The two verses use the same root and the directions are exactly opposite.** In the one a man wants to make his child the price; in the other a price is given for the child himself. **Whether the text sets up this link cannot be said; the sharing of the root is a datum.**

***Dhibḥ* — root ذ-ب-ح.** **And the word is read *dhibḥ*, not *dhabḥ*, and this difference is meaningful:**

| Form | Pattern | Its sense |
|---|---|---|
| **ذَبْح** (*dhabḥ*) | Verbal noun | **The act of slaughtering** |
| **ذِبْح** (*dhibḥ*) | A noun (the *fiʿl* pattern) | **The thing to be slaughtered — a sacrificial animal** |

**In Arabic the *fiʿl* pattern often announces "the thing that is the object of the act"** (*raqm* — writing / *riqm*; *ḥiml* — a load). **The verse, then, speaks not of an act but of an animal.**

**This is a linguistic datum and must be recorded:** the verse does not say what it was (which animal), but by the pattern of the word it announces **that a living creature is meant.**

***ʿAẓīm* — root ع-ظ-م: great, mighty.** **And what "great" means is disputed:**

| View | In what respect *ʿaẓīm* |
|---|---|
| **In respect of bulk** | A large animal |
| **In respect of worth** | Its value is great — because a human being is saved in return for it |
| **In respect of its source** | That it was given directly by God |
| **In respect of its outcome** | That a practice remained lasting after it |

**All of these are transmitted in the classical commentaries. I do not impose a preference.**

**As my own reading I record only this:** the sūra uses the attribute *ʿaẓīm* in two other places — *al-karbi'l-**ʿaẓīm*** (76, 115) and *al-fawzu'l-**ʿaẓīm*** (60). **All three uses announce a *scale*:** a great distress, a great triumph, a great sacrifice. **This is a count of a word.**

---

## Which son is the sacrifice? — the disagreement

**This is one of the oldest and most disputed questions of Qurʾānic exegesis. And what the text says must be written first.**

### What the verses say

**Through al-Ṣāffāt 37/99-113 the child's name never occurs. This is certain and is a countable fact.**

| Verse | How the child is named |
|---|---|
| 100 | *mina'ṣ-ṣāliḥīn* — from among the righteous (no name) |
| 101 | *ghulāmin ḥalīm* — a forbearing boy |
| 102 | *yā bunayya* — my dear son |
| 103 | *tallahu* — a pronoun |
| 107 | *fadaynāhu* — a pronoun |
| **112** | **Isaac — plainly by name, and *as a piece of good news*** |

**In the sūra, then, only one name occurs, and that is given in the hundred and twelfth verse, after the scene of the sacrifice has ended.**

### The views and their grounds

**I give them in a table. No preference is imposed in this commentary.**

| | **Ishmael (Ismāʿīl)** | **Isaac (Isḥāq)** |
|---|---|---|
| **Ground of word order** | Verse 112 names Isaac **after the scene of the sacrifice and as a new piece of good news** (*wa bashsharnāhu bi-Isḥāq*). If the one sacrificed had been Isaac, the same child would have had to be announced twice | The good news in verse 101 and that in verse 112 may be **the same child**; 112 may be counted the explanation of 101. In that case the whole section is about Isaac |
| **Ground of attribute** | In 101 *ghulāmin **ḥalīm***, in 112 *nabiyyan mina'ṣ-ṣāliḥīn* — **two different attributes, two different children** | That the attributes differ does not require different persons |
| **Intra-Qurʾānic indication** | At al-Dhāriyāt 51/28 and al-Ḥijr 15/53 the good news given to Abraham is of *ghulāmin **ʿalīm***, and **Isaac** is named there (plainly at Hūd 11/71: *fa-bashsharnāhā bi-Isḥāqa wa min warāʾi Isḥāqa Yaʿqūb*). In al-Ṣāffāt the attribute is *ḥalīm* — different | At Hūd 11/71 the good news is given to Abraham's **wife**, and after Isaac, Jacob (Yaʿqūb) is announced as well. It has been objected that a command to sacrifice a child whose grandson has been announced would produce a contradiction — **but this objection is a difficulty for the Isaac view, not for the Ishmael view** |
| **Ground of transmission** | A number of the early exegetes hold this view; in the later period the majority of Muslim scholars inclined to it | A significant number of the early exegetes hold this view; and it appears in reports attributed to the Companions and the Followers |
| **Its weak side** | The Qurʾān does not separately recount the birth of Ishmael as a piece of good news; that the good news in al-Ṣāffāt belongs to Ishmael is not established by a verse | The order of verse 112 is strained: that the same child should be announced again after the scene of the sacrifice requires explanation |

### A further note: the question of "the information that reached us"

**In the classical commentaries a great many reports belonging to the parties of this disagreement are transmitted. Some of these reports are of People of the Book origin, and the exegetes record this openly.**

**In this commentary:**

- **I transmit no report with a name and a chain**, because I am not in a position to assess them.
- **I do not enter into *isrāʾīliyyāt* details** — details such as a place name, an age, a date, the kind of the animal are not transmitted.
- **I impose no preference.**

### And the verse's own bearing

**I record this plainly, and it is not an interpretation but an observation of the text:**

**The verse does not give a name. And the verse could have given a name** — indeed it names Isaac in the hundred and twelfth verse, Elijah in the hundred and twenty-third, Lot in the hundred and thirty-third, Jonah in the hundred and thirty-ninth, each by name. **This is a sūra that knows how to give names.** That it does not give one in the scene of the sacrifice is a choice that can be read off the word order.

**As my own reading — and it is not binding:** at the centre of the section there is not a person but a **situation**. There are two parties in the scene and both are named by an attribute: the son who is *ḥalīm* and the father who fulfils the dream. **When no name is given the reader enters the scene; when a name is given the scene closes into a history.** This is an explanation; the verse gives no ground.

**This disagreement will also be recorded in the table of "disagreements where no preference is adopted" in the section `The sūra as a whole`.**

---

## 37/108-113 — وَتَرَكْنَا عَلَيْهِ فِى ٱلْءَاخِرِينَ · سَلَٰمٌ عَلَىٰٓ إِبْرَٰهِيمَ · كَذَٰلِكَ نَجْزِى ٱلْمُحْسِنِينَ · إِنَّهُۥ مِنْ عِبَادِنَا ٱلْمُؤْمِنِينَ · وَبَشَّرْنَٰهُ بِإِسْحَٰقَ نَبِيًّا مِّنَ ٱلصَّٰلِحِينَ · وَبَٰرَكْنَا عَلَيْهِ وَعَلَىٰٓ إِسْحَٰقَ ۚ وَمِن ذُرِّيَّتِهِمَا مُحْسِنٌ وَظَالِمٌ لِّنَفْسِهِۦ مُبِينٌ

*Wa taraknā ʿalayhi fi'l-ākhirīn · Salāmun ʿalā Ibrāhīm · Kadhālika najzi'l-muḥsinīn · Innahu min ʿibādina'l-muʾminīn · Wa bashsharnāhu bi-Isḥāqa nabiyyan mina'ṣ-ṣāliḥīn · Wa bāraknā ʿalayhi wa ʿalā Isḥāq, wa min dhurriyyatihimā muḥsinun wa ẓālimun li-nafsihi mubīn*

"And We left upon him, among those who came after: 'Peace be upon Abraham.' Thus do We requite those who do good. He was one of Our believing servants. And We gave him the good news of Isaac, a prophet from among the righteous. And We blessed him and Isaac. And of the progeny of them both there is the one who does good and the one who plainly wrongs himself."

### كَذَٰلِكَ نَجْزِى ٱلْمُحْسِنِينَ — the missing إِنَّا

**And this is the one omission in the table of the sūra's closing moulds.**

| Verse | Mould | Is *innā* present |
|---|---|---|
| **80** (Noah) | *innā kadhālika najzi'l-muḥsinīn* | **Yes** |
| **105** (Abraham, in the middle) | *innā kadhālika najzi'l-muḥsinīn* | **Yes** |
| **110** (Abraham, at the end) | ***kadhālika** najzi'l-muḥsinīn* | **NO** |
| **121** (Moses-Aaron) | *innā kadhālika najzi'l-muḥsinīn* | **Yes** |
| **131** (Elijah) | *innā kadhālika najzi'l-muḥsinīn* | **Yes** |

**This is a fact that can be verified from the text: in four of the five occurrences *innā* is present, in one it is not.**

**And the reason is not said in the verse. The explanations transmitted in the classical commentaries:**

| Explanation | What it says |
|---|---|
| **Avoidance of repetition** | The same mould had occurred in its full form five verses earlier (105); at the second occurrence it is shortened |
| **Verse-ending / rhythm** | The verse comes shorter and changes the rhythm of the sequence |
| **The place of the emphasis changes** | With the *innā* reinforcement removed the sentence turns from an announcement into a rule |

**All three are explanations that can be transmitted. I issue no firm ruling.**

**But the first explanation is supported by a textual datum, and I record it:** **the only section in which the mould occurs twice is the Abraham section, and the shortening is there too.** The repetition and the shortening are in the same section. **This is a fact; it is not a claim of cause and effect.**

### وَبَشَّرْنَٰهُ بِإِسْحَٰقَ نَبِيًّا مِّنَ ٱلصَّٰلِحِينَ — the second annunciation

**And the hundred and twelfth verse comes after the section of the sacrifice has closed.**

**A point of word order must be recorded:** the verse says *wa bashsharnāhu **bi-Isḥāq*** — **plainly by name.** In the hundred and first verse it had said *bi-**ghulāmin ḥalīm*** — **without a name and with an attribute.**

| Verse | The good news | The naming |
|---|---|---|
| **101** | *bi-ghulāmin ḥalīm* | **No name**, an attribute |
| **112** | *bi-Isḥāqa nabiyyan mina'ṣ-ṣāliḥīn* | **A name**, and two attributes |

**This is the most important ground of word order in the question of the sacrifice, and it was given in the table above. I do not repeat it.**

***Nabiyyan* — root ن-ب-أ (news) or ن-ب-و (to rise).** **Two derivations are transmitted:**

| Root | Its sense |
|---|---|
| **ن-ب-أ** | *Nabaʾ* — weighty news. **A *nabī*: one who brings news / of whom news is given** |
| **ن-ب-و** | *Nabwa* — height. **A *nabī*: one whose station has been raised** |

**The root ن-ب-أ was analysed in `078-nebe.md`.** Both derivations are transmitted by the lexicographers; **I issue no firm ruling.**

***Mina'ṣ-ṣāliḥīn* — and the answer to the prayer.** **In the hundredth verse Abraham had said *rabbi hab lī mina'ṣ-ṣāliḥīn*. In the hundred and twelfth the same construction comes as the answer. This is a link that can be verified from the text and is one of the plainest instances of prayer and response in the sūra.**

| Verse | Expression | Who |
|---|---|---|
| **100** | *hab lī **mina'ṣ-ṣāliḥīn*** | **Abraham — the request** |
| **112** | *nabiyyan **mina'ṣ-ṣāliḥīn*** | **The text — the response** |

### وَبَٰرَكْنَا عَلَيْهِ وَعَلَىٰٓ إِسْحَٰقَ — blessing

***Bāraka* — root ب-ر-ك.** **And the concrete picture of the root is worth noting:** *birka* — a pool, a gathering of water; *baraka'l-baʿīr* — the camel put its chest to the ground and knelt. **The common denominator: to stay in a place, to accumulate.**

*Baraka*, then, is not abundance but **lastingness and continuance**: a thing's staying in its place and increasing.

**And the preposition *ʿalā*:** *bāraknā **ʿalayhi*** — "We sent down blessing **upon** him". Not *lahu* (to him). ***ʿAlā*** announces a thing that comes from above and settles upon.

**The two names are named separately:** *ʿalayhi **wa ʿalā** Isḥāq*. **The preposition is repeated** — in Arabic this announces that the two parties are meant separately. **The blessing, that is, is not shared but comes in two separate portions.**

### وَمِن ذُرِّيَّتِهِمَا مُحْسِنٌ وَظَالِمٌ لِّنَفْسِهِۦ مُبِينٌ — and the most careful sentence in the sūra

**And this sentence sets down one of the most important notes of the sūra.**

***Dhurriyyatihimā* — "the progeny of them both", a dual pronoun.**

**And the sentence says this: from the same line two kinds of human being come.**

| Side | Word | Root |
|---|---|---|
| The one | **مُحْسِنٌ** — one who does good | ح-س-ن |
| The other | **ظَالِمٌ لِّنَفْسِهِۦ مُبِينٌ** — one who plainly wrongs himself | ظ-ل-م |

***Muḥsin* is the word of the sūra's closing mould** (*najzi'l-**muḥsinīn***). **The attribute occurring in the mould of reward, that is, is here given to a part of the line — not to all of it.**

**And this is the direct textual counterpart of a principle recorded in `STYLE.md`:**

> *"A judgement is not built wholesale upon an ethnic or religious group; it is the attributes the verse describes, and whoever bears that attribute is included in it."*

**The verse does exactly this.** Descent from a prophet gives no attribute; **the attribute belongs to individual persons.**

**I record this as my own reading:** the placing of the verse here is meaningful in point of word order. **A moment ago two names were blessed; immediately after, it is said that blessing does not pass automatically to the line.** With the two sentences side by side, the second draws the limit of the first.

***Ẓālim li-nafsihi* — "one who wrongs himself".** **And note: the wrong is not to another but to himself.**

***Ẓalama* — root ظ-ل-م: to put a thing somewhere other than its place.** The root was analysed in `002-bakara.md`. **The note there: the original sense of the root is not "injustice" but *displacement*.**

**And the qualification *li-nafsihi* accords exactly with this root:** one who wrongs himself is one who has put himself out of the place he ought to be in.

***Mubīn* — "plainly".** **The attribute belongs to *ẓālim* and comes last.** The wrong is not hidden, then; **it is visible.**

**And the balance within the sūra must be recorded — this is a textual datum:**

| Verse | *Muḥsin* | *Ẓālim* |
|---|---|---|
| **80, 105, 110, 121, 131** | In the closing mould | — |
| **22** | — | *uḥshuru'lladhīna **ẓalamū*** |
| **63** | — | *fitnatan li'ẓ-**ẓālimīn*** |
| **113** | **Both in the same sentence** | **Both in the same sentence** |

**After using the two attributes separately, the sūra joins them in a single sentence in the last verse of the Abraham section.** This is a countable fact.

---

## 37/114-122 — وَلَقَدْ مَنَنَّا عَلَىٰ مُوسَىٰ وَهَٰرُونَ · وَنَجَّيْنَٰهُمَا وَقَوْمَهُمَا مِنَ ٱلْكَرْبِ ٱلْعَظِيمِ · وَنَصَرْنَٰهُمْ فَكَانُوا۟ هُمُ ٱلْغَٰلِبِينَ · وَءَاتَيْنَٰهُمَا ٱلْكِتَٰبَ ٱلْمُسْتَبِينَ · وَهَدَيْنَٰهُمَا ٱلصِّرَٰطَ ٱلْمُسْتَقِيمَ · وَتَرَكْنَا عَلَيْهِمَا فِى ٱلْءَاخِرِينَ · سَلَٰمٌ عَلَىٰ مُوسَىٰ وَهَٰرُونَ · إِنَّا كَذَٰلِكَ نَجْزِى ٱلْمُحْسِنِينَ · إِنَّهُمَا مِنْ عِبَادِنَا ٱلْمُؤْمِنِينَ
