*Wa laqad manannā ʿalā Mūsā wa Hārūn · Wa najjaynāhumā wa qawmahumā mina'l-karbi'l-ʿaẓīm · Wa naṣarnāhum fa-kānū humu'l-ghālibīn · Wa ātaynāhuma'l-kitāba'l-mustabīn · Wa hadaynāhuma'ṣ-ṣirāṭa'l-mustaqīm · Wa taraknā ʿalayhimā fi'l-ākhirīn · Salāmun ʿalā Mūsā wa Hārūn · Innā kadhālika najzi'l-muḥsinīn · Innahumā min ʿibādina'l-muʾminīn*

"And We bestowed favour upon Moses and Aaron. We saved them both and their people from the great distress. We helped them, and they were the ones who prevailed. We gave them both the Book that makes all clear and guided them both to the straight path. And We left upon them both, among those who came after: 'Peace be upon Moses and Aaron.' Thus do We requite those who do good. They were both of Our believing servants."

### The one pair in the sequence

**And this section is the one paired section of the sequence: all the verbs and pronouns are dual.**

| Verse | The dual element |
|---|---|
| 114 | *ʿalā Mūsā wa Hārūn* |
| 115 | *najjaynā**humā** wa qawma**humā*** |
| 117 | *ātaynā**humā*** |
| 118 | *hadaynā**humā*** |
| 119 | *ʿalayhi**mā*** |
| 120 | *ʿalā Mūsā wa Hārūn* |
| 122 | *inna**humā*** |

And the one exception is the hundred and sixteenth verse: *wa naṣarnā**hum*** — **plural.** Because there the people are included as well.

**This is a fact of word order that can be verified from the text and is worth noting:** in a section of nine verses, eight duals and one plural. **And the plural comes precisely in the verse of help (*naṣr*)** — the help, that is, is not to the two but to the community.

**I record this as my own reading:** the Qurʾān constantly names Aaron beside Moses, and al-Ṣāffāt is one of the sūras that does this most consistently. **In the section not one act done by Moses alone is recounted.**

### وَلَقَدْ مَنَنَّا — favour

***Manna* — root م-ن-ن.** **The root has two branches, and both are used in the Qurʾān:**

| Branch | Its sense |
|---|---|
| **To grant a favour** | *Manna ʿalayhi* — he bestowed a favour on him |
| **To hold a gift over someone** | *Mann* — to throw what one gave in another's face (al-Baqara 2/264) |

**And the original picture the lexicographers record: *mann* is to cut a thing.** *Mannahu* — he cut it. **The link is made thus: a favour is a portion cut off from something and given; holding a gift over another cuts the bond of the favour. I give this link as a transmission.**

**The Qurʾān uses the same verb of other prophets too.** And in al-Ṣāffāt it occurs only here.

**And when the opening moulds of the sequence are looked at, this section is seen to have the same structure as the Noah section:**

| Prophet | Opening | Structure |
|---|---|---|
| **Noah (75)** | *wa laqad **nādānā** Nūḥun* | *wa laqad* + verb |
| **Moses-Aaron (114)** | *wa laqad **manannā** ʿalā Mūsā wa Hārūn* | *wa laqad* + verb |

**And the direction of the verbs is opposite: in the Noah section the subject is the prophet (he called), in the Moses section the subject is God (We bestowed).** This is the one change of direction in the sequence, and it is countable.

### مِنَ ٱلْكَرْبِ ٱلْعَظِيمِ — the second occurrence

**The construction was analysed in the seventy-sixth verse (Noah) and a table was given there. I do not repeat it.**

**The only thing to be added here: those saved here are saved together with their people.** In the Noah section those saved were *wa ahlahu* (his household). **This is a countable difference:**

| Prophet | Who is saved |
|---|---|
| **Noah (76)** | *wa **ahlahu*** — his household |
| **Moses-Aaron (115)** | *wa **qawmahumā*** — their people |
| **Lot (134)** | *wa **ahlahu** ajmaʿīn* — his whole household |

**Three sections, three different extents. And the Moses section is the only one in which the whole of the people is saved.**

### وَنَصَرْنَٰهُمْ فَكَانُوا۟ هُمُ ٱلْغَٰلِبِينَ — the ones who prevailed

***Naṣara* — root ن-ص-ر: to help.** The root was treated at `054-kamer.md` 54/10, 44 and in `061-saff.md`.

**And a point the lexicographers record:** one of the concrete senses of the root is **rain's coming to the aid** — *naṣara'l-ghaythu'l-arḍa* — the rain came to the aid of the earth. **Help is like water coming to parched soil.**

***Al-ghālibīn* — root غ-ل-ب: to prevail, to overcome.**

**A point of word order:** *fa-kānū **hum** al-ghālibīn* — the pronoun *hum* between, **the *ḍamīr al-faṣl*.** The same structure was present in the seventy-seventh verse: *jaʿalnā dhurriyyatahu **hum** al-bāqīn.* **The sūra uses this mould twice, and in both it sets up a *ḥaṣr* (restriction):** "the ones who prevailed were **they**", "the ones who remained were **they**".

**And the word will return at the end of the sūra:** *wa inna jundanā la-humu'l-**ghālibūn*** (173). **The same mould: *la-humu'l-ghālibūn*.** This is a repetition that can be verified from the text and binds the sūra's sequence of prophets to its closing block.

| Verse | Expression | Who |
|---|---|---|
| **116** | *fa-kānū **humu'l-ghālibīn*** | Moses, Aaron and their people |
| **173** | *wa inna jundanā **la-humu'l-ghālibūn*** | **God's host** |

**What was realised in the past, that is, is said with the same word as the promise given for the future.**

### ٱلْكِتَٰبَ ٱلْمُسْتَبِينَ — the Book that makes all clear

***Mustabīn* — root ب-ي-ن, the active participle of Form X.** **And the pattern is worth noting.**

| Pattern | Example | Its sense |
|---|---|---|
| **Form IV** — *mubīn* | 15, 106, 113, 156 | **What is clear / what makes clear** |
| **Form X** — *mustabīn* | **117** | **What seeks clarity / whose clarity is marked** |

**In Arabic Form X can announce "to find a quality in oneself / to go far in that quality".** *Mustabīn*, then — **whose clarity has thoroughly come out.**

**And the word occurs only here in the Qurʾān.** *Mubīn*, by contrast, is used very often in the Qurʾān. **In this one place the sūra chooses the stronger pattern.** This is a datum of word choice.

### وَهَدَيْنَٰهُمَا ٱلصِّرَٰطَ ٱلْمُسْتَقِيمَ — and the link with al-Fātiḥa

**The construction *al-ṣirāṭu'l-mustaqīm* was analysed in detail at `001-fatiha.md` 1/6. I do not repeat it.**

**And the contrast within the sūra is completed here — this is a textual datum:**

| Verse | Verb | *Ṣirāṭ* | Direction |
|---|---|---|---|
| **23** | *fa'hdūhum ilā* | *ṣirāṭi'l-**jaḥīm*** | **The road of the blaze** |
| **118** | *wa hadaynāhumā* | *al-ṣirāṭa'l-**mustaqīm*** | **The straight path** |

**The same verb (ه-د-ي), the same word (*ṣirāṭ*), opposite attributes.** And ninety-five verses lie between the two. **The sūra uses the word at two extremes.**

**A point of word order:** *hadaynāhuma'ṣ-ṣirāṭa* — the verb takes **two objects**, and the second without a preposition. In the twenty-third verse it was transitive with *ilā* (*fa'hdūhum **ilā** ṣirāṭi'l-jaḥīm*).

**The difference the grammarians record:** the verb *hadā*, when directly transitive, announces **bringing to the way**; when transitive with *ilā*, **directing toward the way**. **Arrival in the one, then, and conveyance in the other.**

**I record this as my own reading:** this distinction deepens the difference between the two verses. **They are *conveyed* to the road of the blaze; they are *brought* to the straight path.** This is a linguistic observation; the verse gives no ground.

### سَلَٰمٌ عَلَىٰ مُوسَىٰ وَهَٰرُونَ — a double peace

**And this is the one double salutation in the sequence. It ties to the table in the section on the structure.**

**And a note: both names are given in full in the salutation** — *ʿalā Mūsā wa Hārūn.* **They are not shortened to a pronoun** (*ʿalayhimā* could have been said). **In the hundred and nineteenth verse a pronoun was used** (*taraknā **ʿalayhimā***); **in the hundred and twentieth the names are repeated.**

**I record this as my own reading:** in all four of the sentences of peace the name occurs plainly (*Nūḥin*, *Ibrāhīm*, *Mūsā wa Hārūn*, *Il Yāsīn*). **The peace is not left to a pronoun.** This suggests that the sentence is framed **to be said** — in accord with the explanation recorded in the section on the seventy-eighth verse, that "what was left is that sentence itself".

---

## 37/123-132 — وَإِنَّ إِلْيَاسَ لَمِنَ ٱلْمُرْسَلِينَ · إِذْ قَالَ لِقَوْمِهِۦٓ أَلَا تَتَّقُونَ · أَتَدْعُونَ بَعْلًا وَتَذَرُونَ أَحْسَنَ ٱلْخَٰلِقِينَ · ٱللَّهَ رَبَّكُمْ وَرَبَّ ءَابَآئِكُمُ ٱلْأَوَّلِينَ · فَكَذَّبُوهُ فَإِنَّهُمْ لَمُحْضَرُونَ · إِلَّا عِبَادَ ٱللَّهِ ٱلْمُخْلَصِينَ · وَتَرَكْنَا عَلَيْهِ فِى ٱلْءَاخِرِينَ · سَلَٰمٌ عَلَىٰٓ إِلْ يَاسِينَ · إِنَّا كَذَٰلِكَ نَجْزِى ٱلْمُحْسِنِينَ · إِنَّهُۥ مِنْ عِبَادِنَا ٱلْمُؤْمِنِينَ

*Wa inna Ilyāsa la-mina'l-mursalīn · Idh qāla li-qawmihi alā tattaqūn · A-tadʿūna baʿlan wa tadharūna aḥsana'l-khāliqīn · Allāha rabbakum wa rabba ābāʾikumu'l-awwalīn · Fa-kadhdhabūhu fa-innahum la-muḥḍarūn · Illā ʿibāda'llāhi'l-mukhlaṣīn · Wa taraknā ʿalayhi fi'l-ākhirīn · Salāmun ʿalā Il Yāsīn · Innā kadhālika najzi'l-muḥsinīn · Innahu min ʿibādina'l-muʾminīn*

"And Elijah too was one of the messengers sent. When he said to his people: 'Will you not guard yourselves? Do you call upon Baʿl and leave the best of creators — God, your Lord and the Lord of your forefathers of old?' They denied him; and so they will surely be brought — except God's chosen servants. And We left upon him, among those who came after: 'Peace be upon Il Yāsīn.' Thus do We requite those who do good. He was one of Our believing servants."

### Elijah — his place in the Qurʾān

**Elijah is named in three places in the Qurʾān:** al-Anʿām 6/85 (in a list of prophets), al-Ṣāffāt 37/123 and 37/130. **Outside this sūra no information is given about him.**

**And in this commentary no information is given about who he was, when and where he lived** — the Qurʾān gives none, and the *isrāʾīliyyāt* sources are not entered.

**What is to be recorded is what the sūra gives: he was a messenger, he forbade his people to serve *Baʿl*, and he was denied.**

### أَلَا تَتَّقُونَ — will you not guard yourselves?

***Ittaqā* — root و-ق-ي, Form VIII: to protect oneself, to guard.** The original sense of the root is **"to protect a thing from what would harm it".** *Wiqāya* — a protecting cover. The root was analysed in `002-bakara.md` (*hudan li'l-muttaqīn*).

**And this is a sentence often repeated in the Qurʾān as the first word of the messengers.** In Sūrat al-Shuʿarāʾ it occurs in the same form from the mouths of more than one prophet.

**A point of word order:** *alā* — in Arabic a particle announcing both **urging** and **warning**. *Alā* + imperfect: "will you not…?"

### أَتَدْعُونَ بَعْلًا — what is *Baʿl*?

**And there is disagreement over this word.**

***Baʿl* — root ب-ع-ل.** **The known sense of the word in Arabic: *husband, owner, master*.** The Qurʾān uses it in this sense: *wa hādhā **baʿlī** shaykhā* (Hūd 11/72) — "and this husband of mine is old"; *wa **buʿūlatuhunna** aḥaqqu bi-raddihinna* (al-Baqara 2/228).

**A second branch of the root is also recorded:** *baʿl* is used too of crops and palm groves that grow by rain alone, without irrigation.

**The views on its sense here:**

| View | What *Baʿl* is | Ground | Its weak side |
|---|---|---|---|
| **The name of an idol** | A particular idol the people served | The structure of the sentence: *tadʿūna baʿlan* — "you call upon Baʿl". *Duʿāʾ* here is in the sense of worship and its object is something served | The verse gives no description of the idol |
| **In the sense of "lord / master"** | "Do you call upon a master?" — that is, upon an owner other than God | The original sense of the word in Arabic | *Baʿlan* comes indefinite; reading it as a common noun is possible but strained |
| **A name of foreign origin** | It is transmitted that the word is the name of a deity that passed into Arabic from another language | This note is present in the classical sources | No firm linguistic evidence is offered |

**Most of the classical commentaries adopt the first view. I impose no preference and give no information about which community the word belonged to** — the Qurʾān gives none.

***Tadʿūna* — root د-ع-و: to call, to entreat.** The root was treated at `040-gafir.md` 40/60 (*udʿūnī astajib lakum*). **The note there: *duʿāʾ* is identified in the Qurʾān with worship itself.**

***Tadharūna* — root و-ذ-ر: to abandon, to leave.** **And the root has a striking property: in Arabic the perfect (past) conjugation of this verb is not used;** only the imperfect and imperative forms are used. **This is a linguistic fact.**

### أَحْسَنَ ٱلْخَٰلِقِينَ — the best of creators

**And this construction occurs in two places in the Qurʾān:** al-Muʾminūn 23/14 (*fa-tabāraka'llāhu **aḥsanu'l-khāliqīn***) and here.

***Aḥsan* — root ح-س-ن, an elative: "the best, the most beautiful".** **And note: *aḥsan* — "the most beautiful", not *akbar* (the greatest) or *aqwā* (the strongest).**

**I record this as my own reading:** Elijah's measure of comparison is not **power** but **beauty.** His people call upon a master or an idol; Elijah sets against it not might but **the beauty of the work done.** **And this is from the same root as the word *muḥsinīn* in the sūra's closing mould.**

| Verse | Word | Root | Who |
|---|---|---|---|
| **125** | *aḥsana'l-**khāliqīn*** | ح-س-ن | **God** |
| **80, 105, 110, 121, 131** | *najzi'l-**muḥsinīn*** | ح-س-ن | The prophets |

**The same root, two sides: the one who does most beautifully, and those who do beautifully.** This is a fact that can be verified from the text, and in the Elijah section the two stand together (125 and 131).

***Al-khāliqīn* — root خ-ل-ق: to measure out, to bring into being by giving measure.** The original sense of the root is given by the lexicographers as **"to measure and cut a hide"**. **The root was analysed in `002-bakara.md`.**

### ٱللَّهَ رَبَّكُمْ وَرَبَّ ءَابَآئِكُمُ ٱلْأَوَّلِينَ — the Lord of the forefathers too

**And this verse is the most balanced sentence of the sūra on the question of following the forefathers.**

**A point of word order:** *Allāha* is in the accusative — it attaches to *aḥsana'l-khāliqīn* as an **appositive**. That is: "the best of creators — **that is, God**".

**And Elijah sets up two attributions:**

1. *rabbakum* — **your Lord**
2. *wa rabba ābāʾikumu'l-awwalīn* — **and the Lord of your forefathers of old**

**I record this as my own reading — and this is the point I indicated in the section on the seventy-first verse:** Elijah does not reject the forefathers. **He binds the forefathers too to the same Lord.**

**In the seventeenth verse of the sūra the deniers had objected *"and our forefathers of old too?"*. In the seventy-first it was said *"most of those of old had gone astray"*. In the hundred and twenty-sixth the Lord of the forefathers is named.**

**The sūra, then, says three different things about the forefathers, and the three do not conflict:**

| Verse | What is said |
|---|---|
| **69-70** | They found their forefathers astray and are rushed along in their tracks — **blind following is rejected** |
| **71** | **Most** of those of old went astray — **not all** |
| **126** | God is **the Lord of your forefathers too** — **the forefathers are not condemned wholesale** |

**This is the textual counterpart of `STYLE.md`'s principle "no wholesale judgement is built", and it runs in the same direction as the distinction of *muḥsin* and *ẓālim* in the hundred and thirteenth verse.**

### فَكَذَّبُوهُ فَإِنَّهُمْ لَمُحْضَرُونَ — denial and outcome

***Kadhdhaba* — root ك-ذ-ب, Form II: to deny.**

**A point of word order:** the two sentences are joined with *fāʾ* — **absence of interval.** *Fa-kadhdhabūhu **fa**-innahum la-muḥḍarūn.* **Between the denial and the outcome there is no intervening step whatever.**

***Muḥḍarūn* was analysed in the section on the fifty-seventh verse and a table was given. I do not repeat it.**

**And the difference within the sequence must be recorded:** in the Noah section the other side is named with the verb *aghraqnā* (We drowned), in the Abraham section with *jaʿalnāhumu'l-asfalīn* (We made them the lowest), in the Lot section with *dammarnā* (We razed). **In the Elijah section, however, no worldly destruction is named; it passes directly to the hereafter.**

| Prophet | The end of the other side | In this world or the next |
|---|---|---|
| Noah (82) | *aghraqna'l-ākharīn* | **In this world** |
| Abraham (98) | *jaʿalnāhumu'l-asfalīn* | Indefinite |
| Moses-Aaron (116) | (the prevailing side is named) | **In this world** |
| **Elijah (127)** | ***la-muḥḍarūn*** | **In the hereafter** |
| Lot (136) | *dammarna'l-ākharīn* | **In this world** |
| Jonah (148) | *fa-āmanū fa-mattaʿnāhum* | **Deliverance** |

**This is a fact that can be verified from the text and shows that the six sections of the sequence give six different ends.**

### إِلَّا عِبَادَ ٱللَّهِ ٱلْمُخْلَصِينَ — the third occurrence

**The sentence for the third time in the sūra (40, 74, 128). It was analysed in the section on the fortieth verse.**

**The note of word order here is important:** the exception attaches to the sentence *fa-innahum la-muḥḍarūn* — **a group is set apart, that is, from among those who denied.**

**I record this as my own reading:** the sentence says that a whole people does not meet the same end. **And this runs in the same direction as the distinction of *muḥsin* and *ẓālim* in the hundred and thirteenth verse and the expression "the Lord of the forefathers too" in the hundred and twenty-sixth.** In three separate places the sūra draws the same limit: **no wholesale judgement.**

### سَلَٰمٌ عَلَىٰٓ إِلْ يَاسِينَ — and the great question of variant readings

**And this verse is the most disputed question of variant reading and of language in the sūra.**

**In the received orthography of the codex the word is written in two parts: إِلْ يَاسِينَ.** In the hundred and twenty-third verse, however, it is إِلْيَاسَ, in one part.

**The differences of reading and pronunciation:**

| Reading | Spelling | Sense |
|---|---|---|
| ***Il Yāsīn*** | إِلْ يَاسِينَ | Another way of saying Elijah's name |
| ***Ilyāsīn*** | إِلْيَاسِينَ | A plural-like form of the same name |
| ***Āl Yāsīn*** | ءَالِ يَاسِينَ | "The family of Yāsīn / those belonging to Yāsīn" |

**All three readings are transmitted in the sources on the variant readings. I give no reciter's name.**

**And the linguistic explanations:**

| Explanation | What it says |
|---|---|
| **Two forms of the same name** | In Arabic proper names of foreign origin can be pronounced in more than one form. Like *Ibrāhīm / Ibrāhām*, *Ilyās / Ilyāsīn*. This is the most widely transmitted explanation |
| **Accord with the verse-ending** | All the verses of the sūra end in **-īn / -ūn / -īm**. The form *Ilyās* would break this sound; the form *Il Yāsīn* fits the verse-ending |
| ***Āl* = family** | "Peace be upon the family of Yāsīn" — in that case the name is not Elijah himself but the community attributed to him |

**The second explanation is supported by a textual datum and must be recorded:** as was given in the section on the structure, **from the twelfth verse to the hundred and eighty-second all the verse-endings are of the pattern long vowel + nūn/mīm. The form *Ilyāsa* would fall outside that pattern.**

**But this does not mean "it was changed merely for the sound"; what is recorded is that the two forms differ in their fit with the verse-ending.**

**And a note:** in the hundred and thirty-second verse the pronoun is **masculine singular** — *inna**hu** min ʿibādina'l-muʾminīn.* **The mould speaks of one person, that is.** This is a datum from within the word order against the "family" reading; **but it does not eliminate it, because the pronoun may also refer to *Ilyās* in verse 123.**

**I do not impose a preference.**

---

## 37/133-138 — وَإِنَّ لُوطًا لَّمِنَ ٱلْمُرْسَلِينَ · إِذْ نَجَّيْنَٰهُ وَأَهْلَهُۥٓ أَجْمَعِينَ · إِلَّا عَجُوزًا فِى ٱلْغَٰبِرِينَ · ثُمَّ دَمَّرْنَا ٱلْءَاخَرِينَ · وَإِنَّكُمْ لَتَمُرُّونَ عَلَيْهِم مُّصْبِحِينَ · وَبِٱلَّيْلِ ۗ أَفَلَا تَعْقِلُونَ

*Wa inna Lūṭan la-mina'l-mursalīn · Idh najjaynāhu wa ahlahu ajmaʿīn · Illā ʿajūzan fi'l-ghābirīn · Thumma dammarna'l-ākharīn · Wa innakum la-tamurrūna ʿalayhim muṣbiḥīn · Wa bi'l-layl, a-fa-lā taʿqilūn*

"And Lot too was one of the messengers sent. When We saved him and his whole household — save an old woman among those who stayed behind. Then We razed the others. And you pass by them in the morning — and by night as well. Will you still not use your reason?"

### The break in the sequence

**And this section is the first break in the sequence: not one of the four closing moulds is present.** It was given in a table in the section on the structure, and the possible explanations were discussed there. **I do not repeat it.**

**What is to be added here: what the section puts in their place.**

| The mould present in the sequence | What comes in its place in the Lot section |
|---|---|
| *wa taraknā ʿalayhi fi'l-ākhirīn* | *wa innakum la-tamurrūna ʿalayhim muṣbiḥīn* (137) |
| *salāmun ʿalā …* | — |
| *innā kadhālika najzi'l-muḥsinīn* | — |
| *innahu min ʿibādina'l-muʾminīn* | — |
| — | *a-fa-lā taʿqilūn* (138) — **a question to the addressee** |

**I record this as my own reading and complete the note in the section on the structure:** in the other sections of the sequence it is said "We left upon him a remembrance"; **here what was left is shown.** The verb "We left" is replaced by the sentence "you pass by them". **And this is a passage from a spoken memory to a visible one.**

### إِذْ نَجَّيْنَٰهُ وَأَهْلَهُۥٓ أَجْمَعِينَ — and the exception

***Ajmaʿīn* — root ج-م-ع: all, the whole.** **And it is used as a word of reinforcement (*taʾkīd*): "his whole household".**

**And the point of word order is worth noting — this is a textual datum:** *ajmaʿīn* ("all") is said and **immediately after comes an exception** (*illā ʿajūzan*). **Reinforcement and exception side by side, that is.**

**The explanation the grammarians record:** *ajmaʿīn* announces the **completeness** of those saved; the exception takes out the one **not included** in that group. **The two do not conflict:** "the whole of his household was saved — that woman was already among those who stayed behind."

**And there is a word that supports this explanation:** *fi'l-**ghābirīn***.

### فِى ٱلْغَٰبِرِينَ — those who stayed behind

***Ghābir* — root غ-ب-ر.** **And the root has an interesting property, which the lexicographers record: the root means both "to pass on" and "to stay behind"** — two senses that are opposites of one another. In Arabic this is called *aḍdād* (words with opposite meanings).

**From the same root *ghabara* — dust.** *Ghabara* — the dust that stays behind. **The link the lexicographers make: the dust left behind a passing caravan announces both pastness and remaining.**

**Its sense here: *those who stayed behind* — that is, those who remained together with those destroyed.**

**And the Qurʾān uses the same word consistently in this account:** *al-ghābirīn* occurs at al-Ḥijr 15/60, al-Shuʿarāʾ 26/171, al-Naml 27/57 and al-ʿAnkabūt 29/32-33 as well. **This is a countable fact.**

***ʿAjūz* — root ع-ج-ز: to be weak, to be unable.** *ʿAjūz* — an old woman. **The link the lexicographers make: old age is the exhaustion of strength.**

**The word was treated at `051-zariyat.md` 51/29** (*wa qālat **ʿajūzun** ʿaqīm*) — there Abraham's wife used it of herself. **The same word, two different women, two different sūras.** This is a datum about the word.

**And a note:** the verse gives no name, only an attribute. **No name is given in this commentary either.** Elsewhere the Qurʾān names this person as *imraʾatu Lūṭ* (the wife of Lot) (al-Taḥrīm 66/10), and **it was treated in `066-tahrim.md`.** **I rely on that treatment and do not repeat it.**

### ثُمَّ دَمَّرْنَا ٱلْءَاخَرِينَ — and the parallel with the Noah section

***Dammara* — root د-م-ر, Form II: to raze, to destroy utterly.**

**And the sentence has exactly the same structure as the eighty-second verse:**

| Verse | Sentence | Verb |
|---|---|---|
| **82** (Noah) | *thumma **aghraqnā**'l-ākharīn* | We drowned |
| **136** (Lot) | *thumma **dammarna**'l-ākharīn* | We razed |

**The same particle (*thumma*), the same object (*al-ākharīn*), the same position (toward the end of the section). Only the verb changes.** **This is a parallel that can be verified from the text.**

**And the observation recorded in the section on the eighty-second verse holds here too:** the sentence of destruction comes not in the middle of the section but at its end, and pushed away with *thumma*.

### وَإِنَّكُمْ لَتَمُرُّونَ عَلَيْهِم مُّصْبِحِينَ · وَبِٱلَّيْلِ — and the address turns to the one addressed

**And this is the one place in the whole sequence where it turns to the one addressed. This is a countable fact.**

**From the seventy-fifth verse to the hundred and thirty-sixth, for sixty-two verses, the telling is in the third person. In the hundred and thirty-seventh it passes suddenly to the second person: *innakum* — "you".**

**In Arabic this transition is called *iltifāt*, and it was treated in several places in this commentary** (`056-vakia.md` 56/51; in this sūra verses 21 and 38).

***Tamurrūn* — root م-ر-ر: to pass, to go by.**

**And the verb is imperfect: *la-tamurrūn* — "you keep passing by".** **Not once, that is, but continually.**

***Muṣbiḥīn* — root ص-ب-ح, the active participle of Form IV: those who are in the morning, who are at the hour of dawn.** In a circumstantial position: "passing at the hour of morning".

***Wa bi'l-layl* — "and by night".**

**A point of word order must be recorded:** the two times are given **in two different forms.** The morning by a **circumstantial** (*muṣbiḥīn* — an active participle), the night by a **preposition** (*bi'l-layl*). **Not symmetrical.**

**The grammarians' explanation:** *bi'l-layl* is an adverbial attached to *muṣbiḥīn*; the sense becomes "in the morning and by night". **The difference of form does not change the sense.**

**And what is said when the two times are named together is worth noting — as my own reading:** morning and night are **the two ends of the day.** When both are named, the sense "at all times" arises. **Passing by the ruin is not an exceptional event, then; it is a daily route.**

**And at `054-kamer.md` 54/38 another verse of this account was treated** (*wa laqad ṣabbaḥahum bukratan ʿadhābun mustaqirr*). **There too the hour of morning was named.** The two sūras use the same hour in the same account. **This is a datum about a word.**

### أَفَلَا تَعْقِلُونَ — will you not use your reason?

***ʿAqala* — root ع-ق-ل.** **And the concrete picture of the root is worth noting: *ʿiqāl* is the cord that ties a camel's knee.** *ʿAqala'l-baʿīr* — he tied the camel. **Reason (*ʿaql*), then, is a power of binding:** what keeps a person from going wherever he pleases.

**The root was treated at `067-mulk.md` 67/10 (*law kunnā nasmaʿu aw naʿqilu*). I rely on that treatment.**

**And the placing of the question here is worth noting — as my own reading:** the verse does not say "do you not see?". **Seeing is already happening** — the hundred and thirty-seventh verse says so: you pass by. **What is missing is not seeing but binding what one sees.**

**And this ties to the thirteenth verse of the sūra:** *wa idhā dhukkirū **lā yadhkurūn*** — "when they are reminded they do not remember". **The two verses describe the same gap: the break between the knowledge that arrives and the conclusion that is drawn.**

**And a note:** the Lot section is the section of the sequence nearest to the one addressed. **The other sections recount events that lie in the past; this section shows something the addressee passes by every day.** This is a fact worth considering together with the section's not receiving the closing mould.

---

## 37/139-148 — وَإِنَّ يُونُسَ لَمِنَ ٱلْمُرْسَلِينَ · إِذْ أَبَقَ إِلَى ٱلْفُلْكِ ٱلْمَشْحُونِ · فَسَاهَمَ فَكَانَ مِنَ ٱلْمُدْحَضِينَ · فَٱلْتَقَمَهُ ٱلْحُوتُ وَهُوَ مُلِيمٌ · فَلَوْلَآ أَنَّهُۥ كَانَ مِنَ ٱلْمُسَبِّحِينَ · لَلَبِثَ فِى بَطْنِهِۦٓ إِلَىٰ يَوْمِ يُبْعَثُونَ · فَنَبَذْنَٰهُ بِٱلْعَرَآءِ وَهُوَ سَقِيمٌ · وَأَنۢبَتْنَا عَلَيْهِ شَجَرَةً مِّن يَقْطِينٍ · وَأَرْسَلْنَٰهُ إِلَىٰ مِا۟ئَةِ أَلْفٍ أَوْ يَزِيدُونَ · فَـَٔامَنُوا۟ فَمَتَّعْنَٰهُمْ إِلَىٰ حِينٍ

*Wa inna Yūnusa la-mina'l-mursalīn · Idh abaqa ila'l-fulki'l-mashḥūn · Fa-sāhama fa-kāna mina'l-mudḥaḍīn · Fa'ltaqamahu'l-ḥūtu wa huwa mulīm · Fa-lawlā annahu kāna mina'l-musabbiḥīn · La-labitha fī baṭnihi ilā yawmi yubʿathūn · Fa-nabadhnāhu bi'l-ʿarāʾi wa huwa saqīm · Wa anbatnā ʿalayhi shajaratan min yaqṭīn · Wa arsalnāhu ilā miʾati alfin aw yazīdūn · Fa-āmanū fa-mattaʿnāhum ilā ḥīn*

"And Jonah too was one of the messengers sent. When he ran off to the laden ship. He drew lots and was among those who lost. Then the fish swallowed him whole, and he was one deserving blame. Had he not been of those who glorify, he would have stayed in its belly until the day they are raised. But We cast him out upon the bare shore, and he was sick. And We caused a gourd plant to grow over him. And We sent him to a hundred thousand — or more. And they believed; so We let them enjoy for a time."

### أَبَقَ — the flight of a slave

**And this is one of the harshest choices of word in the sūra.**

***Abaqa* — root أ-ب-ق.** **And the word is analysed here for the first time in this commentary.**

**The sense the lexicographers give is very narrow, and this narrowness must be recorded: *ibāq* is *a slave's running away from his master without leave*.** *Ābiq* — a runaway slave. **The word is not used in Arabic of any other flight; the flight of a free man is called *haraba* or *farra*.**

**In the Qurʾān the word occurs only here.**

**And the harshness of the word lies in this — and I record it as a linguistic datum:**

| Word | Of whom | What it announces |
|---|---|---|
| **هَرَبَ** | Anyone | To flee (general) |
| **فَرَّ** | Anyone | To flee away |
| **أَبَقَ** | **Of a slave alone** | **To leave his owner without leave** |

**The verse, that is, recounts a prophet's departure in the language of the relation of slave and master.**

**And what this requires must be written:** the word sets up a **relation of authority.** The one who flees is not counted ill-intentioned; **it is said that he departed without leave.** The offence of a runaway slave is not theft or rebellion but **not being where he ought to be.**

**And that this word is used of a prophet is one of the striking places of the Qurʾān.**

**At `068-kalem.md` 68/48-50 the same person was named as *a negative example*** (*wa lā takun ka-ṣāḥibi'l-ḥūt*), **and it was recorded there: "a prophet is named as a negative example; this is rare in the Qurʾān and must be read carefully." I rely on that treatment.**

**And the difference between the two sūras must be recorded:**

| | **Al-Qalam 68/48-50** | **Al-Ṣāffāt 37/139-148** |
|---|---|---|
| **His name** | **Not given** — *ṣāḥibu'l-ḥūt* | ***Yūnus*** — plainly |
| **His presentation** | A negative example: "do not be like him" | **Positive:** *la-mina'l-mursalīn* |
| **The verb** | *nādā wa huwa makẓūm* — he called | ***abaqa*** — he ran off |
| **The deliverance** | *tadārakahu niʿmatun min rabbih* | *kāna mina'l-musabbiḥīn* |
| **The end** | *fa'jtabāhu rabbuhu fa-jaʿalahu mina'ṣ-ṣāliḥīn* | *fa-āmanū fa-mattaʿnāhum* — **the people believe** |

**The two sūras recount the same person from two different angles: the one as a warning, the other as a messenger.** **And al-Ṣāffāt holds both the praise and the harsh word in the same section** — in 139 *mina'l-mursalīn*, in 140 *abaqa*, in 142 *mulīm*.

**I record this as my own reading:** the section sets up neither an acquittal nor a condemnation. **It leaves the two side by side.**

### ٱلْفُلْكِ ٱلْمَشْحُونِ — the laden ship

***Fulk* — a ship.** **And in Arabic the word is used both as singular and as plural** — its form does not change. Both uses are present in the Qurʾān.

***Mashḥūn* — root ش-ح-ن, a passive participle: *filled, loaded to the brim.*** *Shaḥana* — he loaded, he filled.

**In the Qurʾān the same attribute is used of Noah's ark too:** *ḥamalnā dhurriyyatahum fi'l-**fulki'l-mashḥūn*** (Yā Sīn 36/41). **The same construction, two different ships.**

**I record this as my own reading:** the attribute announces that the ship was **full**, and this prepares the drawing of lots in the next verse. **On a full ship an extra passenger is a problem.** The verse does not state the reason, but the attribute implies it.

### فَسَاهَمَ فَكَانَ مِنَ ٱلْمُدْحَضِينَ — the lots

***Sāhama* — root س-ه-م, Form III.** **And the word is analysed here for the first time in this commentary.**

***Sahm* — an arrow.** And the concrete picture of the root is this: **when the Arabs divided a matter or made a choice they used marked arrows;** whoever's name the drawn arrow bore received the portion. **From this *sahm* took the sense of "a portion, a share"** — the Turkish word *sehim* ("share") comes from this root.

***Musāhama* (Form III) — to draw lots against one another, to compete in a drawing of lots.** **Form III announces two parties entering into a matter with one another.**

*Sāhama*, that is, is not "he drew lots" but more exactly **"he took part in the drawing of lots"**. This is a datum of the pattern.

***Mudḥaḍīn* — root د-ح-ض.** The sense the lexicographers give: **to slip, for the foot to slip, to fail to hold.** *Daḥaḍa* — it slipped; *madḥaḍa* — a slippery place. **And from this the sense "to be void, to be refuted" arises:** in the Qurʾān *ḥujjatuhum **dāḥiḍatun** ʿinda rabbihim* (al-Shūrā 42/16) — "their argument is void with their Lord". **Treated in `042-sura.md`.**

***Mudḥaḍ* — the passive participle of Form IV: made to slip, brought down, the loser in a drawing of lots.**

**I record this as my own reading:** the word does not say "he lost"; **it says "he was among those made to slip"** — a passive. **Losing in a drawing of lots is not something within a person's power.** And this sets up a balance beside the harshness of the verb *abaqa*: the departure was his act, the outcome of the lot was not.

**And a note: the verse does not say why lots were drawn.** In the classical commentaries it is said that the ship was in danger of sinking and the excess was to be thrown overboard. **The verse does not give this; nor do I.**

### فَٱلْتَقَمَهُ ٱلْحُوتُ — swallowed him whole

***Iltaqama* — root ل-ق-م, Form VIII.** ***Luqma* — a mouthful.** **The verb, then, is "to swallow in a single mouthful".**

**I record this as my own reading:** the verb is not *akala* (he ate) or *balaʿa* (he swallowed). ***Iltaqama*** — without chewing, at one go. **And the picture of "a mouthful" in the root of the word reduces a human being to the size of a mouthful.** This is what sets up the scene in respect of scale.

***Al-ḥūt* — the fish.** **And the word comes definite: *al-ḥūt*.**

**The grammarians' explanation:** the definiteness here announces **the class** — "the thing called fish" — or points to a known event. **The verse does not say the species of the fish, and neither do I.**

***Wa huwa mulīm* — a circumstantial clause.**

***Mulīm* — root ل-و-م, the active participle of Form IV.** ***Lawm* — to blame.** **Form IV here announces "to have done something that deserves blame":** *alāma* — he did something blameworthy. ***Mulīm*, then, is not "one who blames" but "one who has done a thing deserving blame".**

**The word occurs in two places in the Qurʾān: one here, one at `051-zariyat.md` 51/40:** *fa-akhadhnāhu wa junūdahu fa-nabadhnāhum fi'l-yammi **wa huwa mulīm*** — **of Pharaoh.**

**And the resemblance between the two verses is striking and is entirely a textual datum:**

| | **Al-Dhāriyāt 51/40 (Pharaoh)** | **Al-Ṣāffāt 37/142-145 (Jonah)** |
|---|---|---|
| **The shared verb** | *fa-**nabadhnā**hum fi'l-yamm* | *fa-**nabadhnā**hu bi'l-ʿarāʾ* |
| **The shared circumstantial** | *wa huwa **mulīm*** | *wa huwa **mulīm*** |
| **Where he was cast** | **Into the sea** | **Onto the shore** |
| **The end** | Destruction | **Deliverance** |

**The same two words (*nabadhnā* and *mulīm*) are used of two different persons, and the outcomes are opposite.** **I record this as a textual observation; I do not claim that the two verses refer to one another.**

### فَلَوْلَآ أَنَّهُۥ كَانَ مِنَ ٱلْمُسَبِّحِينَ — and what saved him

***Lawlā* — "had it not been".** It announces a condition that was not realised.

***Musabbiḥ* — root س-ب-ح, the active participle of Form II: one who glorifies.** **And the original sense of the root: *to swim in water or in air, to glide on.*** The Qurʾān: *kullun fī falakin **yasbaḥūn*** (al-Anbiyāʾ 21/33). **The root was treated at `056-vakia.md` 56/74, 96 (*fa-sabbiḥ bi'smi rabbika*) and in `087-ala.md`.**

**The note there holds here too: *tasbīḥ*, by its root, is *to keep at a distance*** — to hold God **away** from everything unworthy of Him, to declare Him free of it. Its link with the sense of swimming: what swims **moves away** from where it is.

**And the word occurs in three places in the sūra — this is a countable fact:**

| Verse | Form | Who |
|---|---|---|
| **143** | *mina'l-**musabbiḥīn*** | **Jonah** |
| **159** | ***subḥāna**'llāhi ʿammā yaṣifūn* | (The text) |
| **166** | *wa innā la-naḥnu'l-**musabbiḥūn*** | **The speaking company** |
| **180** | ***subḥāna** rabbika rabbi'l-ʿizzati* | (The text) |

**And the hundred and forty-third and hundred and sixty-sixth verses share the same mould:** *al-musabbiḥīn* / *al-musabbiḥūn*. **The one a prophet inside a belly, the other a company standing in ranks in the sky.** **The same act, two extreme positions.**

**I record this as my own reading:** the work done in the belly of the fish and the work done in the heavens are named with the same word. **And the sūra ties the deliverance to this act:** *fa-lawlā annahu kāna mina'l-musabbiḥīn.*

**And a point of word order:** the verse does not say "because he glorified" but **"because he was of those who glorify"**. **What saves, then, is not an act at that moment but a belonging.** *Kāna mina'l-musabbiḥīn* — *kāna* announces the past: **he was of that company already.**

**This is the most meaningful use of the sūra's preference for the mould of the group (see the section on verse 81).**

### لَلَبِثَ فِى بَطْنِهِۦٓ إِلَىٰ يَوْمِ يُبْعَثُونَ — until the day they are raised

***Labitha* — root ل-ب-ث: to remain in a place, to linger.**

And the apodosis of the condition is worth noting: *ilā yawmi **yubʿathūn*** — "until the day they are raised".

**I record this as my own reading:** the verse does not say "he would have remained a long time". **It gives a date: until the resurrection.** And in the sixteenth verse the deniers had asked *a-innā la-**mabʿūthūn*** ("are we really to be raised?"). **The same root (ب-ع-ث), the same sūra.** Here the fact of the resurrection is used, without dispute, as a measure of time.

**And the verb is plural: *yubʿathūn* — "they are raised".** Jonah is one person; the plural means **all human beings.** **The verse ties a personal confinement, that is, to a general day.**

### فَنَبَذْنَٰهُ بِٱلْعَرَآءِ — We cast him onto the bare shore

***Nabadha* — root ن-ب-ذ: to throw a thing away as worthless, to fling it down.** The root was treated at `051-zariyat.md` 51/40.

**And the harshness of the verb is worth noting:** *nabadha* is not to set down carefully but **to throw**. **Yet the outcome is deliverance.**

***Al-ʿarāʾ* — root ع-ر-ي: to be bare.** ***ʿArāʾ*** — **a flat place with nothing upon it, without tree, without shade, without shelter.**

**And the same word had occurred at `068-kalem.md` 68/49:** *la-nubidha **bi'l-ʿarāʾi** wa huwa **madhmūm*** — "he would have been cast onto the bare shore, blamed".

**The difference between the two verses is exactly this, and it is read off the text:**

| | **Al-Qalam 68/49** | **Al-Ṣāffāt 37/145** |
|---|---|---|
| **Verb** | *la-**nubidha*** — passive, an **unrealised** condition | *fa-**nabadhnā**hu* — active, **realised** |
| **Place** | *bi'l-ʿarāʾ* | *bi'l-ʿarāʾ* |
| **His state** | *wa huwa **madhmūm*** — blamed | *wa huwa **saqīm*** — sick |
| **The condition** | *lawlā an tadārakahu niʿmatun min rabbih* | *fa-lawlā annahu kāna mina'l-musabbiḥīn* |

**The two sūras recount the same scene, that is, and part at the last word: the one *madhmūm* (blamed), the other *saqīm* (sick).**

**Al-Qalam says "he would have been cast, blamed", and announces that he was not cast. Al-Ṣāffāt says "We cast him" and gives his state as *sick*.**

**I record this as my own reading, and it is not binding:** the two verses do not conflict, because what is negated is not the same. **In al-Qalam what is negated is being cast *madhmūm*** (blamed); in al-Ṣāffāt what is realised is being cast *saqīm* (sick). **The blame has been lifted, the sickness has remained.** This is an explanation; the text does not set up the link between the two verses openly.

***Saqīm* was analysed in the section on the eighty-ninth verse, and the sharing of the word between Abraham and Jonah was given there in a table.**

### وَأَنۢبَتْنَا عَلَيْهِ شَجَرَةً مِّن يَقْطِينٍ — a gourd plant

***Anbata* — root ن-ب-ت, Form IV: to cause to grow.** The root was treated at `071-nuh.md` 71/17 (*wa'llāhu anbatakum mina'l-arḍi nabātā*) and in `050-kaf.md` and `078-nebe.md`.

***ʿAlayhi* — "over him".** **The preposition is worth noting:** not *lahu* (for him). ***ʿAlā*** announces being above — **the plant covers him over.**

***Yaqṭīn* — root ي-ق-ط-ن (or ق-ط-ن).** **And the word occurs only here in the Qurʾān.**

**The definition the lexicographers give: *yaqṭīn* is the general name of plants that do not stand upright on a stem but spread on the ground.** The gourd, the watermelon and the melon fall into this class. **In most of the classical commentaries it is explained as "gourd" (*kad*).**

**And its link with the root's sense of "settling" is transmitted:** *qaṭana* — he settled in a place, he dwelt. **The link is made thus: *yaqṭīn* is the plant that lies on the ground, that settles upon it.** I give this link as a transmission from the lexicographers.

**And a point of word order: *shajaratan min yaqṭīn* — "a tree/plant of the *yaqṭīn* kind".**

***Shajara* in Arabic means not only "tree" but also a plant that has a stem.** **And the lexicographers record a tension here:** *shajara* announces an upright plant, while *yaqṭīn* announces one that spreads on the ground. **The construction joins the two.**

**The grammarians' explanation: the construct *min yaqṭīn* specifies the kind of the *shajara*** — that is, "something of the plant kind, of the *yaqṭīn* sort". **The wide use of the word makes this construction possible.**

**And the work of the plant is not said in the verse.** The preposition *ʿalayhi* (over him) carries the idea of shade and covering. **In the classical commentaries explanations such as its giving shade, keeping off flies, or its fruit serving as food are transmitted. The verse does not say, and I issue no ruling.**

**As my own reading I record only this:** the hundred and forty-fifth verse said *al-ʿarāʾ* — **a place without shade, without cover.** The hundred and forty-sixth sets a cover there. **The two verses complete one another: a bare place and a plant caused to grow over it.**

### وَأَرْسَلْنَٰهُ إِلَىٰ مِا۟ئَةِ أَلْفٍ أَوْ يَزِيدُونَ — "or more"

**And this expression is one of the most disputed linguistic questions of the sūra.**

***Miʾati alfin* — "a hundred thousand".** ***Aw yazīdūn* — "or more / or they exceed that".**

**The problem is this:** *aw* (or) in Arabic announces **doubt** or **hesitation**. **To say "a hundred thousand or more" in a declarative sentence seems to imply that the number is not known.**

**The explanations the classical lexicographers give. I give them in a table and impose no preference:**

| Explanation | What *aw* is doing | Assessment |
|---|---|---|
| **In the sense of *bal*** | "A hundred thousand — **indeed** more". *Aw* is sometimes used in Arabic in place of *bal* (rather) | It is transmitted; but this use of *aw* is not widespread |
| **According to the beholder** | The doubt belongs not to the speaker but **to the one who sees**: "one who saw them would have said a hundred thousand or more". The number is given, that is, by the estimate of the beholder | **The most defended explanation.** The lexicographers call this *"speech according to the state of the addressee"* |
| **An announcement of multitude** | The intent is not to give an exact number but to announce **the multitude**. Like the English "there were a hundred thousand, maybe more" | The usage is familiar in the language |
| **An exact number with an open end** | That they were at least a hundred thousand is certain; what is above that is not announced. *Aw* fixes the lower bound | It preserves the informational value of the sentence |
| **In the sense of *wāw*** | "A hundred thousand **and** more" | The grammarians record that *aw* can be used in place of *wāw*; but this view is a minority one |

**And there is a datum of word order that must be recorded:** the verse does not say *aw **akthar*** ("or more"); it uses ***aw yazīdūn*** — **a verbal clause**. *Yazīdūn* — "they increase / they are more".

**I record this as my own reading, and it is not binding:** the use of a verb takes the number out of being a fixed figure. **A community is not fixed; it increases.** *Aw yazīdūn* may also be read not as "their number is this much or that much" but as "a hundred thousand — and increasing". **This eliminates none of the explanations above.**

**And a note of limitation:** no historical, geographical or demographic conclusion is drawn from this number. **The Qurʾān gives no place name, and none is given in this commentary either.**

### فَـَٔامَنُوا۟ فَمَتَّعْنَٰهُمْ إِلَىٰ حِينٍ — and the one act of believing in the sequence

**And this is the most striking closing in the sequence of prophets.**

**As was recorded in the section on the structure: in five of the sequence's six sections the subject of the verb that determines the end is God. Here the subject of the last verb is the people themselves: *fa-āmanū* — "they believed".**

**This is a fact that can be verified from the text.**

***Mattaʿnā* — root م-ت-ع, Form II: to let enjoy, to provide enjoyment for a time.** *Matāʿ* — a passing benefit, a provision. **The root was treated at `051-zariyat.md` 51/43 (*tamattaʿū ḥattā ḥīn*) and in `040-gafir.md`.**

***Ilā ḥīn* — "for a time".** ***Ḥīn* — an indefinite stretch of time.** **And the word will occur twice more in the sūra:** *fa-tawalla ʿanhum **ḥattā ḥīn*** (174) and *wa tawalla ʿanhum **ḥattā ḥīn*** (178).

| Verse | Expression | To whom |
|---|---|---|
| **148** | *fa-mattaʿnāhum **ilā ḥīn*** | **The people who believed** |
| **174** | *fa-tawalla ʿanhum **ḥattā ḥīn*** | A command to the Prophet |
| **178** | *wa tawalla ʿanhum **ḥattā ḥīn*** | A command to the Prophet |

**The same indefinite term in all three occurrences. This is a countable repetition.**

**I record this as my own reading:** after recounting through six sections destruction, drowning, razing and being brought low, the sequence closes with **a people who believe.** **And this people is the only one for which the verse gives a number: a hundred thousand.** The most numerous community of the sequence, that is, is the community that is saved. **This is a textual observation; the verse does not set it down as a ruling.**

---

## 37/149-157 — فَٱسْتَفْتِهِمْ أَلِرَبِّكَ ٱلْبَنَاتُ وَلَهُمُ ٱلْبَنُونَ · أَمْ خَلَقْنَا ٱلْمَلَٰٓئِكَةَ إِنَٰثًا وَهُمْ شَٰهِدُونَ · أَلَآ إِنَّهُم مِّنْ إِفْكِهِمْ لَيَقُولُونَ · وَلَدَ ٱللَّهُ وَإِنَّهُمْ لَكَٰذِبُونَ · أَصْطَفَى ٱلْبَنَاتِ عَلَى ٱلْبَنِينَ · مَا لَكُمْ كَيْفَ تَحْكُمُونَ · أَفَلَا تَذَكَّرُونَ · أَمْ لَكُمْ سُلْطَٰنٌ مُّبِينٌ · فَأْتُوا۟ بِكِتَٰبِكُمْ إِن كُنتُمْ صَٰدِقِينَ

*Fa'staftihim a-li-rabbika'l-banātu wa lahumu'l-banūn · Am khalaqna'l-malāʾikata ināthan wa hum shāhidūn · Alā innahum min ifkihim la-yaqūlūn · Walada'llāhu wa innahum la-kādhibūn · Aṣṭafa'l-banāti ʿala'l-banīn · Mā lakum kayfa taḥkumūn · A-fa-lā tadhakkarūn · Am lakum sulṭānun mubīn · Fa'tū bi-kitābikum in kuntum ṣādiqīn*

"Now ask them for their opinion: are the daughters your Lord's and the sons theirs? Or did We create the angels female while they were witnesses? Take note! Out of their fabrication they say, 'God has begotten' — and they are certainly liars. — Has He chosen daughters over sons? What is the matter with you, how do you judge? Will you not reflect? Or have you a plain warrant? Bring your book, then, if you are truthful."

### فَٱسْتَفْتِهِمْ — a second time

**It was analysed in the eleventh verse, and the table of the two occurrences was given there. I do not repeat it.**

**The note here: the verb opens another block.** In the eleventh verse creation was asked about; here, **attribution.** **Both are a "which of the two" question, and both ask for an opinion, not for information.**

### أَلِرَبِّكَ ٱلْبَنَاتُ وَلَهُمُ ٱلْبَنُونَ — and the link with `053-necm.md`

**And the analysis of this verse was made in detail at `053-necm.md` 53/21-22** (*a-lakumu'dh-dhakaru wa lahu'l-unthā · tilka idhan qismatun ḍīzā*), **and the structure of the proof, the catching of the addressee by his own admission, and the point about *taqdīm* were analysed there. I do not repeat it and I rely on that treatment.**

**The most important note there holds here unchanged, and it must be repeated:**

> **The verse does not accept that a daughter is of less worth; it uses the addressee's own admission against him.** This is a technique the Qurʾān often uses: to speak by the other side's own measure.

**In the section on `081-tekvir.md` 81/8-9 (*wa idha'l-mawʾūdatu suʾilat · bi-ayyi dhanbin qutilat*) this social background was treated in detail** — the notes about the position of the girl child in that society, the verse al-Naḥl 16/58-59, and the extent of the practice were given there. **I rely on that treatment and do not repeat it.**

**What is to be added here is the difference of word order between al-Najm and al-Ṣāffāt — and this is a textual datum:**

| | **Al-Najm 53/21** | **Al-Ṣāffāt 37/149** |
|---|---|---|
| **Order** | *a-**lakumu'dh-dhakaru** wa **lahu'l-unthā*** — **they** first | *a-**li-rabbika'l-banātu** wa **lahumu'l-banūn*** — **the Lord** first |
| **Words** | *al-dhakar / al-unthā* — **names of sex** | *al-banāt / al-banūn* — **names of offspring** |
| **The sentence of judgement** | *tilka idhan qismatun ḍīzā* — "an unjust division" | *mā lakum kayfa taḥkumūn* (154) — "how do you judge?" |

**Two differences are worth noting:**

**One — the order is reversed.** In al-Najm the addressee's portion is named first, then God's. In al-Ṣāffāt God's portion first. **As my own reading: in al-Ṣāffāt the address is to the Prophet (*li-rabbika* — "your Lord"), in al-Najm directly to those addressed (*lakum* — "yours"). The order changes according to the direction of address.**

**Two — the choice of words differs.** Al-Najm says *dhakar/unthā* (male/female) — **biological**; al-Ṣāffāt says *banāt/banūn* (daughters/sons) — **kinship.** **In al-Ṣāffāt the matter is set up as *the attribution of offspring*, in al-Najm as *a portion of sex*.**

**And the sentence of judgement differs too in al-Ṣāffāt:** al-Najm gives a **quality** (*qismatun ḍīzā* — an unjust division); al-Ṣāffāt asks a **question** (*kayfa taḥkumūn* — how do you judge?). **Al-Najm states the judgement, that is; al-Ṣāffāt makes the judgement be asked for.** This is consistent with the sūra's method of *istiftāʾ* (asking for an opinion).

### أَمْ خَلَقْنَا ٱلْمَلَٰٓئِكَةَ إِنَٰثًا وَهُمْ شَٰهِدُونَ — were they witnesses?

**And this verse questions the *source of knowledge* of the claim.**

***Shāhid* — root ش-ه-د: to be present at an event, to see with one's eye.** *Shahāda* — a report resting on witnessing. **The root was treated at `050-kaf.md` 50/21, 37.**

**A point of word order:** *wa hum shāhidūn* is **a circumstantial clause** — "were they present when We created the angels female?"

**The question sets up this logic:** to be able to say how a thing was created one must have **witnessed** that moment. **And they are not witnesses.**

**The Qurʾān sets up the same proof elsewhere too:** *mā ashhadtuhum khalqa's-samāwāti wa'l-arḍi wa lā khalqa anfusihim* (al-Kahf 18/51).

**And at `053-necm.md` 53/27-28 the same claim was handled in the same way:** *wa mā lahum bihi **min ʿilm**, in yattabiʿūna **illa'ẓ-ẓann***. **There the name of the proof is *the absence of knowledge*, here *the absence of witnessing*.** The two sūras show the same gap with two different words.

### أَلَآ إِنَّهُم مِّنْ إِفْكِهِمْ لَيَقُولُونَ · وَلَدَ ٱللَّهُ — and the break in the word order

***Alā*** — in Arabic the particle of **drawing attention**: "take note, know well that".

**And a point of word order is worth noting: the sentence is divided between two verses, and the verse ends exactly where the quotation is to begin.**

| Verse | Content |
|---|---|
| **151** | *alā innahum min ifkihim **la-yaqūlūn*** — "they certainly **say**" |
| **152** | ***walada'llāh*** — "God has begotten" — and immediately after: *wa innahum la-kādhibūn* |

**The words to be said, that is, have been thrown to the other side of the verse boundary.** **And at the moment they are said the judgement upon them is given in the same verse:** *wa innahum la-kādhibūn.*

**I record this as my own reading:** the claim is only two words (*walada'llāh*) and is not left standing alone — it is refuted within the same verse. **The sūra does not allow this sentence to stand on its own.**

***Ifk* was analysed in the section on the eighty-sixth verse.** **And as was recorded there: *ifk* is not "a lie" but "speech that has turned the truth about".**

**And the link between the two occurrences must be recorded — this is a textual datum:**

| Verse | Expression | Who |
|---|---|---|
| **86** | *a-**ifkan** ālihatan dūna'llāhi turīdūn* | **Abraham's question** |
| **151** | *innahum **min ifkihim** la-yaqūlūn* | **The text — about those addressed** |

**The question Abraham put to his people returns as a judgement upon those the sūra addresses.** Sixty-five verses lie between the two.

***Walada* — root و-ل-د: to beget.** **And the choice of the verb is sharp: not *ittakhadha waladan* ("He took a child") but *walada* ("He begot").** The Qurʾān uses both expressions. **The form here is rawer and more direct.**

***Kādhibūn* — root ك-ذ-ب.** **And this is the third use of this root in the sūra:** *tukadhdhibūn* (21), *kadhdhabūhu* (127), *kādhibūn* (152).

### أَصْطَفَى ٱلْبَنَاتِ عَلَى ٱلْبَنِينَ — has He chosen?

***Iṣṭafā* — root ص-ف-و, Form VIII: to select the best, the purest part of a thing.** *Ṣafwa* — the essence of a thing, its part free of dregs. **In the Qurʾān: *inna'llāha'ṣṭafā Ādama wa Nūḥan* (Āl ʿImrān 3/33).**

**A point of word order and of pronunciation:** the hamza at the head is **the interrogative hamza** and has merged with the connecting hamza of the verb *iṣṭafā*. **The reading is therefore *a-iṣṭafā* → *aṣṭafā*.** This is a regular merging in Arabic.

**And the choice of the verb carries the proof — as my own reading:** *iṣṭifāʾ* is to select **the better** of two things. **The question is this: "if He chose one of the two sexes, did He choose the one you count of less worth?"**

**The verse, that is, again uses the addressee's own measure. This is the same technique recorded in `053-necm.md`.**

### مَا لَكُمْ كَيْفَ تَحْكُمُونَ — how do you judge?

***Mā lakum* — for the third and last time in the sūra** (25, 92, 154). **The table was given in the section on the twenty-fifth verse.**

***Taḥkumūn* — root ح-ك-م.** **And the original sense of the root was analysed at `068-kalem.md` 68/36: *to bridle, to rein in*.** *Ḥakama* — he put the bit into the horse's mouth. **A "judgement", then, is to set a thing in its place instead of leaving it loose.**

**And the same sentence had occurred at `068-kalem.md` 68/36:** *mā lakum **kayfa taḥkumūn***. **The same sentence, two sūras.** This is a countable fact.

**I record this as my own reading:** the question is not "why do you judge thus?" but **"how do you judge?"** — that is, what is questioned is not the ground of the judgement but **the authority to judge.** **And the following verses will ask this openly: where is your evidence?**

### أَفَلَا تَذَكَّرُونَ — will you not reflect?

***Tadhakkarūn* — root ذ-ك-ر, Form V: to remember of oneself, to think and draw a lesson.**

**And the root for the fourth time in the sūra.** In the third verse *dhikrā* (what is carried), in the thirteenth *lā yadhkurūn* (what is not taken), here *a-fa-lā tadhakkarūn* (what is asked for), in the hundred and sixty-eighth *dhikran mina'l-awwalīn* (what they asked for and rejected). **It was given in a table in the section on the structure.**

**And a note of word order:** the verse is very short — three words. **One of the shortest verses of the sūra.** **A short pause set in the middle of a long interrogation.**

### أَمْ لَكُمْ سُلْطَٰنٌ مُّبِينٌ · فَأْتُوا۟ بِكِتَٰبِكُمْ — the demand for evidence

***Sulṭān* was analysed in the section on the thirtieth verse:** an argument, a proof; and the power of rule. **The root was treated at `053-necm.md` 53/23 as well** (*mā anzala'llāhu bihā min sulṭān*) — **and there the same word was used about the same claim.**

***Kitāb* — root ك-ت-ب: to write.** **And the demand is concrete: *fa'tū bi-kitābikum* — "bring your book".**

**I record this as my own reading:** the verse does not say "bring a proof" but **"bring your book"**. **Not a spoken claim, that is, but a written document is asked for.** And this has to do with the nature of the claim: **the ground of one who judges about an unseen realm can only be an announcement.**

**And a similar demand was treated at `046-ahkaf.md` 46/4** (*iʾtūnī bi-kitābin min qabli hādhā aw athāratin min ʿilm*). **I rely on that treatment.**

***In kuntum ṣādiqīn* — "if you are truthful".** It is the common closing of the Qurʾān's sentences of challenge.

---

## 37/158-163 — وَجَعَلُوا۟ بَيْنَهُۥ وَبَيْنَ ٱلْجِنَّةِ نَسَبًا ۚ وَلَقَدْ عَلِمَتِ ٱلْجِنَّةُ إِنَّهُمْ لَمُحْضَرُونَ · سُبْحَٰنَ ٱللَّهِ عَمَّا يَصِفُونَ · إِلَّا عِبَادَ ٱللَّهِ ٱلْمُخْلَصِينَ · فَإِنَّكُمْ وَمَا تَعْبُدُونَ · مَآ أَنتُمْ عَلَيْهِ بِفَٰتِنِينَ · إِلَّا مَنْ هُوَ صَالِ ٱلْجَحِيمِ

*Wa jaʿalū baynahu wa bayna'l-jinnati nasaban, wa laqad ʿalimati'l-jinnatu innahum la-muḥḍarūn · Subḥāna'llāhi ʿammā yaṣifūn · Illā ʿibāda'llāhi'l-mukhlaṣīn · Fa-innakum wa mā taʿbudūn · Mā antum ʿalayhi bi-fātinīn · Illā man huwa ṣāli'l-jaḥīm*

"They have set up a kinship between Him and the jinn — while the jinn know well that they themselves will be brought. Glory be to God above what they describe — except God's chosen servants. Neither you nor what you serve · can lead anyone astray against Him — save the one who is to enter the blaze."

### نَسَبًا — a kinship

***Nasab* — root ن-س-ب: to attach a thing to a thing, to attribute.** *Nisba* — a relation; *nasab* — a tie of blood, lineage.

***Al-jinna* — root ج-ن-ن: to cover, to hide.** The root was analysed in detail in `072-cin.md`. **I rely on that treatment.**

**And there is disagreement over the sense of the word *al-jinna* here:**

| View | Who *al-jinna* are | Ground |
|---|---|---|
| **The angels** | The word, in the sense of "hidden beings", can be given to angels too; and the context is the angels (150) | The preceding verses take up the counting of angels as female; this verse would be the continuation of the same claim |
| **The jinn** | The known sense of the word | The Qurʾān recounts that some communities served the jinn (Sabaʾ 34/41) |
| **The satans / another order** | Another unseen order | It is transmitted |

**All three are transmitted in the classical commentaries. I do not impose a preference.**

**And a note of word order: *baynahu wa bayna'l-jinnati* — "between Him and the jinn".** **The pronoun (*hu*) refers to God, and His name is not mentioned.** This is a choice that increases the weight of the sentence: **not even the subject of the claim is written out.**

***Wa laqad ʿalimati'l-jinnatu innahum la-muḥḍarūn* — a point of word order.**

**Whom the sentence concerns can be read in two ways:**

| Reading | To whom *innahum* refers |
|---|---|
| **To the jinn** | "The jinn know that they themselves will be brought" — that is, that they will be called to account |
| **To those who make the claim** | "The jinn know that they (the claimants) will be brought" |

**Both are transmitted by the grammarians.** **The first is the more widespread, and its logic is this:** a being that is to be called to account cannot have a kinship with God. **The proof, that is, comes from the state of the very party who is claimed.**

**I record this as my own reading:** the first reading gives the sentence the value of a testimony. **The party claimed refutes the claim by its own condition** — in Sūrat al-Jinn it was the jinn themselves who said that the sky was closed (`072-cin.md`). **The same method: the knowledge comes from the subject of the claim.**

***Muḥḍarūn* — for the third and last time in the sūra** (57, 127, 158). **The table was given in the section on the fifty-seventh verse.**

### سُبْحَٰنَ ٱللَّهِ عَمَّا يَصِفُونَ — declaring Him free

***Subḥān* — root س-ب-ح, a verbal noun: declaring free of all unworthiness.** **The root was analysed in the section on the hundred and forty-third verse, and the table of the four occurrences in the sūra was given there.**

***Yaṣifūn* — root و-ص-ف: to qualify and describe a thing.** ***Ṣifa* is from the same root.** **This root is analysed here for the first time in this commentary.**

**The sense the lexicographers give: *waṣf* is to speak of a thing by a property found in it.** *Waṣafa'sh-shayʾ* — he described it.

**And a point of word order must be recorded: *ʿammā yaṣifūn* — "above what they describe".** **The verse does not say "above what they say" or "above their lies"; it says *above what they describe*.**

**I record this as my own reading:** *waṣf* is to describe a thing — that is, to draw its bounds. **And declaring God free is set exactly against this:** what is described has been bounded; **God is outside the bound the describer draws.** The choice of the word suggests that the matter is not merely a false claim but **the very attempt at description.** This is an explanation.

**And the sentence occurs twice in the sūra — this is a textual datum:**

| Verse | Sentence | Difference |
|---|---|---|
| **159** | *subḥāna'**llāhi** ʿammā yaṣifūn* | The divine name |
| **180** | *subḥāna **rabbika rabbi'l-ʿizzati** ʿammā yaṣifūn* | **Two constructs added** |

**The second is the expanded form of the first, and it is the closing of the sūra.** **The sūra closes by repeating in a longer form, in the hundred and eightieth verse, the sentence it said in the hundred and fifty-ninth.**

### إِلَّا عِبَادَ ٱللَّهِ ٱلْمُخْلَصِينَ — the fourth and last occurrence

**The sentence for the fourth time in the sūra (40, 74, 128, 160). It was analysed in the section on the fortieth verse.**

**And the dispute of word order here must be recorded:**

| Attachment | Sense |
|---|---|
| **To 158** | "They will be brought — except God's chosen servants" |
| **To 159** | "He is above what they describe — except the describing of God's chosen servants", that is, they describe God rightly |

**Both are transmitted by the grammarians.** **The second reading is worth noting because it turns the exception into a *praise*:** there is a company that can describe rightly. **I do not impose a preference.**

### فَإِنَّكُمْ وَمَا تَعْبُدُونَ · مَآ أَنتُمْ عَلَيْهِ بِفَٰتِنِينَ — you cannot lead astray

**And the address turns once more to the second person: *innakum* — "you".**

***Wa mā taʿbudūn* — "and what you serve".** **And the particle is again *mā*.** The same choice was present in the twenty-second and ninety-fifth verses. **The sūra is consistent in this, and it is a countable datum of the language.**

***Fātin* — root ف-ت-ن, an active participle.** **The root was analysed in the section on the sixty-third verse:** to put gold into fire and separate it; to test, to lead astray.

**Its sense here: *one who leads astray, who turns another from his way.***

**A point of word order:** *mā antum **ʿalayhi** bi-fātinīn* — the preposition *ʿalayhi* has been brought forward. **The grammarians divide over the referent of the pronoun:**

| To whom the pronoun refers | Sense |
|---|---|
| **To God** | "You cannot lead anyone astray against Him" — that is, you have no power to turn His servants |
| **To what they serve** | "You cannot deceive anyone about it (the idol)" |

**Both are transmitted. I do not impose a preference.**

**And the letter *bi-* has been added at the head of *fātinīn*** — in Arabic this *bāʾ*, coming on the predicate in negative sentences, announces **reinforcement**: "you are in no way leaders astray".

### إِلَّا مَنْ هُوَ صَالِ ٱلْجَحِيمِ — the exception

***Ṣālī* — root ص-ل-ي: to enter the fire, to be scorched in fire.** *Ṣalā* — he cast into / entered the fire. In the Qurʾān: *wa taṣliyatu jaḥīm* (al-Wāqiʿa 56/94) — **treated in `056-vakia.md`.**

**A point of word order:** *ṣāli'l-jaḥīm* — the construct of an active participle; the final *yāʾ* has dropped (*ṣālī* → *ṣāli*). **This is a regular dropping in Arabic in the construct state.**

***Al-jaḥīm* — for the sixth and last time in the sūra** (23, 55, 64, 68, 97, 163).

**And the sense of the exception is worth noting — as my own reading:** after saying "you cannot lead anyone astray", the verse sets a group apart: **those who are to enter the blaze in any case.** **Leading astray, that is, works upon one who deserves to be led astray.** This accords with the thirty-second verse of the sūra: there too those who led astray said *"we ourselves had gone astray"*, and those led astray were told *"you were not believers to begin with"* (29). **Both places say the same thing: an influence coming from outside does not work without finding a response within.**

---

## 37/164-166 — وَمَا مِنَّآ إِلَّا لَهُۥ مَقَامٌ مَّعْلُومٌ · وَإِنَّا لَنَحْنُ ٱلصَّآفُّونَ · وَإِنَّا لَنَحْنُ ٱلْمُسَبِّحُونَ

*Wa mā minnā illā lahu maqāmun maʿlūm · Wa innā la-naḥnu'ṣ-ṣāffūn · Wa innā la-naḥnu'l-musabbiḥūn*

"'There is not one of us but has a known station. We, we indeed are the ones who stand in ranks. We, we indeed are the ones who glorify.'"

### Who is speaking?

**And this is the most important question of word order in the sūra — because its answer bears on the oath in the first verse.**

The three verses are built in the first person plural (*minnā*, *innā*, *naḥnu*), and who is speaking **is not said.** There is no verb such as *qālū* ("they said") at the head of the verse.

**The views transmitted in the classical commentaries:**

| View | Who speaks | Ground | Its weak side |
|---|---|---|---|
| **The angels** | The angels counted female in the hundred and fiftieth verse speak for themselves | **The most widespread view.** The context is the angels (150, 158); *standing in ranks* and *glorifying* are attributed to angels in the Qurʾān | The verb of speaking (*qālū*) is not mentioned; the reader must supply it |
| **The Prophet and the believers** | The company that forms ranks and glorifies in prayer | *Ṣaff* and *tasbīḥ* are the work of believers as well | The context speaks of angels |
| **The prophets** | The messengers named in the sequence | The sūra recounted them for seventy-four verses | The sequence had ended at 148 |

**The overwhelming majority of the classical commentaries transmit the first view, and its ground is the context. I impose no preference, but I record that the context runs in the direction of the angels:** in the hundred and fiftieth verse the angels were named, in the hundred and fifty-eighth it was said that a kinship was set up with *al-jinna*, in the hundred and sixtieth the exception came. **These three verses stand inside that chain.**

**A point of word order and an ellipsis:** a verb *yaqūlūn* or *qālū* is supplied at the head of the sentence. **Most of the grammarians accept this.** **And the effect the ellipsis produces is worth noting — as my own reading:** because the verb is not stated, the voice comes in suddenly. **The text makes someone speak without saying who is speaking** — and this is the same method as in the first three verses of the sūra: **there too the name of what was sworn by was not given.**

### وَمَا مِنَّآ إِلَّا لَهُۥ مَقَامٌ مَّعْلُومٌ — a known station

**A point of word order:** the sentence is built with the *mā … illā* mould — **negation + exception**, that is, the strongest form of affirmation. **And there is an ellipsis between:** *wa mā minnā **[aḥadun]** illā lahu maqāmun maʿlūm* — "there is **not one** of us but has a known station".

**The grammarians supply the elided word as *aḥadun* (anyone).**

***Maqām* — root ق-و-م: to stand, to be upright.** A noun of place: **the place where one stands, a station.**

***Maʿlūm* was analysed in the section on the forty-first verse** (*rizqun maʿlūm*), **and the table of the two occurrences was given there.**

| Verse | What *maʿlūm* qualifies |
|---|---|
| **41** | *rizqun maʿlūm* — **provision** |
| **164** | *maqāmun maʿlūm* — **station** |

**The same attribute, the one of what is given and the other of where one stands.** This is a fact that can be verified from the text.

**I record this as my own reading:** what the sentence says is **an acceptance of a limit.** The speaking party claims no superiority for itself; **it says that a place has been assigned to it.** And this is the exact opposite of the claim in the hundred and fifty-second verse (*walada'llāh*): **a kinship is a claim of nearness; "a known station" is an acceptance of a position.**

**When the two sentences are set side by side the result is this:** the nearness attributed to them is refused by themselves. **The sūra refutes the claim from the mouth of the party claimed** — the same work done in the hundred and fifty-eighth verse by saying *"the jinn know"*.

### وَإِنَّا لَنَحْنُ ٱلصَّآفُّونَ — and the link with the first verse

**And this is the plainest internal ring of the sūra.**

| Verse | Expression | Pattern |
|---|---|---|
| **1** | *wa'**ṣ-ṣāffāt**i ṣaffā* | A feminine plural active participle — **what is sworn by** |
| **165** | *wa innā la-naḥnu'**ṣ-ṣāffūn*** | A masculine plural active participle — **the speaker** |

**The same root (ص-ف-ف), the same pattern (an active participle, definite), at the two ends of the sūra. This is a fact that can be verified from the text.**

**And the word order is very strong: three reinforcements.**

| Element | Its work |
|---|---|
| **إِنَّ** | Reinforcement |
| **لَـ** | *Lām al-ibtidāʾ* |
| **نَحْنُ** | *Ḍamīr al-faṣl* — "we, and not another" |

**The same threefold structure was present in the sixtieth and hundred and sixth verses** (*inna hādhā la-huwa…*). **Here it is used for the third time, and this time the subject is the first person plural.**

The sentence is therefore not "we form ranks" but **"the ones who form ranks are we"**. There is *ḥaṣr* (restriction).

### And the question of the subject of the oath

**Now we must return to the discussion in the section on the first verse.**

**It was recorded there:** the Qurʾān uses the word *ṣāffāt* plainly of **birds** (al-Nūr 24/41; al-Mulk 67/19), and this is an intra-Qurʾānic indication against the angel reading. **But the indication within the sūra pulls the other way: in the hundred and sixty-fifth verse a company uses the same root of itself.**

**These two indications conflict, and no preference was imposed in this commentary. Here I add only this note:**

**The hundred and sixty-fifth verse is the one ground *from within the sūra* for the angel reading of the first verse.** And the force of that ground depends on two things: (a) that those speaking in the hundred and sixty-fourth verse really are angels, and (b) that a link has been set up between the two verses. **Neither is stated openly in the text.**

**I record this as my own reading, and it is not binding:** the sharing of the root between the two verses is a fact and is hard to explain as coincidence — because the sūra's name too is from this root. **But this does not show that the first verse means *only* the angels;** at most it shows that at its own end the sūra leaves a voice supporting that reading. **The table of disagreement recorded in the section on the first verse keeps its validity.**

### وَإِنَّا لَنَحْنُ ٱلْمُسَبِّحُونَ — and the link with Jonah

**The same threefold reinforcement, the same mould.**

***Musabbiḥ* was analysed in the section on the hundred and forty-third verse, and the table of the four occurrences in the sūra was given there.**

**And the link here must be recorded:** *mina'l-**musabbiḥīn*** (143 — Jonah) and *la-naḥnu'l-**musabbiḥūn*** (166). **The same mould, the one the group a single person belongs to, the other a group speaking collectively.**

**I record this as my own reading:** in the hundred and forty-third verse a prophet in the belly of a fish is delivered by this act; in the hundred and sixty-sixth a company announces the same act as its own work. **The sūra names with the same word the act that saves and the act of the order of the heavens.**

**And the order of the two verses is worth noting too:** first *ṣāffūn* (standing in ranks — order), then *musabbiḥūn* (glorification — speech). **The order of the three oaths in the first verses was the same: first *ṣaff*, then *zajr*, then *tilāwa* (speech).** **The three verses, that is, repeat the order of the sūra's opening:** stance → voice. **This is an observation.**

---

## 37/167-170 — وَإِن كَانُوا۟ لَيَقُولُونَ · لَوْ أَنَّ عِندَنَا ذِكْرًا مِّنَ ٱلْأَوَّلِينَ · لَكُنَّا عِبَادَ ٱللَّهِ ٱلْمُخْلَصِينَ · فَكَفَرُوا۟ بِهِۦ ۖ فَسَوْفَ يَعْلَمُونَ

*Wa in kānū la-yaqūlūn · Law anna ʿindanā dhikran mina'l-awwalīn · La-kunnā ʿibāda'llāhi'l-mukhlaṣīn · Fa-kafarū bihi fa-sawfa yaʿlamūn*

"They used to say: 'If only we had a reminder from those of old, we would surely have been God's chosen servants.' But then they denied it. Soon they will know."

### وَإِن كَانُوا۟ لَيَقُولُونَ — word order

***In*** here is not the negative but ***mukhaffafa mina'th-thaqīla*** — the lightened *inna*. **The grammarians know it by the presence of the *lām*:** *la-yaqūlūn*. **The same structure was present in the fifty-sixth verse:** *in kidta la-turdīn.*

**The sense: "they really used to say".**

***Kānū* + imperfect:** a habit continuing in the past. **A word said not once but continually.**

### لَوْ أَنَّ عِندَنَا ذِكْرًا مِّنَ ٱلْأَوَّلِينَ — and the sharpest irony of the sūra

**And this is the sūra's most elegant internal link, and it is read entirely off the text.**

***Dhikran mina'l-awwalīn* — "a reminder from those of old".**

**And the root ذ-ك-ر occurs for the fourth and last time in the sūra. The table was given in the section on the structure. Now the table is completed:**

| Verse | Expression | What happens |
|---|---|---|
| **3** | *fa't-tāliyāti **dhikrā*** | The reminder is **carried** |
| **13** | *wa idhā **dhukkirū lā yadhkurūn*** | The reminder **arrives but is not taken** |
| **155** | *a-fa-lā **tadhakkarūn*** | The reminder **is asked for** |
| **168** | *law anna ʿindanā **dhikran** mina'l-awwalīn* | The reminder **is asked for as though it did not exist** |
| **170** | *fa-**kafarū** bihi* | The reminder that came **is denied** |

**This is a single story the sūra builds from beginning to end, and it is countable at five verse-points.**

**And the word *al-awwalūn* also occurs for the fourth and last time** (17, 71, 126, 168). **The table was given in the section on the seventy-first verse.**

**I record this as my own reading:** the irony of the sentence is this — **what they ask for is in their hands.** *Dhikr* is one of the Qurʾān's own names. **And the hundred and seventieth verse says so directly: *fa-kafarū bihi* — "then they denied it".**

**The conditional clause (*law*), that is, is a condition that has been realised, but the apodosis has not been realised.** In Arabic *law* announces an unrealised condition; **here the condition has been realised and the apodosis has not.** This is the structure of word order that refutes the sentence's claim.

### لَكُنَّا عِبَادَ ٱللَّهِ ٱلْمُخْلَصِينَ — the sūra's refrain inside a falsehood

**And this is the sūra's most striking structural fact.**

**The construction had occurred four times in the sūra as an *exception*** (40, 74, 128, 160) — **each time naming the group saved from a ruling.** The table was given in the section on the fortieth verse.

**The fifth occurrence is here, and this time the construction is:**

- Not an **exception** but a **claim**.
- Not in the mouth of the text but of **the deniers**.
- Not realised, but **the apodosis of an unrealised condition**.

| Occurrence | Who says it | Form |
|---|---|---|
| 40, 74, 128, 160 | **The text** | *illā ʿibāda'llāhi'l-mukhlaṣīn* — **an exception** |
| **169** | **The deniers** | *la-kunnā ʿibāda'llāhi'l-mukhlaṣīn* — **the apodosis of a condition** |

**This is a fact that can be verified from the text and is the one transformation performed with the sūra's key construction.**

**I record this as my own reading:** the construction the sūra set down four times as the address of deliverance it sets, the fifth time, inside **an unrealised claim.** **And immediately after, it says how the claim breaks down.** The construction itself does not change; **what changes is the state of the one who says it.**

### فَكَفَرُوا۟ بِهِۦ ۖ فَسَوْفَ يَعْلَمُونَ — and the threat

***Kafarū* — root ك-ف-ر: to cover, to hide.** *Kāfir* in Arabic also means **a farmer** (one who covers the seed with earth). **The root was analysed in `002-bakara.md`.**

***Bihi* — a pronoun.** **What does it refer to?** To the *dhikr* (168). **What they asked for has come, that is, and they have covered it over.**

***Fa-sawfa yaʿlamūn* — "soon they will know".**

***Sawfa*** — in Arabic the particle announcing the future. **And it differs from *sa-*: *sawfa* announces a more distant future and a heavier emphasis.**

**I record this as my own reading:** the sentence of threat is built with a verb of knowing: *yaʿlamūn* — **"they will know".** Not punishment but **learning** is promised. **And this is consistent with the subject of the block:** the block was built from beginning to end on a lack of knowledge — *wa hum shāhidūn* (150 — were they witnesses?), *sulṭānun mubīn* (156 — have you a warrant?), *fa'tū bi-kitābikum* (157 — bring your book), *dhikran mina'l-awwalīn* (168 — if only there were an announcement).

**Through five verses the side asked "do you know?" is warned "you will know".**

**And the root ع-ل-م will occur once more in the sūra, in a different pattern:** *fa-sawfa **yubṣirūn*** (175, 179) — there not knowing but **seeing**. **When the two moulds stand side by side, the two verbs of the sūra's last block emerge: to know and to see.**

---

## 37/171-179 — وَلَقَدْ سَبَقَتْ كَلِمَتُنَا لِعِبَادِنَا ٱلْمُرْسَلِينَ · إِنَّهُمْ لَهُمُ ٱلْمَنصُورُونَ · وَإِنَّ جُندَنَا لَهُمُ ٱلْغَٰلِبُونَ · فَتَوَلَّ عَنْهُمْ حَتَّىٰ حِينٍ · وَأَبْصِرْهُمْ فَسَوْفَ يُبْصِرُونَ · أَفَبِعَذَابِنَا يَسْتَعْجِلُونَ · فَإِذَا نَزَلَ بِسَاحَتِهِمْ فَسَآءَ صَبَاحُ ٱلْمُنذَرِينَ · وَتَوَلَّ عَنْهُمْ حَتَّىٰ حِينٍ · وَأَبْصِرْ فَسَوْفَ يُبْصِرُونَ
